# 2:30: 60 questions

## 2:30/q01 How does this account connect with its surrounding verses and sūra?  [* · links]
- 2:30/q01/p1 After recalling life, earth and its contents, and heaven as blessings, the passage turns to Adam's special excellence and returns to the argument of 2:28–29. — TABRISI (author): After recalling life, earth and its contents, and heaven as blessings, the passage turns to Adam's special excellence and returns to the argument of 2:28–29. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q01/p2 Those who put angels before prophets cite 2:285's order of belief and the Prophet's instruction to begin with what God began with. — RAZI-FULL (بعض الناس): Those who put angels before prophets cite 2:285's order of belief and the Prophet's instruction to begin with what God began with. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(بعض الناس)]
- 2:30/q01/p3 The contraction is compared with إنا معكم earlier in the sūra. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The contraction is compared with إنا معكم earlier in the sūra. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q01/p4 The Ottoman order of Baqarah, Ali Imran, al-Nisa, then al-Ma'idah is especially well arranged; Ibn Masud's codex put al-Nisa before Ali Imran, and surah order was chosen by scholarly judgment, not divinely prescribed. — BURHAN (author): The Ottoman order of Baqarah, Ali Imran, al-Nisa, then al-Ma'idah is especially well arranged; Ibn Masud's codex put al-Nisa before Ali Imran, and surah order was chosen by scholarly judgment, not divinely prescribed. [1 notes: BURHAN+]
- 2:30/q01/p5 Biqai outlines four aims of the surah’s discourse: show divine names, awaken hope and fear about the unseen end, recount God’s past acts to spur worship, and reveal the inward reality of the present. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Biqai outlines four aims of the surah’s discourse: show divine names, awaken hope and fear about the unseen end, recount God’s past acts to spur worship, and reveal the inward reality of the present. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p6 Al-Harali links the Adam account to the preceding stages of the surah: after categorizing people and issuing a general call beneath the veil of predestination, revelation now discloses the beginning of human creation. — BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Harali links the Adam account to the preceding stages of the surah: after categorizing people and issuing a general call beneath the veil of predestination, revelation now discloses the beginning of human creation. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 2:30/q01/p7 Adam’s story appears as the first Quranic story in al-Baqara; al-Araf later starts from creation and formation, as a continuation of the earlier account. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): Adam’s story appears as the first Quranic story in al-Baqara; al-Araf later starts from creation and formation, as a continuation of the earlier account. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q01/p8 The preceding verse begins by honoring humanity through creation of the earth’s contents for people and ends by affirming God’s knowledge; Adam’s story develops both themes. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): The preceding verse begins by honoring humanity through creation of the earth’s contents for people and ends by affirming God’s knowledge; Adam’s story develops both themes. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q01/p9 Al-Baqara’s account centers on Adam’s honor, with knowledge and vicegerency serving that theme; al-Araf has another purpose, and Adam’s honor there is secondary. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): Al-Baqara’s account centers on Adam’s honor, with knowledge and vicegerency serving that theme; al-Araf has another purpose, and Adam’s honor there is secondary. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q01/p10 The account in al-Baqara begins at the earliest point in Adam's story, a point not narrated elsewhere in the Quran and the starting point for Quranic narratives. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): The account in al-Baqara begins at the earliest point in Adam's story, a point not narrated elsewhere in the Quran and the starting point for Quranic narratives. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:30/q01/p11 Al-Baqara's account centers on Adam's honor and the knowledge he bears; teaching Adam extends to his descendants' succession, which entails both the right and the ability to act, with ability established through knowledge. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-Baqara's account centers on Adam's honor and the knowledge he bears; teaching Adam extends to his descendants' succession, which entails both the right and the ability to act, with ability established through knowledge. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:30/q01/p12 Al-A'raf presents Adam's story as a rebuke for human ingratitude, unlike al-Baqara's focus on honor; its opening recalls God's provision and says people give little thanks. — SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT (author): Al-A'raf presents Adam's story as a rebuke for human ingratitude, unlike al-Baqara's focus on honor; its opening recalls God's provision and says people give little thanks. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-LAMASAT-HALAQAT]
- 2:30/q01/p13 Al-Suddī says this was obligatory but abrogated by the zakāt verse. — JISHUMI (السدي): Al-Suddī says this was obligatory but abrogated by the zakāt verse. [1 notes: JISHUMI(السدي)]
- 2:30/q01/p14 Al-Mufaddal says it means raising one's voice in God's remembrance; Jarir's verse about pilgrims' loud praise illustrates the sense. — TABRISI (المفضل): Al-Mufaddal says it means raising one's voice in God's remembrance; Jarir's verse about pilgrims' loud praise illustrates the sense. [1 notes: TABRISI(المفضل)]
- 2:30/q01/p15 Al-Zarkashi calls this analysis profound and says it opens further insights into Qur'anic passages, including the Throne Verse. — BURHAN (author): Al-Zarkashi calls this analysis profound and says it opens further insights into Qur'anic passages, including the Throne Verse. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q01/p16 The account of humanity’s beginning continues the command to worship the Lord and explains the description of God as Lord of the worlds. — BIQAI-FULL (author): The account of humanity’s beginning continues the command to worship the Lord and explains the description of God as Lord of the worlds. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p17 Biqai says every verse connects with what precedes and prepares for what follows; this coherence is part of the Qur’an’s inimitability. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Biqai says every verse connects with what precedes and prepares for what follows; this coherence is part of the Qur’an’s inimitability. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p18 Al-Ālūsī acknowledges that this account exceeds rational demonstration but professes belief in it. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Ālūsī acknowledges that this account exceeds rational demonstration but professes belief in it. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p19 The story is joined to what precedes as one narrative episode following another. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The story is joined to what precedes as one narrative episode following another. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p20 Another view makes Iblīs the sole speaker, with the plural attribution like saying a clan killed someone when only one member did; al-Ālūsī favors his prior account. — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another view makes Iblīs the sole speaker, with the plural attribution like saying a clan killed someone when only one member did; al-Ālūsī favors his prior account. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q01/p21 The story clearly and subtly reveals how divine lordship manifests, recalls inward blessings embedded in human nature, and leads into the later direct-address warning. — ELMALILI (author): The story clearly and subtly reveals how divine lordship manifests, recalls inward blessings embedded in human nature, and leads into the later direct-address warning. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q01/p22 Following al-Kashshaf and Fakhr al-Din al-Razi, the author identifies a Qur’anic pattern: warnings are followed by promises and threats by glad tidings. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Following al-Kashshaf and Fakhr al-Din al-Razi, the author identifies a Qur’anic pattern: warnings are followed by promises and threats by glad tidings. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q01/p23 The ensuing allegory about reason implanted in human beings connects logically with the preceding passages. — ASAD-NOTES (author): The ensuing allegory about reason implanted in human beings connects logically with the preceding passages. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 2:30/q01/p24 The verses about humanity's creation to worship God and its creation as steward express one truth; worldly blessings are allotted to serve that purpose. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The verses about humanity's creation to worship God and its creation as steward express one truth; worldly blessings are allotted to serve that purpose. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:30/q02 What does the opening إذ mean?  [وَإِذْ · meaning]
- 2:30/q02/p1 An isolated gloss attributed to Abu Malik renders the opening as “and when—it had been”; the excerpt gives no fuller explanation. — IBNABIHATIM (أبو مالك): An isolated gloss attributed to Abu Malik renders the opening as “and when—it had been”; the excerpt gives no fuller explanation. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(أبو مالك)]
- 2:30/q02/p2 Thalabi reads the opening as ‘And your Lord said’ in the past tense. — THALABI (author): Thalabi reads the opening as ‘And your Lord said’ in the past tense. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q02/p3 Idh and idha mark time: idh normally refers to the past and idha to the future, though they can exchange places. — THALABI (author): Idh and idha mark time: idh normally refers to the past and idha to the future, though they can exchange places. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q02/p4 Al-Mubarrad says future-form verbs after idh refer to past events, citing ‘when they plotted against you’ and ‘when he says.’ — THALABI (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says future-form verbs after idh refer to past events, citing ‘when they plotted against you’ and ‘when he says.’ [1 notes: THALABI(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p5 Al-Mubarrad says idha with a past-form verb can refer to the future, citing Qur’anic examples and a poem whose idh means ‘will reward.’ — THALABI (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idha with a past-form verb can refer to the future, citing Qur’anic examples and a poem whose idh means ‘will reward.’ [1 notes: THALABI(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p6 An unnamed poet uses past idh for a future reward; Thalabi glosses the line as ‘he will reward him.’ — THALABI (الشاعر): An unnamed poet uses past idh for a future reward; Thalabi glosses the line as ‘he will reward him.’ [1 notes: THALABI(الشاعر)]
- 2:30/q02/p7 Al-Mubarrad says idh paired with a future-form verb gives it past meaning, as in ‘when he plotted’ (8:30). — WAHIDI-BASIT (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idh paired with a future-form verb gives it past meaning, as in ‘when he plotted’ (8:30). [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p8 Idh normally refers to the past and idhā to the future, though either can stand in place of the other. — JISHUMI (author): Idh normally refers to the past and idhā to the future, though either can stand in place of the other. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q02/p9 Al-Mubarrad says idh with a future verb takes past-time meaning, while idhā with a past verb takes future-time meaning; he cites 8:30, 79:34, and 110:1. — JISHUMI (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idh with a future verb takes past-time meaning, while idhā with a past verb takes future-time meaning; he cites 8:30, 79:34, and 110:1. [1 notes: JISHUMI(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p10 Al-Mubarrad says idh is past and idha future, though either can stand in place of the other; idh with a future form means a past event, as in 8:30. — BAGHAWI-FULL (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idh is past and idha future, though either can stand in place of the other; idh with a future form means a past event, as in 8:30. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p11 Al-Zajjaj takes إذ as a time marker: after mentioning creation, the verse means “at the time when your Lord said.” — TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj takes إذ as a time marker: after mentioning creation, the verse means “at the time when your Lord said.” [1 notes: TABRISI(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q02/p12 The Prophet said Adam was created from a handful taken from all the earth, so descendants vary in color, temperament, and goodness; al-Tirmidhī graded this report ṣaḥīḥ. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said Adam was created from a handful taken from all the earth, so descendants vary in color, temperament, and goodness; al-Tirmidhī graded this report ṣaḥīḥ. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q02/p13 Idh normally refers to the past and idha to the future, though each can stand in the other’s place. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Idh normally refers to the past and idha to the future, though each can stand in the other’s place. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q02/p14 Al-Mubarrad says idh with a future verb refers to the past, as in 8:30 and 33:37. — QURTUBI-FULL (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idh with a future verb refers to the past, as in 8:30 and 33:37. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p15 Al-Mubarrad says idha with a past verb can refer to the future, as in 79:34, 80:33, and 110:1. — QURTUBI-FULL (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad says idha with a past verb can refer to the future, as in 79:34, 80:33, and 110:1. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q02/p16 Al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur’s line is cited as evidence for Abu ʿUbayda’s reading of idh. — QURTUBI-FULL (الأسود بن يعفر): Al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur’s line is cited as evidence for Abu ʿUbayda’s reading of idh. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الأسود بن يعفر)]
- 2:30/q02/p17 A sixth analysis gives إذ the meaning قد. — SAMIN-DURR (author): A sixth analysis gives إذ the meaning قد. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q02/p18 The conjunction wāw joins what follows to something stated or understood before it; idh is an ambiguous noun for a past event or time. — BIQAI-FULL (author): The conjunction wāw joins what follows to something stated or understood before it; idh is an ambiguous noun for a past event or time. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q02/p19 Asad renders idh as “lo” here because it can mark a sudden or unexpected event or a turn in discourse, rather than simply “when.” — ASAD-NOTES (author): Asad renders idh as “lo” here because it can mark a sudden or unexpected event or a turn in discourse, rather than simply “when.” [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 2:30/q02/p20 An alternative takes the phrase as an instruction to remember when the Lord spoke. — THALABI (قيل): An alternative takes the phrase as an instruction to remember when the Lord spoke. [1 notes: THALABI(قيل)]
- 2:30/q02/p21 The earlier how-can-you-disbelieve clause itself implies an omitted remember, supporting the estimate remember when God spoke. — JISHUMI (author): The earlier how-can-you-disbelieve clause itself implies an omitted remember, supporting the estimate remember when God spoke. [1 notes: JISHUMI]

## 2:30/q03 What governs إذ and how does it function grammatically?  [وَإِذْ · grammar]
turns on: Whether idh contributes temporal meaning and which stated or implied clause governs it.
- 2:30/q03/p1 Idh is redundant — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Introductory idh is extra, so the Lord said. IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL (author): Idh may be extra in 2:30 and 31:13. IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL (author): Ibn Mayyada's line is poetry evidence for extra idh. NAHHAS (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda says «إذ» is a temporal adverb and is not an extra word. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Alternatively, إذ is not extra: the omitted sense is “He began your creation when,” with ذهب understood after the poet’s أينما. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The phrase may mean And your Lord said, with idh redundant, or may mean Remember when your Lord said; this explains similar Quranic constructions. IBNATIYYA-FULL (معمر بن المثنى): Maʿmar ibn al-Muthannā says the particle idh is redundant, yielding the sense “and your Lord said.” IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو إسحاق الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj calls Abū ʿUbayda’s claim that idh is redundant an audacious interpretation; Ibn ʿAṭiyya says all exegetes rejected it. TABRISI (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda calls إذ extra; al-Zajjaj and others reject this because a particle that adds sound meaning cannot be discarded. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو عبيدة وابن قتيبة): Abū ʿUbayda, followed by Ibn Qutayba, treats idh as redundant, yielding ‘and your Lord said.’ RAZI-FULL (بعض الناس): One view treats إذ as an extra particle customary in Arabic; Razi rejects it because the Qur'an contains nothing without meaning. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu ʿUbayda (Maʿmar ibn al-Muthanna) takes idh as redundant; al-Qurtubi rejects this, following the grammarians and exegetes. BAYDAWI-FULL (معمر): Maʿmar regards إذ as extra. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda and Ibn Qutayba take idh as redundant; Abū Ḥayyān rejects this and criticizes their grammatical competence. SAMIN-DURR (أبو عبيد): A fifth analysis treats إذ as redundant, attributed to Abu Ubayd. SAMIN-DURR (author): إذ is not declinable except when attached to a time noun, is not a direct object despite many grammarians' analysis, and is not a place adverb, redundant, causal particle or suddenness particle. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu ʿUbayda claimed idh is extra, making the sense simply “And your Lord said”; Ibn Jarir rejects this, as do all exegetes, and al-Zajjaj calls it rash. BURHAN (قيل): Some treat إذ in the creation account as extra; another analysis takes it to mean قد. ALUSI-FULL (author): Idh generally remains adverbial, though grammarians dispute whether it can be an object, causal particle, suddenness marker, place adverb, or redundant; al-Baḥr denies those uses outside contextual implication. ALUSI-FULL (المعربون): Grammarians variously call idh redundant or equivalent to qad, place it as a subject or an object of an omitted verb, attach it to earlier wording, or connect it to dhikr. ZAJJAJ (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda calls idh redundant; al-Zajjāj rejects this as reckless because idh is a noun meaning ‘time.’ IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج وابن القاسم): Al-Zajjāj and Ibn al-Qāsim reject the claim that idh is redundant; al-Zajjāj takes it to mean ‘at the time’ of the beginning of creation. QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج والنحاس): Al-Zajjaj, al-Nahhas, and the exegetes reject Abu ʿUbayda: idh is a temporal noun, not an extra particle. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Idh cannot be an object, causal or sudden particle, locative adverb, or redundant word, contrary to claims that it has those uses. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Making idh depend on khalaqakum from the earlier command requires a redundant wāw and separates the governor from it with long, syntactically independent verses. SAMIN-DURR (author): A proposal links إذ to خلقكم in 4:1 and calls the wāw redundant, but the author dismisses it because of the long distance. [20 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; IBNQUTAYBA-MUSHKIL; NAHHAS(أبو عبيدة); MAWARDI-FULL; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو إسحاق الزجاج,معمر بن المثنى); TABRISI(أبو عبيدة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو عبيدة وابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(بعض الناس); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عبيدة); BAYDAWI-FULL(معمر); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة); SAMIN-DURR(أبو عبيد); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو عبيدة); BURHAN(قيل); ALUSI-FULL(المعربون); ZAJJAJ(أبو عبيدة)-; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج وابن القاسم)-; QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج والنحاس)-; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-; SAMIN-DURR-]
- 2:30/q03/p2 An implied command to remember governs idh — JISHUMI (author): The governing verb for idh is omitted, with the primary estimate wa-udhkur idh, remember when. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الجمهور): The majority reject redundancy and connect idh to an implied command: “remember when your Lord said.” TABRISI (علي بن عيسى): Ali ibn Isa supplies the omitted command اذكر, making إذ its accusative object. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi's preferred analysis makes إذ accusative through an implied اذكر: remember that your Lord said this to the angels; the omission is clear from context. NASAFI-FULL (author): إذ is governed by an omitted imperative, اذكر, meaning recall the story. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn ʿAṭiyya, and others permit construing idh as an object of udhkur, though Abū Ḥayyān rejects that analysis. BURSEVI (author): Wa-idh is the object of an omitted imperative, 'remember'; commanding recollection of the time also requires remembering its events and details. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Some parse idh as an object of an unspoken udhkur, but this wrongly removes it from its temporal-adverbial function. SAMIN-DURR (author): One proposal governs إذ by an implied اذكر; the author has already rejected making إذ itself a direct object. [7 notes: JISHUMI; TABRISI(علي بن عيسى); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); BURSEVI; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الجمهور)+; RAZI-FULL+; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-; SAMIN-DURR-]
- 2:30/q03/p3 The following qālū governs idh — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): إذ is accusative with an implied اذكر, though it can also be governed by قالوا. RAZI-FULL (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf also allows إذ to be governed by قالوا. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The عامل for إذ may be قالوا, اذكر understood as in its many explicit Qur’anic uses, or an implicit phrase from the preceding verse; on the last analysis this clause coordinates with خلق لكم within its relative clause. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He prefers governing idh with qālū: when God says He will place a khalifa, the angels respond, Would You place one there who corrupts? This is clear temporal usage. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He reiterates that the preferred governor of idh is qālū, with the following clause أتجعل as its governed complement. SAMIN-DURR (author): قالوا governs إذ قال ربك, which al-Samīn identifies as the preferred parsing. SAMIN-DURR (author): Of nine analyses of إذ, the best and simplest makes it governed by قالوا أتجعل فيها: the angels said this at the time God said إني جاعل في الأرض خليفة. ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Ālūsī judges several proposed parses false or strained and favors making idh accusative through the following qālū, whose relation to it is clear. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Idh cannot be governed by the following qāla because idh is annexed to that clause, and an annexed complement cannot govern its annexer. [8 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL(صاحب الكشاف); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; ALUSI-FULL+; SAMIN-DURR+; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:30/q03/p4 Idh has the sense qad — MUQATIL (author): Idh means qad: the Lord had said this. ITQAN (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda and Ibn Qutayba treat some Qur'anic instances of إذ as emphatic, equivalent to qad, including وإذ قال ربك للملائكة. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): An alternative reads idh as qad, yielding wa-qad qāla rabbuka, but Abū Ḥayyān rejects it. [2 notes: MUQATIL; ITQAN(أبو عبيدة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]

- 2:30/q03/p6 The waw of ونحن is circumstantial, also called the waw of interruption, resumption, or inception; the implied relation is إذ نحن. — TABRISI (سيبويه): The waw of ونحن is circumstantial, also called the waw of interruption, resumption, or inception; the implied relation is إذ نحن. [1 notes: TABRISI(سيبويه)]
- 2:30/q03/p7 إذ marks a past time in which another event occurred, while إذا marks a future time; both are annexed to clauses, built like relative forms, used causally or conditionally, and always adverbial because they are nondeclinable. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): إذ marks a past time in which another event occurred, while إذا marks a future time; both are annexed to clauses, built like relative forms, used causally or conditionally, and always adverbial because they are nondeclinable. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p8 Idh is a built temporal noun for past time, built because it resembles a particle in form or need. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Idh is a built temporal noun for past time, built because it resembles a particle in form or need. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p9 After idh comes a nominal or verbal clause; with a verbal clause, fronting its subject and annexing idh to a present-initial clause are poor usage. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): After idh comes a nominal or verbal clause; with a verbal clause, fronting its subject and annexing idh to a present-initial clause are poor usage. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p10 A present verb governed by idh is made past in meaning; idh remains adverbial unless another time noun is annexed to it. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A present verb governed by idh is made past in meaning; idh remains adverbial unless another time noun is annexed to it. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p11 An implied beginning of creation governs idh — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Some make idh nominative as the beginning of creation; others make it accusative as the beginning of creation occurred when God spoke. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p12 General rule: open إن when its clause can replace a verbal noun; close it when it cannot, such as after قول, initially, in a relative clause or circumstantial clause. Both are possible when substitution is ambiguous, — SAMIN-DURR (author): General rule: open إن when its clause can replace a verbal noun; close it when it cannot, such as after قول, initially, in a relative clause or circumstantial clause. Both are possible when substitution is ambiguous, such as after فاء الجزاء or إذا الفجائية. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q03/p13 إذ is a past-time adverb that shifts a present verb into the past, is indeclinable because it resembles a particle in form and dependence, and may be followed by clauses; fronting a noun before the verb — SAMIN-DURR (author): إذ is a past-time adverb that shifts a present verb into the past, is indeclinable because it resembles a particle in form and dependence, and may be followed by clauses; fronting a noun before the verb in a verbal clause is awkward. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q03/p14 When the clause governed by إذ is omitted and understood, tanwin compensates for it and kasra avoids two sukuns rather than marking inflection; the author rejects al-Akhfash's alternative. — SAMIN-DURR (author): When the clause governed by إذ is omitted and understood, tanwin compensates for it and kasra avoids two sukuns rather than marking inflection; the author rejects al-Akhfash's alternative. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q03/p15 Idh is a past-time adverb, indeclinable because of its particle-like form and dependence; its clause is verbal or nominal, with time inferred from its predicate or context. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Idh is a past-time adverb, indeclinable because of its particle-like form and dependence; its clause is verbal or nominal, with time inferred from its predicate or context. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p16 Idh is a time noun for a past relation concurrent with another past relation; because its time is vague, it is always annexed to a clarifying clause. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Idh is a time noun for a past relation concurrent with another past relation; because its time is vague, it is always annexed to a clarifying clause. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p17 Idh usually functions as an adverbial accusative governed by the verb, but al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Hishām allow it to function as an object, contrary to the apparent majority view. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Idh usually functions as an adverbial accusative governed by the verb, but al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Hishām allow it to function as an object, contrary to the apparent majority view. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p18 In expressions such as yawmaʾidh, idh is added to another time noun in a formal construction, signaling that its normally required clause has been omitted. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): In expressions such as yawmaʾidh, idh is added to another time noun in a formal construction, signaling that its normally required clause has been omitted. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q03/p19 Gramma — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Grammarians classify idh and idha as time particles: idh is for the past and idha for the future. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:30/q03/p20 Abū ʿUbayda treats idh as extra, citing al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Abū ʿUbayda treats idh as extra, citing al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(أبو عبيدة)]
- 2:30/q03/p21 Abū Isḥāq connects the mention of human and other creation to an implied beginning: their creation began when the Lord spoke to the angels. — NAHHAS (أبو إسحاق): Abū Isḥāq connects the mention of human and other creation to an implied beginning: their creation began when the Lord spoke to the angels. [1 notes: NAHHAS(أبو إسحاق)]
- 2:30/q03/p22 Al-Zajjaj supplies an omitted clause about beginning creation; al-Qurtubi also allows an implied command, “Remember when,” or a connection to the — QURTUBI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj supplies an omitted clause about beginning creation; al-Qurtubi also allows an implied command, “Remember when,” or a connection to the command to worship the Creator. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q03/p23 Parsing idh under aḥyākum requires unsupported deletion; revival did not occur at that time, and it deletes a relative clause while retaining its complement. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Parsing idh under aḥyākum requires unsupported deletion; revival did not occur at that time, and it deletes a relative clause while retaining its complement. [0 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:30/q03/p24 A seventh makes إذ predicate to an omitted subject; an eighth governs it by an implied ابتدأ خلقكم. The author rejects both since creation's beginning was not the time of the statement, and إذ is indeclinable. — SAMIN-DURR (author): A seventh makes إذ predicate to an omitted subject; an eighth governs it by an implied ابتدأ خلقكم. The author rejects both since creation's beginning was not the time of the statement, and إذ is indeclinable. [0 notes: SAMIN-DURR-]
- 2:30/q03/p25 A ninth proposal governs إذ with an implied أحياكم; it is rejected because the two events occurred at different times. — SAMIN-DURR (author): A ninth proposal governs إذ with an implied أحياكم; it is rejected because the two events occurred at different times. [0 notes: SAMIN-DURR-]
- 2:30/q03/p26 A proposal makes the later قال govern إذ, but this fails because a mudaf ilayh cannot govern its mudaf. — SAMIN-DURR (author): A proposal makes the later قال govern إذ, but this fails because a mudaf ilayh cannot govern its mudaf. [0 notes: SAMIN-DURR-]

## 2:30/q04 Why does the verse address Muhammad as “your Lord”?  [رَبُّكَ · rhetoric]
- 2:30/q04/p1 God’s possessive address to Muḥammad, “your Lord,” and the second-person suffix honor him and display special favor toward him. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): God’s possessive address to Muḥammad, “your Lord,” and the second-person suffix honor him and display special favor toward him. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q04/p2 The return of direct address to Muhammad reveals the unseen past of God's conversation with the angels about Adam, encouraging the guided toward higher ranks and calling the heedless back; revelation leads the rationally guided from — BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): The return of direct address to Muhammad reveals the unseen past of God's conversation with the angels about Adam, encouraging the guided toward higher ranks and calling the heedless back; revelation leads the rationally guided from faith to certainty. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي)]
- 2:30/q04/p3 “Your Lord” is the benefactor who showed mercy to humanity through Muhammad and kept him concealed within the disclosure of Adam’s creation. — BIQAI-FULL (author): “Your Lord” is the benefactor who showed mercy to humanity through Muhammad and kept him concealed within the disclosure of Adam’s creation. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q04/p4 The direct address to Muhammad signals his greatest share in the announced affair: he is the greatest vicegerent and foremost imam in creation. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The direct address to Muhammad signals his greatest share in the announced affair: he is the greatest vicegerent and foremost imam in creation. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:30/q05 What does rabb mean?  [رَبُّكَ · meaning]
- 2:30/q05/p1 قول introduces quoted speech; رب means master or lord, applies to a house or horse, and with the definite article is reserved for God. — TABRISI (author): قول introduces quoted speech; رب means master or lord, applies to a house or horse, and with the definite article is reserved for God. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q05/p2 رب is traced to ربيته, managing someone's affairs; a rabbani scholar manages the community's affairs. — TABRISI (author): رب is traced to ربيته, managing someone's affairs; a rabbani scholar manages the community's affairs. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q05/p3 Rabb means owner, master, rectifier, and restorer. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Rabb means owner, master, rectifier, and restorer. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q05/p4 A poem’s طربت وما شوقا إلى البيض أطرب is also analyzed with an omitted hamza. — SAMIN-DURR (الأخفش): A poem’s طربت وما شوقا إلى البيض أطرب is also analyzed with an omitted hamza. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الأخفش)]
- 2:30/q05/p5 An anonymous poem is analyzed with القوانس governed by an implied ضرب, not by أضرب. — SAMIN-DURR (author): An anonymous poem is analyzed with القوانس governed by an implied ضرب, not by أضرب. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q05/p6 قال ربك is a verbal clause in the genitive because it is annexed to the temporal adverb. — SAMIN-DURR (author): قال ربك is a verbal clause in the genitive because it is annexed to the temporal adverb. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:30/q06 What is the origin and form of malāʾika?  [لِلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةِ · meaning]
- 2:30/q06/p1 Malak is connected with words for sending a message — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malak and malaika from the language of message, discussing hamza movement, plurals and cited Arabic poetry. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari derives malaika from message, since angels carry God's messages, citing alikni. IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatadah says God knew this khalifa's line would include messengers, prophets, righteous people, and people who would dwell in the Garden. THALABI (author): Malak means messenger; the singular is malak, from malik, and the plural is malaika, from terms for message or messenger. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Malāʾika is the plural of malak, connected to message; alaka means “send me.” JISHUMI (author): Malak derives from the language of sending a message: alīkni means send me, and maʾlaka or alūk means message; the original hamza was dropped for ease. JISHUMI (author): Malak has the pattern mafʿal after deletion of its hamza and became the established name for a class of God’s nonhuman messengers. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Malak derives from ma'lak, from words meaning message; the hamza shifted after the lam, then was dropped for ease of frequent use and its vowel moved to the lam. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): A poetic line preserves the word malʾak in its original form, supporting the underlying hamza analysis. IBNATIYYA-FULL (قوم): Another etymology derives malak from maʾlak, from alaka, “to send,” and parses malāʾika as its plural. IBNATIYYA-FULL (عدي بن زيد): The poet ʿAdī ibn Zayd uses maʾlakan as evidence for the alternative derivation of malak. TABRISI (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda derives the word from لأك, “to send”; ملأك and ملائكة are then unaltered forms with an extra initial mīm. TABRISI (ابن كيسان): Ibn Kaysan derives it from الملك: ملأك is فعال like شمأل, ملائكة is فعائلة, the mīm is original, and hamza added. TABRISI (author): Though “angel” originally meant messenger, it became the established label for God's nonhuman messengers, as “heaven” came to name the familiar skies. TABRISI (أصحابنا): The author's companions say not all angels are messengers, citing 22:75; if all were messengers all would be selected, so ملك is generic rather than derived from message. TABRISI (أكثر العلماء والخليل): Most scholars derive angels from الألوكة, “message”; al-Khalil defines ألوك as message and gives the related form مألكة. TABRISI (غيره): Another etymology says a message is called ألوكة because it is chewed in the mouth, like a horse chewing its bridle. TABRISI (عدي بن زيد): Adi ibn Zayd's مألكا in his poem witnesses the message derivation. TABRISI (لبيد): Labid's ألوك in his poem witnesses the message derivation. TABRISI (الهذلي): The Hudhali poet's ألكني in his verse is another witness to the message derivation. TABRISI (author): On the message derivation, ملائكة is a reversed plural of ملأك meaning messenger; a poem attests the hamzated form. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Malāʾika derives from al-ūlūk, meaning a message; Labīd’s line about a mother sending a message is evidence. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): The singular of malāʾika is malak, originally malʾak, as Sibawayh’s verse demonstrates. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو إسحاق): Abū Isḥāq glosses malʾak as ‘bearer of a message’; related forms include maʾlaka and malʾaka, with maʾālik as a plural, illustrated by a poem to al-Nuʿmān. RAZI-FULL (author): The word ملك derives from a root meaning message: ألكني إليه means send me to him, and المألكة or الألوكة means a message. RAZI-FULL (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf derives الملائك as the plural of the original ملأك, like شمائل from شمأل, with feminine plural تاء. RAZI-FULL (بعض الناس): They also argue angels precede prophets because angels mediate revelation and the law to the messengers. RAZI-FULL (علي): Ali says angels serve as trustees of revelation and messengers, execute God's decrees, guard people, and keep Paradise's gates. RAZI-FULL (author): Angels are God's messengers, as stated in 35:1; the verse saying God chooses messengers from angels does not mean only some angels are messengers, since من is explanatory. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reads من in 22:75 as explanatory rather than partitive, answering the objection that only some angels are messengers. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن مسعود وابن عباس): Ibn Masʿud, Ibn ʿAbbas, and exegetes identify Adam as the khalifa, God’s deputy in carrying out His rulings and commands and the first messenger to earth. QURTUBI-FULL (آخر): An unnamed poet uses maʾlak for a message to al-Nuʿman. QURTUBI-FULL (الشاعر): A poetic line preserves malaʾak for a messenger descending from the sky. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu ʿUbayda derives malak from laʾaka, “to send,” and explains al-uluka and related forms as “message.” BAYDAWI-FULL (author): الملائكة is the plural of ملأك in its original form, like الشمائل from شمأل; مألك is inverted from مألكة, a message, since angels convey God’s messages to people or are messengers to them. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another derivation makes the first radical hamza and the second lām from al-ʾalūka, message; māʾlak was inverted into malʾak. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A third derivation links malak to lāka, to roll something in the mouth; the messenger carries a message in the mouth, with the middle radical deleted for ease. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): One analysis reconstructs the original form as faʿʾal like shamʾal, then transfers the vowel and deletes letters; malʾak is also attested. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): One derivation connects malak to lāka, to send: the original was malʾak, reduced by moving the vowel and deleting, with malāʾika on mufāʿala. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda gives the malʾak derivation, which Abū al-Fatḥ also selects; on this account malāʾika is mufāʿala. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Attributing the speech to the Lord fits the prior mention of provision on earth and honors the Messenger through a special address that introduces humanity's origin and destiny. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): Another original-mīm derivation starts from ملأك with an extra hamza like شمأل; its vowel shifts to the lām and the hamza drops, while the plural preserves the original addition. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): A third derivation makes mīm extra and derives the word from ألك, to send. Poems with مألكة, بألوك and مألك attest the message sense; the derivation involves metathesis and hamza deletion, with the plural restoring hamza. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): A fourth analysis derives it from لأك, also meaning to send; the poem فلست لإنسي ولكن لملأك witnesses the form, and the plural restores hamza. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): A fifth derivation connects ملك to لاكه يلوكه, to turn something over, since an angel turns a message in the mouth; it derives singular ملوك and plural ملاوكة with vowel change, deletion and hamza formation. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): God answers that He knows the greater good outweighing the harms: humanity will include prophets, messengers, truthful ones, martyrs, righteous people, worshippers, scholars, the humble, and those who love and follow Him. BIQAI-FULL (أهل العربية): The grammarians derive malāʾika from the inverted form malāʾik, itself from maʾlak and the root of sending a message; on this account the mīm is extra. BIQAI-FULL (author): Biqai proposes that malāʾika gathers both senses, messenger and ordering power, as a coined word can unite meanings from two roots. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says God knew that this creation would include prophets, messengers, righteous people, and inhabitants of Paradise. BURSEVI (author): Malāʾika is the plural of malak, with tāʾ marking a feminine collective; angels are named as intermediaries and messengers between God and people, from alūka, message. ALUSI-FULL (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī, whose account the majority favor, derives malāʾika from malʾak by metathesis and connects it to al-ūlūka, meaning the message; angels are messengers to people. STUDYQURAN (author): Khalīfah can mean successor or deputy; khalīfat rasūl Allāh, successor or steward of God’s Messenger, was shortened to khalīfah and anglicized as caliph. MAWARDI-FULL (author): The name al-ūlūka is linked to a message being moved in the mouth; a horse “chews” its iron bit. [60 notes: TAB-FULL(أمية بن أبي الصلت,لبيد بن ربيعة); IBNABIHATIM(قتادة); THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(عدي بن زيد,قوم); TABRISI(أبو عبيدة,أصحابنا,أكثر العلماء والخليل,ابن كيسان,الهذلي,عدي بن زيد,غيره,لبيد); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو إسحاق); RAZI-FULL(بعض الناس,صاحب الكشاف,علي); QURTUBI-FULL(آخر,أبو عبيدة,ابن مسعود وابن عباس,الشاعر); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة); SAMIN-DURR(بعضهم); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(أهل العربية); DURR-FULL(قتادة); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الكسائي); STUDYQURAN]
- 2:30/q06/p2 Malak is derived from dominion or strength — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن كيسان): Ibn Kaysān derives malak from malaka, treating the hamza as extra as in shamʾal from shamal, and gives the plural pattern faʿāʾila. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن كيسان): Another derivation makes the original malaʾak from malaka, with an augmentative hamza, like shamʾal from shamila. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda treats the mīm in malak as original, derives it from strength, and calls its plural malāʾika on faʿāʾila anomalous. SAMIN-DURR (بعضهم): One etymology makes ملك a form فعَل from الملك with original mīm; on this account only its plural فعائلة is irregular. SAMIN-DURR (النضر بن شميل): Al-Nadr ibn Shumayl says ملك has no derivation in Arab usage. BIQAI-FULL (أهل العربية): A second derivation connects malak to dominion and control, as in kneading dough, with an original mīm and the sense of shaping or ordering. ALUSI-FULL (ابن كيسان): Ibn Kaysān rejects metathesis and derives the word as faʿāl from al-mulk, with an added hamza, meaning one who rules what God entrusts or has strength; al-Ālūsī calls the derivation remote. [7 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن كيسان); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن كيسان); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة); SAMIN-DURR(النضر بن شميل,بعضهم); BIQAI-FULL(أهل العربية); ALUSI-FULL(ابن كيسان)]
- 2:30/q06/p3 Arab usage gives no known derivation of malak — THALABI (النضر بن شميل): Al-Naḍr ibn Shumayl says Arabs neither derive a verb from malak nor inflect it; its origin is beyond their knowledge. QURTUBI-FULL (النضر بن شميل): Al-Nadr ibn Shumayl says Arabs recognize no derivation for malak. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (النضر بن شميل): Al-Naḍr ibn Shumayl says Arabs neither derive nor inflect malak, whose etymology has escaped knowledge. [3 notes: THALABI(النضر بن شميل); QURTUBI-FULL(النضر بن شميل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(النضر بن شميل)]
- 2:30/q06/p4 Tabari gives malak as singular of malaika and explains transfer of hamza vowel to lam. — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari gives malak as singular of malaika and explains transfer of hamza vowel to lam. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p5 «الملائكة» is genitive after the lām; the group is grammatically feminine. — NAHHAS (author): «الملائكة» is genitive after the lām; the group is grammatically feminine. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q06/p6 Thaʿlabī explains malak as formed by dropping the hamza for ease and frequent use. — THALABI (author): Thaʿlabī explains malak as formed by dropping the hamza for ease and frequent use. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q06/p7 Taqdīs derives from purification; “the holy land” means the purified land, as the cited line uses al-muqaddas for “purified.” — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Taqdīs derives from purification; “the holy land” means the purified land, as the cited line uses al-muqaddas for “purified.” [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p8 Another etymology says qiddas means silver beads because they are white and pure; a poetic line compares tears to a broken string of qiddas. — WAHIDI-BASIT (غيره): Another etymology says qiddas means silver beads because they are white and pure; a poetic line compares tears to a broken string of qiddas. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(غيره)]
- 2:30/q06/p9 الملائكة follows the original form ملاك in its plural pattern, like شمائل from شمأل; the feminine suffix marks a plural group. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): الملائكة follows the original form ملاك in its plural pattern, like شمائل from شمأل; the feminine suffix marks a plural group. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p10 The verb has readings with ḍamma or fatḥ on fāʾ, and forms derived from أسفك and سفك. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The verb has readings with ḍamma or fatḥ on fāʾ, and forms derived from أسفك and سفك. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p11 In ordinary speech the hamza is eased and its vowel transferred to the lām or ʿayn; the tāʾ in malāʾika marks a nonliteral feminine plural or intensification, with the first account clearer to Ibn ʿAṭiyya. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن كيسان): In ordinary speech the hamza is eased and its vowel transferred to the lām or ʿayn; the tāʾ in malāʾika marks a nonliteral feminine plural or intensification, with the first account clearer to Ibn ʿAṭiyya. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن كيسان)]
- 2:30/q06/p12 Abū ʿUbayda says the hamza in malāʾika is inserted because its singular is malak. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda says the hamza in malāʾika is inserted because its singular is malak. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو عبيدة)]
- 2:30/q06/p13 The singular malak is derived from malāk on the pattern mafʿal, from lāka, “to send”; malāʾika is its plural on the pattern mafāʿila. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The singular malak is derived from malāk on the pattern mafʿal, from lāka, “to send”; malāʾika is its plural on the pattern mafāʿila. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p14 A poem supports دم on فعل; another derives it as فعِل and explains the restored, vowelled yāʾ in its dual as evidence of its original form. — TABRISI (author): A poem supports دم on فعل; another derives it as فعِل and explains the restored, vowelled yāʾ in its dual as evidence of its original form. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q06/p15 سبحان derives from السبح, going or flowing; this supreme religious glorification is reserved for God, as worship is the highest gratitude due only to Him. — TABRISI (author): سبحان derives from السبح, going or flowing; this supreme religious glorification is reserved for God, as worship is the highest gratitude due only to Him. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q06/p16 جاعل derives from جعل, which takes two objects; في الأرض and خليفة are the two objects, meaning to make a successor on earth. — RAZI-FULL (author): جاعل derives from جعل, which takes two objects; في الأرض and خليفة are the two objects, meaning to make a successor on earth. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p17 Tasbih derives from sabh, movement or going; one who glorifies God proceeds in declaring Him free of evil, linked to the long daytime sabh in 73:7. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Tasbih derives from sabh, movement or going; one who glorifies God proceeds in declaring Him free of evil, linked to the long daytime sabh in 73:7. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p18 The term qudus is associated with purity; the term for a bucket used in ablution and qādus derive from the same idea of washing and purification. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The term qudus is associated with purity; the term for a bucket used in ablution and qādus derive from the same idea of washing and purification. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p19 Alikni means “send me”; the hamza moves from initial to medial position, then the form is eased to malak. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Alikni means “send me”; the hamza moves from initial to medial position, then the form is eased to malak. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p20 Ibn Kaysan and others derive malak from milk, on the pattern faʿal. — QURTUBI-FULL (ابن كيسان): Ibn Kaysan and others derive malak from milk, on the pattern faʿal. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن كيسان)]
- 2:30/q06/p21 ملائكة is the plural of ملاك, like شمائل from شمأل. — NASAFI-FULL (author): ملائكة is the plural of ملاك, like شمائل from شمأل. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p22 On the third derivation, malāʾika is mufāʿala and its hamza replaces wāw, as in maṣāʾib. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): On the third derivation, malāʾika is mufāʿala and its hamza replaces wāw, as in maṣāʾib. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p23 The tāʾ in malāʾika marks a feminine plural, or, according to another view, intensification; poetry uses the form without tāʾ. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The tāʾ in malāʾika marks a feminine plural, or, according to another view, intensification; poetry uses the form without tāʾ. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p24 A competing account supposes malak came from malāk on faʿʿāl, although masculine and feminine faʿʿāl plurals on faʿāʾil are uncommon. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A competing account supposes malak came from malāk on faʿʿāl, although masculine and feminine faʿʿāl plurals on faʿāʾil are uncommon. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p25 An unnamed poet uses malāʾik without the tāʾ found in the ordinary plural malāʾika. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): An unnamed poet uses malāʾik without the tāʾ found in the ordinary plural malāʾika. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p26 Tasbīḥ means declaring God free of evil, is used only for God, and derives from sabḥ, running; the one who glorifies runs in that declaration. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Tasbīḥ means declaring God free of evil, is used only for God, and derives from sabḥ, running; the one who glorifies runs in that declaration. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p27 The lām in lil-malāʾika marks conveying speech, one of the lām's functions. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The lām in lil-malāʾika marks conveying speech, one of the lām's functions. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p28 للملائكة attaches to قال, and ل is the particle of communication. — SAMIN-DURR (author): للملائكة attaches to قال, and ل is the particle of communication. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q06/p29 The ة in ملائكة marks a feminine plural as in صلادمة, or could be an intensifier as in علامة and نسابة; the author rejects that latter view. Poetry attests rare deletion of the suffix in الملائك. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The ة in ملائكة marks a feminine plural as in صلادمة, or could be an intensifier as in علامة and نسابة; the author rejects that latter view. Poetry attests rare deletion of the suffix in الملائك. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q06/p30 Al-Zamakhsharī derives تقديس from traveling far in the land, making its sense close to تسبيح. — SAMIN-DURR (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī derives تقديس from traveling far in the land, making its sense close to تسبيح. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري)]
- 2:30/q06/p31 Ibn Jarir also derives khalifa from one person succeeding another, and says succession here means one generation following another, as in Yunus 14. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir also derives khalifa from one person succeeding another, and says succession here means one generation following another, as in Yunus 14. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير)]
- 2:30/q06/p32 Al-Dānī explains the attested articulation as caused by an irregular middle consonant: the Basrans derive it from أهل, the Kufans from أول, with substitutions changing the word. — NASHR (الداني): Al-Dānī explains the attested articulation as caused by an irregular middle consonant: the Basrans derive it from أهل, the Kufans from أول, with substitutions changing the word. [1 notes: NASHR(الداني)]
- 2:30/q06/p33 Biqai compares the blended derivation to insān, which he says carries both the senses of sociability and forgetfulness. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Biqai compares the blended derivation to insān, which he says carries both the senses of sociability and forgetfulness. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p34 It also assigns jaʿl to the world of Command and malakut, in contrast with creation of the sensory world; the Qur’an calls heavens and earth created but darkness and light made. — BURSEVI (التأويلات النجمية): It also assigns jaʿl to the world of Command and malakut, in contrast with creation of the sensory world; the Qur’an calls heavens and earth created but darkness and light made. [1 notes: BURSEVI(التأويلات النجمية)]
- 2:30/q06/p35 This reading treats darkness and light as malakuti rather than sensory realities, since sensory darkness and light are already contained in the heavens and earth. — BURSEVI (author): This reading treats darkness and light as malakuti rather than sensory realities, since sensory darkness and light are already contained in the heavens and earth. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q06/p36 What is unseen depends on the observer: the malakut is hidden from sensory humans but visible to angels and to people granted spiritual sight. — BURSEVI (author): What is unseen depends on the observer: the malakut is hidden from sensory humans but visible to angels and to people granted spiritual sight. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q06/p37 The malakut is witnessed by angels, while the divine presence remains hidden from them; they cannot ascend to that presence. — BURSEVI (author): The malakut is witnessed by angels, while the divine presence remains hidden from them; they cannot ascend to that presence. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q06/p38 Humans unite a sensory body, a spirit from the unseen malakut, and a secret prepared to receive divine illumination; spiritual training raises them from the witnessed world to malakut. — BURSEVI (author): Humans unite a sensory body, a spirit from the unseen malakut, and a secret prepared to receive divine illumination; spiritual training raises them from the witnessed world to malakut. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q06/p39 Through following the Prophet, the human secret rises from malakut to jabarut and the hidden of the hidden, witnessing the lights of divine beauty and majesty and becoming a caliph of the seen and unseen. — BURSEVI (author): Through following the Prophet, the human secret rises from malakut to jabarut and the hidden of the hidden, witnessing the lights of divine beauty and majesty and becoming a caliph of the seen and unseen. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q06/p40 Abū ʿUbayda derives the term from lāka, “to send,” as a passive verbal noun or intensive place noun; al-Ālūsī calls this remote because lāka is uncommon. — ALUSI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda derives the term from lāka, “to send,” as a passive verbal noun or intensive place noun; al-Ālūsī calls this remote because lāka is uncommon. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أبو عبيدة)]
- 2:30/q06/p41 Some derive tasbīḥ from rapid swimming, extended to the courses of stars and a running horse, and suggest it evokes swift passage in worship. — IBNASHUR-FULL (قوم من أهل اللغة): Some derive tasbīḥ from rapid swimming, extended to the courses of stars and a running horse, and suggest it evokes swift passage in worship. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(قوم من أهل اللغة)]
- 2:30/q06/p42 Khalifah derives from khalafa, “he succeeded”; in this allegory it denotes humanity’s rightful supremacy on earth, which Asad renders as inheriting the earth. — ASAD-NOTES (author): Khalifah derives from khalafa, “he succeeded”; in this allegory it denotes humanity’s rightful supremacy on earth, which Asad renders as inheriting the earth. [1 notes: ASAD-NOTES]
- 2:30/q06/p43 The angels, created earlier in the malakut realm and unlike humans, expected themselves to govern earth and enact the divine will stated plainly in a book binding on every believer. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Melekler): The angels, created earlier in the malakut realm and unlike humans, expected themselves to govern earth and enact the divine will stated plainly in a book binding on every believer. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Melekler)]
- 2:30/q06/p44 Human knowledge of angels is chiefly revelation-based because people cannot know beings of the unseen and malakut through ordinary means; interpretations drawn by human reasoning and analogy differ. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Human knowledge of angels is chiefly revelation-based because people cannot know beings of the unseen and malakut through ordinary means; interpretations drawn by human reasoning and analogy differ. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q06/p45 Some commentators derive khalīfah from khalafa, to come after: humans follow other creatures and summarize all grades of being in the human state. — STUDYQURAN (some commentators): Some commentators derive khalīfah from khalafa, to come after: humans follow other creatures and summarize all grades of being in the human state. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(some commentators)]
- 2:30/q06/p46 God's disclosure of unseen matters to Muhammad, inaccessible except by revelation, establishes his prophethood before the Jews and shows his message comes from God. — TAB-FULL (author): God's disclosure of unseen matters to Muhammad, inaccessible except by revelation, establishes his prophethood before the Jews and shows his message comes from God. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p47 Al-Awlukah means a message; Labīd’s verse uses it for a message sent by a boy’s mother. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Al-Awlukah means a message; Labīd’s verse uses it for a message sent by a boy’s mother. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q06/p48 God’s knowledge included that Adam’s descendants would produce prophets, messengers, righteous people and inhabitants of Paradise. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (قتادة): God’s knowledge included that Adam’s descendants would produce prophets, messengers, righteous people and inhabitants of Paradise. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q06/p49 Although Arabic speakers derive Adam from brownness or the earth’s surface, the author says Adam is a foreign name, probably on the فاعل pattern. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Although Arabic speakers derive Adam from brownness or the earth’s surface, the author says Adam is a foreign name, probably on the فاعل pattern. [0 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL-]
- 2:30/q06/p50 The author considers derivation from swimming by metaphor remote; he prefers derivation from subḥān, with the doubled verb pattern, and notes no un-doubled form meaning glorify is attested. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The author considers derivation from swimming by metaphor remote; he prefers derivation from subḥān, with the doubled verb pattern, and notes no un-doubled form meaning glorify is attested. [0 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL-]

## 2:30/q07 What are angels by nature?  [لِلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةِ · theology]
- 2:30/q07/p1 Angels are subtle bodies that can assume forms — RAZI-FULL (أكثر المسلمين): Most Muslims hold that angels are subtle airy bodies, able to take different forms, and dwelling in the heavens. BAYDAWI-FULL (أكثر المسلمين): Most Muslims hold that angels are subtle bodies able to assume different forms, reasoning that messengers saw them in such forms. BURSEVI (أكثر المسلمين): Most Muslims hold that angels are subtle bodies able to assume different forms; the prophets' sightings are evidence. ALUSI-FULL (أكثر المسلمين): Most Muslims hold that angels are luminous bodies; another view calls them airy beings able, by God's permission, to assume different forms. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Müslüman âlimlerin çoğu): Most Muslim scholars describe angels as subtle, shape-shifting beings whose primary abode is the transcendent heavens and who multiply through continual creation rather than reproduction. MAWARDI-FULL (author): They have subtle bodies and are unseen unless God strengthens human sight to see them. JISHUMI (author): Angels are a distinct kind of living creature, with wings, no eating, drinking, or sexual relations; their subtlety makes them invisible unless God strengthens our vision or gives them density. [7 notes: RAZI-FULL(أكثر المسلمين); BAYDAWI-FULL(أكثر المسلمين); BURSEVI(أكثر المسلمين); ALUSI-FULL(أكثر المسلمين); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Müslüman âlimlerin çoğu); MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q07/p2 Angels are immaterial substances — RAZI-FULL (الفلاسفة): Philosophers describe angels as self-subsisting, nonspatial substances distinct from human souls and greater in power and knowledge, as the Sun is to its light. BAYDAWI-FULL (الحكماء): The philosophers describe angels as immaterial substances distinct from rational souls; one class is absorbed in knowing God and free of other occupation, the exalted, close angels, who glorify without tiring. ALUSI-FULL (الفلاسفة): Philosophers describe angels as immaterial substances distinct in essence from rational souls; some identify them with ten intellects and celestial souls. ELMALILI (author): Modern non-Islamic theories reducing matter to force and force to spirit reproduce the philosophers’ doctrine of immaterial substances; treating such entities as conceptual aligns with theologians. [4 notes: RAZI-FULL(الفلاسفة); BAYDAWI-FULL(الحكماء); ALUSI-FULL(الفلاسفة); ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q07/p3 Angels are purified disembodied souls — RAZI-FULL (طوائف من النصارى): Some Christians identify angels with disembodied rational souls: purified, good souls are angels, while evil, clouded souls are devils. ALUSI-FULL (النصارى): Christians identify angels with separate rational souls; their pure souls are good angels, while the wicked are demons. BAYDAWI-FULL (طائفة من النصارى): A group of Christians holds that angels are virtuous human souls separated from their bodies. [3 notes: RAZI-FULL(طوائف من النصارى); ALUSI-FULL(النصارى); BAYDAWI-FULL(طائفة من النصارى)]
- 2:30/q07/p4 Angels are identified with celestial bodies — RAZI-FULL (طوائف من عبدة الأوثان): Some idol worshippers identify angels with the actual planetary natures they deem auspicious or inauspicious: auspicious planets are mercy angels and inauspicious ones punishment angels. ALUSI-FULL (عبدة الأوثان): Idolaters identify angels with stars, regarding auspicious stars as angels of mercy and unlucky ones as angels of punishment. [2 notes: RAZI-FULL(طوائف من عبدة الأوثان); ALUSI-FULL(عبدة الأوثان)]
- 2:30/q07/p5 Nuqaddisu laka means angels ascribe purity to God. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Nuqaddisu laka means angels ascribe purity to God. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB]
- 2:30/q07/p6 Al-Tabari prefers that the angels asked God to inform them whether he would put such corrupt beings on earth and leave his deputies among them; their question was inquiry, not denial, though they abhorred disobedience. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari prefers that the angels asked God to inform them whether he would put such corrupt beings on earth and leave his deputies among them; their question was inquiry, not denial, though they abhorred disobedience. [1 notes: TAB-FULL+]
- 2:30/q07/p7 Al-Tabari says it is compatible with reports from Ibn Abbas and Ibn Masud, via al-Suddi and supported by Qatada, that God disclosed Adam’s descendants’ corruption and the angels asked to learn more. — TAB-FULL (السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وقتادة): Al-Tabari says it is compatible with reports from Ibn Abbas and Ibn Masud, via al-Suddi and supported by Qatada, that God disclosed Adam’s descendants’ corruption and the angels asked to learn more. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وقتادة)]
- 2:30/q07/p8 The angels ask to learn the wisdom, not to object: they seek clarification of the truth if their expectation is correct. — ZAJJAJ (author): The angels ask to learn the wisdom, not to object: they seek clarification of the truth if their expectation is correct. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q07/p9 Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet named the words God chose for His angels as the best speech. — THALABI (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet named the words God chose for His angels as the best speech. [1 notes: THALABI(أبو ذر)]
- 2:30/q07/p10 Angels neither eat nor drink, marry, or reproduce. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Angels neither eat nor drink, marry, or reproduce. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q07/p11 One report says ten thousand angels asked the question and God sent a fire that burned them. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): One report says ten thousand angels asked the question and God sent a fire that burned them. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:30/q07/p12 Some explain an omitted alternative: ‘Will You place one who corrupts and sheds blood, or one who does not?’ Like 39:9 and Abu Dhuʾayb’s ‘is her suitor rightly guided or astray?’, the angels ask to learn, — WAHIDI-BASIT (بعض أهل المعاني): Some explain an omitted alternative: ‘Will You place one who corrupts and sheds blood, or one who does not?’ Like 39:9 and Abu Dhuʾayb’s ‘is her suitor rightly guided or astray?’, the angels ask to learn, not to object; God had not disclosed Adam’s descendants’ conduct. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(بعض أهل المعاني)]
- 2:30/q07/p13 Other grammarians take the hamza as interrogative: the angels ask whether they are placed alongside a corrupter despite their glorification. — JISHUMI (غيرهما): Other grammarians take the hamza as interrogative: the angels ask whether they are placed alongside a corrupter despite their glorification. [1 notes: JISHUMI(غيرهما)]
- 2:30/q07/p14 Some say the angels asked God to make them the earthly inhabitants because earthly duties would be lighter. — JISHUMI (author): Some say the angels asked God to make them the earthly inhabitants because earthly duties would be lighter. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q07/p15 The angels asked with God’s permission, which He granted because He knew asking benefited them. — JISHUMI (author): The angels asked with God’s permission, which He granted because He knew asking benefited them. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q07/p16 Khalīfa means someone succeeded by another; here it may denote Adam alone with descendants included, or successors collectively, with the angels as the earlier earth-dwellers. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Khalīfa means someone succeeded by another; here it may denote Adam alone with descendants included, or successors collectively, with the angels as the earlier earth-dwellers. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q07/p17 Abu Abdillah reports that angels asked to be khalifa because they obey unlike others; after learning they had overstepped, they sought forgiveness at the Throne, and God directed Adam to build an earthly refuge for sinners. — TABRISI (أبو عبد الله): Abu Abdillah reports that angels asked to be khalifa because they obey unlike others; after learning they had overstepped, they sought forgiveness at the Throne, and God directed Adam to build an earthly refuge for sinners. [1 notes: TABRISI(أبو عبد الله)]
- 2:30/q07/p18 The other view identifies them as the angels with Iblīs when he was sent down to earth; Abū Ṣāliḥ transmits this from Ibn ʿAbbās. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): The other view identifies them as the angels with Iblīs when he was sent down to earth; Abū Ṣāliḥ transmits this from Ibn ʿAbbās. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q07/p19 Al-Zajjāj says the angels asked to learn the wisdom behind the decision, not as an objection. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says the angels asked to learn the wisdom behind the decision, not as an objection. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q07/p20 A third reading makes the angels ask about their own standing: will God place a corrupting creature there while they glorify Him, or not? — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): A third reading makes the angels ask about their own standing: will God place a corrupting creature there while they glorify Him, or not? [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 2:30/q07/p21 In their account, Light continually generates angels as friends without sexual reproduction, like wisdom from a wise person; Darkness similarly generates devils as enemies. — RAZI-FULL (معظم المجوس والثنوية): In their account, Light continually generates angels as friends without sexual reproduction, like wisdom from a wise person; Darkness similarly generates devils as enemies. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(معظم المجوس والثنوية)]
- 2:30/q07/p22 Most religious scholars hold that every angel is protected from every sin. — RAZI-FULL (الجمهور الأعظم من علماء الدين): Most religious scholars hold that every angel is protected from every sin. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الجمهور الأعظم من علماء الدين)]
- 2:30/q07/p23 The Mu'tazilite answer says God cannot do evil; its support is that the angels assign corruption to creatures, not the Creator. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): The Mu'tazilite answer says God cannot do evil; its support is that the angels assign corruption to creatures, not the Creator. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q07/p24 Most philosophers and many determinists say angels are pure good with no capacity for evil. — RAZI-FULL (جمهور الفلاسفة وكثير من أهل الجبر): Most philosophers and many determinists say angels are pure good with no capacity for evil. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(جمهور الفلاسفة وكثير من أهل الجبر)]
- 2:30/q07/p25 If the angels ask for the wisdom, God gives the general point; revealing the full detail might itself be harmful. — RAZI-FULL (author): If the angels ask for the wisdom, God gives the general point; revealing the full detail might itself be harmful. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q07/p26 The wording may be expanded elliptically: God announced a khalifa who would do these acts, and the angels asked whether this was the foretold one; both explanations are persuasive, and the first draws scholarly inference from — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The wording may be expanded elliptically: God announced a khalifa who would do these acts, and the angels asked whether this was the foretold one; both explanations are persuasive, and the first draws scholarly inference from the wording. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q07/p27 Al-Suddi's report says the angels asked what the khalifa would be, and God answered that he would have descendants who corrupt, envy one another, and kill one another. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi's report says the angels asked what the khalifa would be, and God answered that he would have descendants who corrupt, envy one another, and kill one another. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(السدي)]
- 2:30/q07/p28 A report says ten thousand angels asked the question and a fire from God burned them. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (يحيى بن أبي كثير): A report says ten thousand angels asked the question and a fire from God burned them. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(يحيى بن أبي كثير)]
- 2:30/q07/p29 The context identifies the unnamed vicegerent in God's announcement to the angels as Adam. — BURHAN (author): The context identifies the unnamed vicegerent in God's announcement to the angels as Adam. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q07/p30 Al-Naḥḥās prefers the explanation attributed to Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās: the angels asked whether the vicegerent would have descendants who corrupt and kill one another. — BURHAN (النحاس): Al-Naḥḥās prefers the explanation attributed to Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās: the angels asked whether the vicegerent would have descendants who corrupt and kill one another. [1 notes: BURHAN(النحاس)+]
- 2:30/q07/p31 In this account, God selected one of those angels as the first intellect and pen, taught it the intended creation and divine names, then derived the Tablet and commanded the Pen to record events through Resurrection. — ALUSI-FULL (أهل الله): In this account, God selected one of those angels as the first intellect and pen, taught it the intended creation and divine names, then derived the Tablet and commanded the Pen to record events through Resurrection. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أهل الله)]
- 2:30/q07/p32 The angels ask about the hidden wisdom and removal of their doubt, not whether the appointment itself will occur, which they already knew. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The angels ask about the hidden wisdom and removal of their doubt, not whether the appointment itself will occur, which they already knew. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q07/p33 God had not yet disclosed the degree of authority or its hidden wisdom, so the angels asked about the risk of corruption and bloodshed. — ELMALILI (author): God had not yet disclosed the degree of authority or its hidden wisdom, so the angels asked about the risk of corruption and bloodshed. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q07/p34 The angels ask to understand what they do not know; this is clarification rather than objection, as their acknowledgment that God is All-knowing and Wise accepts His wisdom. — TABATABAI (author): The angels ask to understand what they do not know; this is clarification rather than objection, as their acknowledgment that God is All-knowing and Wise accepts His wisdom. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:30/q07/p35 Angels have high rank, nearness to God, and great powers bestowed by him; they are set to act according to divine command and will and never depart from it. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Angels have high rank, nearness to God, and great powers bestowed by him; they are set to act according to divine command and will and never depart from it. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q07/p36 Some philosophers regard angels as beings of intellect, analogous to the human intellect. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Bazı filozoflar): Some philosophers regard angels as beings of intellect, analogous to the human intellect. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Bazı filozoflar)]
- 2:30/q07/p37 Angels assigned to the material realm are present throughout the physical cosmos and ensure God's laws are carried out there. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Angels assigned to the material realm are present throughout the physical cosmos and ensure God's laws are carried out there. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q07/p38 Mujahid glosses angelic praise as exalting and magnifying God. — MUJAHID (author): Mujahid glosses angelic praise as exalting and magnifying God. [1 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد)]
- 2:30/q07/p39 The author’s position is that angels are superior to all prophets, citing Qur’anic statements about not being an angel, becoming two angels, and Christ and the nearest angels serving God. — JISHUMI (author): The author’s position is that angels are superior to all prophets, citing Qur’anic statements about not being an angel, becoming two angels, and Christ and the nearest angels serving God. [1 notes: JISHUMI+]
- 2:30/q07/p40 Ali says angels neither imagine God bodily nor assign Him creaturely qualities, places, or likenesses. — RAZI-FULL (علي): Ali says angels neither imagine God bodily nor assign Him creaturely qualities, places, or likenesses. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(علي)]
- 2:30/q07/p41 Because many angels are assigned to take lives, Azrail may be their chief. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Because many angels are assigned to take lives, Azrail may be their chief. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q07/p42 Many angels are in Paradise to serve believers who enter it; their leader is called Ridvan. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Many angels are in Paradise to serve believers who enter it; their leader is called Ridvan. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q07/p43 Ibn Abbas says the angels thought none would be more honored or knowledgeable than them; Adam’s creation tested them, as all creation is tested, and heaven and earth were commanded to obey. — TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the angels thought none would be more honored or knowledgeable than them; Adam’s creation tested them, as all creation is tested, and heaven and earth were commanded to obey. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q07/p44 Ibn Ishaq paraphrases the angels’ words as a promise not to disobey or do anything God dislikes. — TAB-FULL (ابن إسحاق): Ibn Ishaq paraphrases the angels’ words as a promise not to disobey or do anything God dislikes. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن إسحاق)]
- 2:30/q07/p45 God will test those the angels expect to obey, and through the test guide them. — ZAJJAJ (author): God will test those the angels expect to obey, and through the test guide them. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q07/p46 God tells Muhammad to recall and tell them when his Lord spoke to the angels. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): God tells Muhammad to recall and tell them when his Lord spoke to the angels. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 2:30/q07/p47 The angels privately concluded that their Lord would create no creature more knowledgeable than they were. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The angels privately concluded that their Lord would create no creature more knowledgeable than they were. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 2:30/q07/p48 God favored Adam over the angels through knowledge and taught him the names of every thing, down to ordinary utensils. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): God favored Adam over the angels through knowledge and taught him the names of every thing, down to ordinary utensils. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 2:30/q07/p49 Ḥamd can mean acting at God’s command, as in the angels’ declaration and the response to the call on the Day of Resurrection. — DAMGHANI (author): Ḥamd can mean acting at God’s command, as in the angels’ declaration and the response to the call on the Day of Resurrection. [1 notes: DAMGHANI]
- 2:30/q07/p50 Qatāda says God meant the future virtuous deeds of prophets, excellent people, and obedient descendants of Adam, which the angels did not know. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says God meant the future virtuous deeds of prophets, excellent people, and obedient descendants of Adam, which the angels did not know. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q07/p51 The angels mean Adam's descendants who are neither prophets nor protected from sin, not Adam himself or other prophets and protected people. — TABRISI (author): The angels mean Adam's descendants who are neither prophets nor protected from sin, not Adam himself or other prophets and protected people. [1 notes: TABRISI]
