## 2:30/q28 Who had the rightful early imamate?  [خَلِيفَةًۭ · law]
- 2:30/q28/p1 Salman defines a caliph as one who rules justly, distributes equally, cares for subjects like family, and judges by God’s Book. — THALABI (سلمان): Salman defines a caliph as one who rules justly, distributes equally, cares for subjects like family, and judges by God’s Book. [1 notes: THALABI(سلمان)]
- 2:30/q28/p2 Ka‘b says he thought no one present besides himself knew the difference between caliph and king, then praises Salman for wisdom, knowledge, and justice. — THALABI (كعب): Ka‘b says he thought no one present besides himself knew the difference between caliph and king, then praises Salman for wisdom, knowledge, and justice. [1 notes: THALABI(كعب)]
- 2:30/q28/p3 Salman tells Umar that a ruler who collects even a little Muslim tax and spends it wrongly is a king; Umar weeps at the answer. — THALABI (سلمان): Salman tells Umar that a ruler who collects even a little Muslim tax and spends it wrongly is a king; Umar weeps at the answer. [1 notes: THALABI(سلمان)]
- 2:30/q28/p4 Mu‘awiya says caliphate means upholding truth, ruling justly, and directing people to God’s command, rather than accumulating or distributing wealth. — THALABI (معاوية): Mu‘awiya says caliphate means upholding truth, ruling justly, and directing people to God’s command, rather than accumulating or distributing wealth. [1 notes: THALABI(معاوية)]
- 2:30/q28/p5 Once the imamate is validly concluded by the council or one member, everyone must pledge obedience and uphold God's Book and the Prophet's Sunna. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Once the imamate is validly concluded by the council or one member, everyone must pledge obedience and uphold God's Book and the Prophet's Sunna. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p6 Abu Saʿid al-Khudri reports the Prophet's instruction to kill the later of two pledged caliphs; Muslim transmits it. — QURTUBI-FULL (النبي): Abu Saʿid al-Khudri reports the Prophet's instruction to kill the later of two pledged caliphs; Muslim transmits it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي)]
- 2:30/q28/p7 Al-Qurtubi says the verse establishes the duty to appoint an imam or caliph to unify the community and enforce rulings; the community agrees except al-Asamm and followers. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Qurtubi says the verse establishes the duty to appoint an imam or caliph to unify the community and enforce rulings; the community agrees except al-Asamm and followers. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p8 Al-Qurtubi adduces this verse, David’s caliphate, and the promise to make believers successors as proof of appointment’s obligation. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Qurtubi adduces this verse, David’s caliphate, and the promise to make believers successors as proof of appointment’s obligation. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p9 Abu Bakr designated ʿUmar, and no one objected that the office was unnecessary; al-Qurtubi calls the imamate obligatory and a pillar sustaining Muslims. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Abu Bakr designated ʿUmar, and no one objected that the office was unnecessary; al-Qurtubi calls the imamate obligatory and a pillar sustaining Muslims. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p10 Those requiring text alone reject analogy and independent reasoning; factions claim designation for Abu Bakr, al-ʿAbbas, or ʿAli. — QURTUBI-FULL (الإمامية): Those requiring text alone reject analogy and independent reasoning; factions claim designation for Abu Bakr, al-ʿAbbas, or ʿAli. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الإمامية)]
- 2:30/q28/p11 Mass transmission would make a designation as universally evident as prayer, Ramadan fasting, and pilgrimage; solitary reports cannot establish certainty. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Mass transmission would make a designation as universally evident as prayer, Ramadan fasting, and pilgrimage; solitary reports cannot establish certainty. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p12 Conflicting groups transmit designation for different imams, so none has priority; without a reliable path to a text, selection and reasoning follow. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Conflicting groups transmit designation for different imams, so none has priority; without a reliable path to a text, selection and reasoning follow. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p13 Al-Qurtubi says reports for Abu Bakr could be assembled against claims of mass designation; the far larger body outside the Imamis denies any designation, so necessary knowledge cannot be denied by a smaller group. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Qurtubi says reports for Abu Bakr could be assembled against claims of mass designation; the far larger body outside the Imamis denies any designation, so necessary knowledge cannot be denied by a smaller group. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p14 The Imamis cite the Prophet’s statement that whoever has him as mawla has ʿAli as mawla as proof of ʿAli’s appointment. — QURTUBI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Imamis cite the Prophet’s statement that whoever has him as mawla has ʿAli as mawla as proof of ʿAli’s appointment. - 2:30/q28/p15 The Imamis say mawla means one with greater right, so the Prophet’s statement establishes ʿAli’s imamate and obligatory obedience. — QURTUBI-FULL (الإمامية): The Imamis say mawla means one with greater right, so the Prophet’s statement establishes ʿAli’s imamate and obligatory obedience. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الإمامية)]
- 2:30/q28/p16 They argue that comparing ʿAli to Aaron implies succession, since Aaron was Moses’ deputy, brother, and prophetic partner. — QURTUBI-FULL (الإمامية): They argue that comparing ʿAli to Aaron implies succession, since Aaron was Moses’ deputy, brother, and prophetic partner. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الإمامية)]
- 2:30/q28/p17 Al-Qurtubi says the Ghadir report is not mass-transmitted and its soundness is disputed; Abu Dawud and Abu Hatim criticize it as contradicted by another prophetic report. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Qurtubi says the Ghadir report is not mass-transmitted and its soundness is disputed; Abu Dawud and Abu Hatim criticize it as contradicted by another prophetic report. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p18 One reported occasion is a dispute between ʿAli and Usama: after Usama denied being ʿAli’s mawla, the Prophet made the statement. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): One reported occasion is a dispute between ʿAli and Usama: after Usama denied being ʿAli’s mawla, the Prophet made the statement. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p19 Another reported occasion is the slander of ʿAli after his comment about Aisha; the Prophet defended him against hypocrites, whom some Companions identified by their hatred of ʿAli. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Another reported occasion is the slander of ʿAli after his comment about Aisha; the Prophet defended him against hypocrites, whom some Companions identified by their hatred of ʿAli. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p20 The comparison means ʿAli was left in charge of the Prophet’s household during his life and absence, as Aaron deputized for Moses. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The comparison means ʿAli was left in charge of the Prophet’s household during his life and absence, as Aaron deputized for Moses. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p21 During Tabuk the Prophet left Ali in Medina; after hypocrites alleged dislike, he told Ali the allegation was false and compared his position to Aaron's with Moses. — QURTUBI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): During Tabuk the Prophet left Ali in Medina; after hypocrites alleged dislike, he told Ali the allegation was false and compared his position to Aaron's with Moses. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q28/p22 The appointment did not uniquely distinguish Ali: the Prophet left other companions in charge during expeditions, and this report is solitary through Saʿd b. Abi Waqqas. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The appointment did not uniquely distinguish Ali: the Prophet left other companions in charge during expeditions, and this report is solitary through Saʿd b. Abi Waqqas. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p23 When sending Muʿadh to Yemen, the Prophet said Abu Bakr and Umar were indispensable to him, like hearing and sight to the head, and called them his ministers on earth. — QURTUBI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When sending Muʿadh to Yemen, the Prophet said Abu Bakr and Umar were indispensable to him, like hearing and sight to the head, and called them his ministers on earth. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q28/p24 A report says the Prophet described Abu Bakr and Umar as being to him like Aaron to Moses; unlike Ali's report, it was stated without a precipitating occasion. — QURTUBI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report says the Prophet described Abu Bakr and Umar as being to him like Aaron to Moses; unlike Ali's report, it was stated without a precipitating occasion. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q28/p25 Because Abu Bakr's and Umar's report was initiated without a specific occasion while Ali's answered one, the author concludes Abu Bakr has the stronger claim to the imamate. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Because Abu Bakr's and Umar's report was initiated without a specific occasion while Ali's answered one, the author concludes Abu Bakr has the stronger claim to the imamate. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 2:30/q28/p26 One route to establish an imam is explicit designation; the Prophet indicated Abu Bakr, who in turn designated Umar. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): One route to establish an imam is explicit designation; the Prophet indicated Abu Bakr, who in turn designated Umar. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p27 A predecessor may designate a group and leave the choice among them; Umar did so, and the companions selected Uthman. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): A predecessor may designate a group and leave the choice among them; Umar did so, and the companions selected Uthman. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p28 Abu al-Maʿali says an imamate concluded by one person's pledge is binding and cannot be revoked absent a change or event; he calls this consensus. — QURTUBI-FULL (أبو المعالي): Abu al-Maʿali says an imamate concluded by one person's pledge is binding and cannot be revoked absent a change or event; he calls this consensus. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو المعالي)]
- 2:30/q28/p29 Al-Jubbaʾi requires four witnesses, a contracting party, and the candidate, using Umar's six-person council as evidence. — QURTUBI-FULL (الجبائي): Al-Jubbaʾi requires four witnesses, a contracting party, and the candidate, using Umar's six-person council as evidence. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الجبائي)]
- 2:30/q28/p30 A less excellent candidate may be appointed when choosing the superior risks civil strife or disables the public functions for which the imam is installed; Umar's council supplies precedent. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): A less excellent candidate may be appointed when choosing the superior risks civil strife or disables the public functions for which the imam is installed; Umar's council supplies precedent. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 2:30/q28/p31 Al-Jubba’i requires four witnesses, a contracting party and the person contracted for; he derives the four witnesses from the remaining members of Umar’s six-person council, while Ibn Kathir finds this reasoning questionable. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (الجبائي): Al-Jubba’i requires four witnesses, a contracting party and the person contracted for; he derives the four witnesses from the remaining members of Umar’s six-person council, while Ibn Kathir finds this reasoning questionable. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الجبائي)]
- 2:30/q28/p32 One Sunni group holds that the imamate is established by explicit designation, as they say it was for Abu Bakr. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (طائفة من أهل السنة): One Sunni group holds that the imamate is established by explicit designation, as they say it was for Abu Bakr. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(طائفة من أهل السنة)]
- 2:30/q28/p33 The imamate may be established when a caliph appoints a successor, as Abu Bakr did with Umar ibn al-Khattab. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): The imamate may be established when a caliph appoints a successor, as Abu Bakr did with Umar ibn al-Khattab. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p34 A caliph may leave the choice to a council of righteous people, as Umar did. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): A caliph may leave the choice to a council of righteous people, as Umar did. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 2:30/q28/p35 The Karramiyya allow two or more imams, treating Ali and Muawiyah as simultaneou — IBNKATHIR-FULL (الكرامية): The Karramiyya allow two or more imams, treating Ali and Muawiyah as simultaneously obligatory-to-obey imams and reasoning by analogy from the permissibility of multiple prophets, whose rank is higher. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الكرامية)]
- 2:30/q28/p36 Political caliphate is a concept and institution that arose after the Prophet from practical need; the Qur'an does not use the term for it. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Political caliphate is a concept and institution that arose after the Prophet from practical need; the Qur'an does not use the term for it. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q28/p37 The Qur'anic term for those who perform the political function of caliphate is ulu al-amr. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The Qur'anic term for those who perform the political function of caliphate is ulu al-amr. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q28/p38 A hadith says caliphate lasts thirty years after the Prophet and is foll — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): A hadith says caliphate lasts thirty years after the Prophet and is followed by oppressive rule; if authentic, it is the first use of caliphate as an office in Islamic sources, though its authenticity is questioned. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q28/p39 Abu Bakr was called the Messenger of God's caliph because he succeeded him as head of state; Umar was called the caliph's caliph, after which the shorter title caliph prevailed, alongside titles such as commander of — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Abu Bakr was called the Messenger of God's caliph because he succeeded him as head of state; Umar was called the caliph's caliph, after which the shorter title caliph prevailed, alongside titles such as commander of the faithful and imam. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q28/p40 Al-Qurtubi replies that even if sound, the Ghadir report proves ʿAli’s merit rather than imamate: mawla can mean wali, as in 66:4. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Qurtubi replies that even if sound, the Ghadir report proves ʿAli’s merit rather than imamate: mawla can mean wali, as in 66:4. ## 2:30/q29 How is the angels' reply joined to the announcement?  [قَالُوٓا۟ · grammar]
- 2:30/q29/p1 The question is introduced with an implied fa, effectively meaning “so they said”; Thaʿlabī cites poetry for this omission. — THALABI (author): The question is introduced with an implied fa, effectively meaning “so they said”; Thaʿlabī cites poetry for this omission. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q29/p2 Al-Farra says a coordinating fa is omitted before the angels’ speech, as in a poem where ‘I was a neighbor to them’ means ‘so I was a neighbor to them.’ — WAHIDI-BASIT (الفراء): Al-Farra says a coordinating fa is omitted before the angels’ speech, as in a poem where ‘I was a neighbor to them’ means ‘so I was a neighbor to them.’ [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الفراء)]
- 2:30/q29/p3 In Arabic, successive quoted statements normally need no conjunction marking sequence because their semantic order is clear; he cites the dialogue about the angels and Adam and many Quranic examples. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): In Arabic, successive quoted statements normally need no conjunction marking sequence because their semantic order is clear; he cites the dialogue about the angels and Adam and many Quranic examples. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q29/p4 In Qur’anic dialogue, replies normally begin with uncoordinated “he said”; a conjunction appears when the narrative shifts to another conversation. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): In Qur’anic dialogue, replies normally begin with uncoordinated “he said”; a conjunction appears when the narrative shifts to another conversation. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:30/q30 What is the force and purpose of the angels' question?  [أَتَجْعَلُ · interpretation]
turns on: Whether the interrogative asks for wisdom, expresses astonishment, affirms a fact, or objects.
- 2:30/q30/p1 The question seeks understanding of God's wisdom — QUSHAYRI (author): The angels' question is inquiry, not objection to God's decree; Qushayri prefers the reading that preserves their immunity from sin. TABRISI (author): A second view takes the angels' question as a request to understand the plan's wisdom, not an objection or claim of foreknowledge. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): God informed the angels to show that their knowledge could not encompass His wisdom, which they acknowledged in their question. RAZI-FULL (author): Their question may express amazement that God grants blessings to corrupt beings, magnifying His perfect knowledge and wisdom. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): The angels' question was neither objection nor envy; it sought the wisdom of creating people who may corrupt and kill, when the angels worship without doing so, and why creation was not limited to them. BURHAN (author): God’s answer to the angels’ question about corruption presupposes wisdom and interests known to Him; otherwise neither their question about His wisdom nor this answer would make sense. ELMALILI (author): The angels' question was an inquiry about wisdom, not an objection, though it implicitly showed their own desire for vicegerency. BURHAN (author): The angels’ question is apparently a request for guidance, with its phrasing shaped by etiquette; another view takes it as wonder. ITQAN (author): Interrogation can seek guidance, as in the angels’ question about placing a corrupter on earth. [9 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BURHAN; ELMALILI; ITQAN; QUSHAYRI+]
- 2:30/q30/p2 The question expresses wonder at the appointment — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Their question may express wonder at God appointing a disobedient creature, at the appointee disobeying despite divine favor, or at both appointment and disobedience. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): The interrogative hamza also conveys astonishment, according to Makkī and others, at God's appointing a successor who would disobey or be disobeyed. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): For surprise, magnification, or confirmation, the question has no complement; for the plain-question view it is من يفسد, while the other view makes the circumstantial clause its complement. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): On this reading, the angels' statement is a circumstantial clause governed by tajʿalu, while the implied astonishment and challenge also let the interrogative act as its governing element. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): One explanation is that God permitted the angels to ask after telling them humans would corrupt the earth; their question expressed amazement at how created beings could disobey their Creator. JISHUMI (author): A further proposal is that the angels wondered whether God’s successor could be corrupt and sinful. NASAFI-FULL (author): The angels wondered that obedient inhabitants would be replaced by sinners, while affirming God’s wisdom and knowledge. BURHAN (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda likens the angels' question to someone striking a servant while saying, “Will you do this?”; another view calls it wonder, which the source weakens. STUDYQURAN (many commentators): Many commentators read the angels’ apparently audacious question as wonder at God’s Wisdom, not doubt or concern; they are not alarmed. [9 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مكي); IBNASHUR-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير); JISHUMI; NASAFI-FULL; BURHAN(أبو عبيدة); STUDYQURAN(many commentators)]
- 2:30/q30/p3 The question affirms a fact rather than requesting information — ZAJJAJ (قوم): Another account says God told the angels some descendants of Adam would shed blood and allowed their question to affirm that He knows the unseen. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): One analysis takes the interrogative alif as emphatic confirmation, not a genuine question; Jarīr’s line uses a question to assert that the addressees are best. RAZI-FULL (جرير): Jarir's verse beginning فألستم خير من ركب المطايا shows an interrogative form can make an affirmative assertion. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda reads the question as a request for confirmation, illustrated by an unnamed poet's rhetorical question. SAMIN-DURR (unspecified): Another view treats the hamza as confirmation, supported by the poetic question ألستم خير من ركب المطايا. [5 notes: ZAJJAJ(قوم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(جرير); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عبيدة); SAMIN-DURR(unspecified)]
- 2:30/q30/p4 The question is an objection — FARISI-HUJJA (author): The passage raises an objection that hamza may open the preceding letter even when hamza itself has ḍamma, but provides no answer in this fragment. RAZI-FULL (المخالف): An opponent says the question is an objection to God and therefore a grave sin. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Their question is neither objection nor backbiting against humanity: angels are too honored for that, as 21:26–27 says they do not precede God in speech and obey Him. [3 notes: FARISI-HUJJA; RAZI-FULL(المخالف); BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q30/p5 In another report, Qatada says God had informed the angels that earthly creatures would corrupt and shed blood, which explains their question. — TAB-FULL (قتادة): In another report, Qatada says God had informed the angels that earthly creatures would corrupt and shed blood, which explains their question. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q30/p6 Al-Rabi b. Anas says angels were created Wednesday, jinn Thursday and Adam Friday; after some jinn rebelled, angels fought them on earth, causing the bloodshed and corruption behind their question. — TAB-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi b. Anas says angels were created Wednesday, jinn Thursday and Adam Friday; after some jinn rebelled, angels fought them on earth, causing the bloodshed and corruption behind their question. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الربيع بن أنس)]
- 2:30/q30/p7 In a report from Ibn Abbas, God says He is creating humans who will envy and kill one another and corrupt the earth, explaining the angels' question. — IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): In a report from Ibn Abbas, God says He is creating humans who will envy and kill one another and corrupt the earth, explaining the angels' question. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q30/p8 The interrogative does not imply doubt that God will appoint a successor; it asks whether the angels’ state and the appointment are equally sound. — JISHUMI (author): The interrogative does not imply doubt that God will appoint a successor; it asks whether the angels’ state and the appointment are equally sound. [1 notes: JISHUMI]

## 2:30/q31 How did the angels know or infer future corruption?  [أَتَجْعَلُ فِيهَا مَن يُفْسِدُ · interpretation]
turns on: Whether the angels received disclosure or inferred from earlier beings, human nature, or the title khalīfa.
- 2:30/q31/p1 The angels learned it from the Preserved Tablet — QASHANI (author): Every created event first has a form in the spirit world of prior decree, then the heart-world of the Preserved Tablet, then the soul-world, Tablet of Effacement and Affirmation or lowest heaven. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو جعفر محمد بن علي الباقر): A report says the angel al-Sijill saw Adam’s creation and future in the Preserved Book and told Harut and Marut, who spoke out of superiority over the angels. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One view says the angels learned of human corruption from the Preserved Tablet; al-Alusi objects that this leaves their question unanswered and needs a strained reply. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): One proposed explanation is that the angels learned about human wrongdoing from the Preserved Tablet, where future events are recorded. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The angels may have learned of the corruption from God’s disclosure or from the Preserved Tablet. RAZI-FULL (الملائكة): They may have read the Preserved Tablet, where events through the Resurrection had been written. NASAFI-FULL (author): The angels knew through God’s information, the Preserved Tablet, or analogy between the two kinds of beings. [7 notes: QASHANI; IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو جعفر محمد بن علي الباقر); ALUSI-FULL(قيل); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL(الملائكة); NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q31/p2 God had informed them — TAB-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Al-Hasan and Qatada report that God told the angels he would act, gave them some knowledge but withheld other knowledge, and they asked on the basis of what they had been taught, knowing bloodshed was a grave sin. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj transmits an unnamed explanation: God told angels humanity would corrupt and shed blood; they asked why, expecting creatures to glorify and purify God like them, and the disclosure confirmed divine knowledge of the unseen. TABRISI (author): The khalifa is Adam, God's deputy on earth who judges justly; Ibn Abbas and Ibn Masud say God informed the angels that some descendants would corrupt. TABRISI (ابن عباس وابن مسعود): Ibn Abbas and Ibn Masud report that God told angels about future sinful descendants; He disclosed the unseen and events later confirmed His report. RAZI-FULL (ابن مسعود وناس من الصحابة): One certainty report says God told angels the successor's descendants would corrupt, envy, and kill. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd and others say God informed the angels that Adam's descendants would corrupt and shed blood, so they expressed astonishment at appointing one who would disobey despite God's favor, or at the disobedience itself, or magnified both matters. ALUSI-FULL (author): The angels' judgment that humans would corrupt and shed blood need not claim knowledge of the unseen or conjecture: God informed them, as the divine reply implies, and the Qur'an omits the explicit explanation. IBNASHUR-FULL (بعض المفسرين): Some commentators proposed that God informed the angels of human traits or showed them the Preserved Tablet; Ibn ʿĀshūr says his explanation makes this speculation unnecessary. [7 notes: TAB-FULL(الحسن وقتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج); TABRISI(ابن عباس وابن مسعود); RAZI-FULL(ابن مسعود وناس من الصحابة); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن زيد); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(بعض المفسرين)-]
- 2:30/q31/p3 The angels inferred it from embodied human nature — RAZI-FULL (author): Another conjecture was that the four humors produce appetite and anger, leading to corruption and bloodshed. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Ibn Kathir says the khalifa is apparently not Adam alone: the angels' prediction of corruption and bloodshed concerns a human kind, and he presents possible grounds for their knowledge, including special information, human nature, or analogy with prior inhabitants. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The angels knew that God's purpose in creating the earth was its order and reform, learned through revelation, the nature of vicegerency, or signs of God's care and intent that it endure. [3 notes: RAZI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL+]
- 2:30/q31/p4 God had disclosed future corruption to the angels — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): God informed the angels beforehand so they would ask and receive an explanation of the wisdom, preventing doubts when the appointment occurred. NASAFI-FULL (author): God informed the angels either to elicit a question and reveal the appointment’s wisdom or to teach people to consult before acting, though God needs no counsel. IBNKATHIR-FULL (قتادة): God told the angels that any creatures placed on earth would cause corruption and shed blood, so they asked this question. [3 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q31/p5 The angels inferred by analogy with earlier earthly beings — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The angels anticipated human corruption by analogy with the jinn, reasoning from the known to the unseen. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): A further explanation is that the angels inferred human conduct by analogy with jinn or predatory animals. [2 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q31/p6 Linguists infer God had told angels descendants would corrupt; the abbreviated question presupposes it. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Linguists infer God had told angels descendants would corrupt; the abbreviated question presupposes it. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB(أهل النظر من أصحاب اللغة)]
- 2:30/q31/p7 God allowed Satan's whisper under prior knowledge and decree. — TUSTARI (author): God allowed Satan's whisper under prior knowledge and decree. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q31/p8 Tabari asks how angels knew future corruption without unseen knowledge. — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari asks how angels knew future corruption without unseen knowledge. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q31/p9 He suspects a transmitter misreported the account; a plausible original reading is that angels inferred too much from God’s statement about corrupt descendants and were challenged to prove what they presumed to know. — TAB-FULL (author): He suspects a transmitter misreported the account; a plausible original reading is that angels inferred too much from God’s statement about corrupt descendants and were challenged to prove what they presumed to know. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q31/p10 Al-Tabari reads this Qatada account as the angels speaking from conjecture, not certain prior knowledge; God counters that Adam’s progeny will include prophets and people devoted to obedience. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari reads this Qatada account as the angels speaking from conjecture, not certain prior knowledge; God counters that Adam’s progeny will include prophets and people devoted to obedience. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]

[1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن زيد)]
