- 2:30/q31/p15 Qatada says God had told the angels that creatures on earth would corrupt and shed blood; they asked whether this khalifa was the one foretold or another. Al-Qurtubi calls this a good view and also praises — QURTUBI-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says God had told the angels that creatures on earth would corrupt and shed blood; they asked whether this khalifa was the one foretold or another. Al-Qurtubi calls this a good view and also praises inference from the wording. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قتادة)+]
- 2:30/q31/p16 Because angels neither know the unseen nor speak first, their question must rest on prior information or a premise. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Because angels neither know the unseen nor speak first, their question must rest on prior information or a premise. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q31/p17 Angels saw Adam’s body before the spirit was breathed into it and perceived its elemental mixture and human, animal, and predatory traits, reasoning by analogy with animals already created. — BURSEVI (author): Angels saw Adam’s body before the spirit was breathed into it and perceived its elemental mixture and human, animal, and predatory traits, reasoning by analogy with animals already created. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q31/p18 The angels question the appointment because of corruption and bloodshed; God cuts off their fears and conjectures by asserting His unlimited knowledge. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): The angels question the appointment because of corruption and bloodshed; God cuts off their fears and conjectures by asserting His unlimited knowledge. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q31/p19 The separate-word hamza in these readings is analogized to a hamza inside one word, as Arabic often extends connected-word patterns across word boundaries. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): The separate-word hamza in these readings is analogized to a hamza inside one word, as Arabic often extends connected-word patterns across word boundaries. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q31/p20 Ibn Masud and a group of Companions are cited for the view that angels knew with certainty. — RAZI-FULL (ابن مسعود وناس من الصحابة): Ibn Masud and a group of Companions are cited for the view that angels knew with certainty. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن مسعود وناس من الصحابة)]
- 2:30/q31/p21 Ibn Zayd says angels learned sin would arise when they asked whom Hell was for and were told it was for disobedient creatures. — RAZI-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says angels learned sin would arise when they asked whom Hell was for and were told it was for disobedient creatures. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن زيد)]
- 2:30/q31/p22 Another proposal is that similar beings had earlier been created and sent to earth, and the angels learned by observing them. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Another proposal is that similar beings had earlier been created and sent to earth, and the angels learned by observing them. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q31/p23 Some commentators analogized to an earlier nation that had vanished; Ibn ʿĀshūr says his prior explanation removes the need for this proposal. — IBNASHUR-FULL (بعض المفسرين): Some commentators analogized to an earlier nation that had vanished; Ibn ʿĀshūr says his prior explanation removes the need for this proposal. [0 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(بعض المفسرين)-]

## 2:30/q32 Who corrupts, and what is the corruption?  [مَن يُفْسِدُ فِيهَا · interpretation]
- 2:30/q32/p1 Abd al-Rahman b. Sabit takes the angels’ description of the corrupt blood-shedders to mean human beings. — TAB-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن سابط): Abd al-Rahman b. Sabit takes the angels’ description of the corrupt blood-shedders to mean human beings. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن سابط)]
- 2:30/q32/p2 Adam names every thing shown to him, including peoples; the angels’ public concern was human corruption, while their concealed claim was that they were better and more knowledgeable. — TAB-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): Adam names every thing shown to him, including peoples; the angels’ public concern was human corruption, while their concealed claim was that they were better and more knowledgeable. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الحسن وقتادة)]
- 2:30/q32/p3 Qatadah says God knew earthly creatures would corrupt and shed blood; the angels meant humans. — IBNABIHATIM (قتادة): Qatadah says God knew earthly creatures would corrupt and shed blood; the angels meant humans. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q32/p4 Ibn Sabit likewise identifies those who corrupt and shed blood as humans. — IBNABIHATIM (ابن سابط): Ibn Sabit likewise identifies those who corrupt and shed blood as humans. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن سابط)]
- 2:30/q32/p5 Another explanation is their astonishment that God would appoint them despite knowing they would corrupt and shed blood. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Another explanation is their astonishment that God would appoint them despite knowing they would corrupt and shed blood. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p6 The angels mean disobedience and unlawful bloodshed, as the jinn had done; they inferred the unseen from what they had witnessed, though they did not know it directly. — BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The angels mean disobedience and unlawful bloodshed, as the jinn had done; they inferred the unseen from what they had witnessed, though they did not know it directly. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p7 Others say God told the angels before this announcement that creatures would corrupt and shed blood; they then asked whether this successor was the one foretold or someone else. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (آخرون): Others say God told the angels before this announcement that creatures would corrupt and shed blood; they then asked whether this successor was the one foretold or someone else. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(آخرون)]
- 2:30/q32/p8 An opponent says accusing humans of corruption and killing is backbiting, a major sin. — RAZI-FULL (المخالف): An opponent says accusing humans of corruption and killing is backbiting, a major sin. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المخالف)]
- 2:30/q32/p9 Al-Dahhak transmits from Ibn Abbas that God addressed angels who fought with Iblis, after their forces expelled corrupting jinn from earth. — RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Al-Dahhak transmits from Ibn Abbas that God addressed angels who fought with Iblis, after their forces expelled corrupting jinn from earth. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q32/p10 The angels did not backbite: those posing the problem had to mention its difficulty, corruption and killing, not worship and monotheism. — RAZI-FULL (author): The angels did not backbite: those posing the problem had to mention its difficulty, corruption and killing, not worship and monotheism. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p11 They argue that if God created people for disbelief, his answer should have admitted he created them to corrupt and kill; its refusal supports the Muʿtazilite doctrine of justice. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): They argue that if God created people for disbelief, his answer should have admitted he created them to corrupt and kill; its refusal supports the Muʿtazilite doctrine of justice. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q32/p12 They argue that if corruption and killing were God’s acts, they would be like colors and bodies, which are not ordinarily met with wonder. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): They argue that if corruption and killing were God’s acts, they would be like colors and bodies, which are not ordinarily met with wonder. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q32/p13 The answer may mean that, despite corruption and killing, God knows there will also be many righteous and devout people among humanity. — RAZI-FULL (author): The answer may mean that, despite corruption and killing, God knows there will also be many righteous and devout people among humanity. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p14 They argue that if God caused corruption and killing, his answer should have been that he owns creation and does as he wills. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): They argue that if God caused corruption and killing, his answer should have been that he owns creation and does as he wills. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q32/p15 In the exchange with the angels, God answers their concern about human corruption by saying He knows what they do not, setting knowledge itself as the relevant answer. — RAZI-FULL (author): In the exchange with the angels, God answers their concern about human corruption by saying He knows what they do not, setting knowledge itself as the relevant answer. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p16 Some allusive interpreters say the angels thought God had consulted them about keeping the vicegerency with worshippers, so they advised against appointing those who would corrupt and kill; God decides. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض أهل الإشارات): Some allusive interpreters say the angels thought God had consulted them about keeping the vicegerency with worshippers, so they advised against appointing those who would corrupt and kill; God decides. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعض أهل الإشارات)]
- 2:30/q32/p17 Another view takes the bloodshed comprehensively, including lawful and unlawful blood, with the point that the angels could not distinguish among its kinds. — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another view takes the bloodshed comprehensively, including lawful and unlawful blood, with the point that the angels could not distinguish among its kinds. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q32/p18 Another reading hears astonishment that an earth-reformer should be appointed from among those who corrupt, or that disobedient people replace obedient inhabitants. — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another reading hears astonishment that an earth-reformer should be appointed from among those who corrupt, or that disobedient people replace obedient inhabitants. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q32/p19 As noted in 2:11–12, corruption can also mean degradation of the natural environment. — STUDYQURAN (author): As noted in 2:11–12, corruption can also mean degradation of the natural environment. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]
- 2:30/q32/p20 God had informed the angels of earlier earthly corruption and bloodshed, says Qatada. — ABDURRAZZAQ (author): God had informed the angels of earlier earthly corruption and bloodshed, says Qatada. [1 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q32/p21 Tabari interprets Adam and just descendants as divine deputies, distinguished from corrupt descendants. — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari interprets Adam and just descendants as divine deputies, distinguished from corrupt descendants. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p22 Al-Hasan al-Basri is among the interpreters who share Qatada’s view that God had already informed the angels of the coming corruption and bloodshed. — TAB-FULL (الحسن البصري): Al-Hasan al-Basri is among the interpreters who share Qatada’s view that God had already informed the angels of the coming corruption and bloodshed. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الحسن البصري)]
- 2:30/q32/p23 If God had disclosed the future corruption, Al-Tabari says the angels could be asking whether it would in fact occur and asking God to make them earth’s deputies so that no one would disobey. — TAB-FULL (author): If God had disclosed the future corruption, Al-Tabari says the angels could be asking whether it would in fact occur and asking God to make them earth’s deputies so that no one would disobey. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p24 Hasan says God put in the angels' hearts that humans would repeat the jinn's corruption and bloodshed, so they asked what they did. — IBNABIHATIM (الحسن): Hasan says God put in the angels' hearts that humans would repeat the jinn's corruption and bloodshed, so they asked what they did. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(الحسن)]
- 2:30/q32/p25 Some grammarians supply an omitted alternative: will God appoint one who does not corrupt or shed blood? They compare the ellipsis in 39:9. — THALABI (بعض أهل المعاني): Some grammarians supply an omitted alternative: will God appoint one who does not corrupt or shed blood? They compare the ellipsis in 39:9. [1 notes: THALABI(بعض أهل المعاني)]
- 2:30/q32/p26 Al-Hasan ibn al-Faḍl selects the interpretation that supplies the unstated contrast with people who do not corrupt or shed blood. — THALABI (الحسن بن الفضل): Al-Hasan ibn al-Faḍl selects the interpretation that supplies the unstated contrast with people who do not corrupt or shed blood. [1 notes: THALABI(الحسن بن الفضل)+]
- 2:30/q32/p27 Ibn ʿAbbās says Adam and his descendants replaced the jinn, who had corrupted the earth and shed blood. — MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says Adam and his descendants replaced the jinn, who had corrupted the earth and shed blood. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q32/p28 One explanation of their remark is dismay that creatures blessed and appointed to earth would corrupt it. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): One explanation of their remark is dismay that creatures blessed and appointed to earth would corrupt it. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p29 Qatāda says God refers to the prophets and messengers among Adam’s descendants, who will act righteously rather than corrupt the earth. — MAWARDI-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says God refers to the prophets and messengers among Adam’s descendants, who will act righteously rather than corrupt the earth. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q32/p30 They may instead have inferred from one of the two humanlike communities that had inhabited and corrupted the earth before the angels. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): They may instead have inferred from one of the two humanlike communities that had inhabited and corrupted the earth before the angels. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p31 Many commentators say angels expelled corrupt jinn from earth and asked whether God would place there beings like those former corrupters. — TABRISI (كثير من المفسرين): Many commentators say angels expelled corrupt jinn from earth and asked whether God would place there beings like those former corrupters. [1 notes: TABRISI(كثير من المفسرين)]
- 2:30/q32/p32 Ibn ʿAbbās says they purify themselves for God from the deeds of the corrupt beings. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says they purify themselves for God from the deeds of the corrupt beings. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q32/p33 Another possibility is that they sought the wisdom for creating beings who would corrupt and kill. — RAZI-FULL (author): Another possibility is that they sought the wisdom for creating beings who would corrupt and kill. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p34 They ask how God can be praised as transcendent if every indecency, ugliness, injustice, oppression, and corruption is created by his making and will. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): They ask how God can be praised as transcendent if every indecency, ugliness, injustice, oppression, and corruption is created by his making and will. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q32/p35 They argue that God’s answer disavows corruption and murder, which would be impossible if those acts were his own. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): They argue that God’s answer disavows corruption and murder, which would be impossible if those acts were his own. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q32/p36 The angels understood humans to combine appetite and anger, which could cause corruption and bloodshed, with intellect, which prompts knowledge and obedience. Considering the powers separately, they thought angels could realize intellect’s benefits without those harms. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The angels understood humans to combine appetite and anger, which could cause corruption and bloodshed, with intellect, which prompts knowledge and obedience. Considering the powers separately, they thought angels could realize intellect’s benefits without those harms. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p37 The angels’ words about corruption and bloodshed refer to Adam’s descendants. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن سابط): The angels’ words about corruption and bloodshed refer to Adam’s descendants. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن سابط)]
- 2:30/q32/p38 Repeating “therein” stresses the extent of corruption; its omission after the mention of bloodshed is sufficient and rhetorically apt. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Repeating “therein” stresses the extent of corruption; its omission after the mention of bloodshed is sufficient and rhetorically apt. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p39 The angels judged what humanity could do by its potential, not by acts already committed or by examining individuals; this does not mean every human, unlike Adam, would corrupt. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The angels judged what humanity could do by its potential, not by acts already committed or by examining individuals; this does not mean every human, unlike Adam, would corrupt. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p40 One proposed implication of the angels' words is that they claim a stronger title to stewardship: their constant obedience contrasts with the expected corruption of the human appointee. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): One proposed implication of the angels' words is that they claim a stronger title to stewardship: their constant obedience contrasts with the expected corruption of the human appointee. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p41 Human virtue fulfills the purpose of cultivating earth, while corruption does not erase that purpose; the whole includes major benefits and displays human differences and divine knowledge, will, and power. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Human virtue fulfills the purpose of cultivating earth, while corruption does not erase that purpose; the whole includes major benefits and displays human differences and divine knowledge, will, and power. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p42 Unlike angels, humans have the nature and capacity to fulfill stewardship, provided they do not corrupt their innate disposition. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Unlike angels, humans have the nature and capacity to fulfill stewardship, provided they do not corrupt their innate disposition. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q32/p43 The angels considered an earth-born human deficient and prone to corruption, and therefore unfit for stewardship; God disclosed his contrary will and its rationale. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Melekler): The angels considered an earth-born human deficient and prone to corruption, and therefore unfit for stewardship; God disclosed his contrary will and its rationale. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Melekler)]
- 2:30/q32/p44 Ibn Sabit says the angels mean people will engage in what is forbidden. — IBNABIHATIM (ابن سابط): Ibn Sabit says the angels mean people will engage in what is forbidden. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن سابط)]
- 2:30/q32/p45 Abū ʿAlī says Satan's misleading corrupts nothing; the author prefers Abū Hāshim's view. — JISHUMI (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī says Satan's misleading corrupts nothing; the author prefers Abū Hāshim's view. [1 notes: JISHUMI(أبو علي)]
- 2:30/q32/p46 Ibn ʿAbbās says the breath entered Adam through his head, and each part it reached became flesh and blood. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the breath entered Adam through his head, and each part it reached became flesh and blood. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q32/p47 Adam’s community was forbidden carrion, blood, and pork. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Adam’s community was forbidden carrion, blood, and pork. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q32/p48 Some angels sent to aid the fighters also shed blood, but do so zealously in defense of God’s religion and law. — BURSEVI (author): Some angels sent to aid the fighters also shed blood, but do so zealously in defense of God’s religion and law. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q32/p49 Al-Ṭabarī argues that if God had already told angels the descendants would corrupt and shed blood, they could not be reproached for repeating that fact or challenged to name what God had hidden. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī argues that if God had already told angels the descendants would corrupt and shed blood, they could not be reproached for repeating that fact or challenged to name what God had hidden. [0 notes: TAB-FULL-]

## 2:30/q33 How is yasfiku read and parsed?  [وَيَسْفِكُ · readings]
- 2:30/q33/p1 Al-Aʿraj is reported to read «ويسفك الدماء» with «الدماء» in the accusative as the answer to the question introduced by wāw. — NAHHAS (الأعرج): Al-Aʿraj is reported to read «ويسفك الدماء» with «الدماء» in the accusative as the answer to the question introduced by wāw. [1 notes: NAHHAS(الأعرج)]
- 2:30/q33/p2 The majority read yasfiku with kasra on the fāʾ. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور): The majority read yasfiku with kasra on the fāʾ. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور)]
- 2:30/q33/p3 Abu Haywa and Ibn Abi Abla read yasfuku with ḍamma on the fāʾ. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو حيوة): Abu Haywa and Ibn Abi Abla read yasfuku with ḍamma on the fāʾ. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو حيوة)]
- 2:30/q33/p4 Ibn Hurmuz reads yasfaka in the accusative with wa al-sarf, coordinating one who combines to corrupt and shed blood; al-Mahdawi parses it as an answer to the question, while Ibn Atiyya prefers the first analysis. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن هرمز): Ibn Hurmuz reads yasfaka in the accusative with wa al-sarf, coordinating one who combines to corrupt and shed blood; al-Mahdawi parses it as an answer to the question, while Ibn Atiyya prefers the first analysis. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن هرمز)]
- 2:30/q33/p5 The majority read yasfiku with kasra on the fāʾ; Ibn Miṣraf and Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla read it with ḍamma, both being Arabic forms. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الجمهور): The majority read yasfiku with kasra on the fāʾ; Ibn Miṣraf and Ibrāhīm ibn Abī ʿAbla read it with ḍamma, both being Arabic forms. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الجمهور)]
- 2:30/q33/p6 Ṭalḥa and Ibn Miqsam read yusaffik with a doubled fāʾ, conveying repeated or intensified action. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (طلحة وابن مقسم): Ṭalḥa and Ibn Miqsam read yusaffik with a doubled fāʾ, conveying repeated or intensified action. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(طلحة وابن مقسم)]
- 2:30/q33/p7 Yasfiku coordinates with yufsidu and can follow either agreement pattern. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Yasfiku coordinates with yufsidu and can follow either agreement pattern. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q33/p8 Asid transmits al-Aʿraj's reading yasfaka with accusative, treating it as a response to the question with waw; a line of poetry illustrates the construction. — QURTUBI-FULL (أسيد عن الأعرج): Asid transmits al-Aʿraj's reading yasfaka with accusative, treating it as a response to the question with waw; a line of poetry illustrates the construction. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أسيد عن الأعرج)]
- 2:30/q33/p9 An unnamed reading makes يسفك passive; the omitted agent referring to من, whether relative or adjective, is understood as ‘blood is shed among them.’ — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): An unnamed reading makes يسفك passive; the omitted agent referring to من, whether relative or adjective, is understood as ‘blood is shed among them.’ [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q33/p10 يسفك means to pour or shed blood. — NASAFI-FULL (author): يسفك means to pour or shed blood. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q33/p11 The majority read yasfiku with kasra on fāʾ and a nominative kāf; other transmitted readings use ḍamma, the forms from asfaka or doubled safaka, or an accusative kāf from Ibn Hurmuz. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الجمهور): The majority read yasfiku with kasra on fāʾ and a nominative kāf; other transmitted readings use ḍamma, the forms from asfaka or doubled safaka, or an accusative kāf from Ibn Hurmuz. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الجمهور)]
- 2:30/q33/p12 The second فيها is governed by يفسد, and يسفك coordinates with يفسد under either parsing. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The second فيها is governed by يفسد, and يسفك coordinates with يفسد under either parsing. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q33/p13 The majority read يسفك in the indicative; another reading makes it subjunctive as an answer to the question with أن understood after wāw. — SAMIN-DURR (الجمهور): The majority read يسفك in the indicative; another reading makes it subjunctive as an answer to the question with أن understood after wāw. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(الجمهور)]
- 2:30/q33/p14 Readings vary between kasra and ḍamma on fāʾ, a ḍamma on the imperfect prefix from أسفك, and a doubled form for intensity. — SAMIN-DURR (author): Readings vary between kasra and ḍamma on fāʾ, a ḍamma on the imperfect prefix from أسفك, and a doubled form for intensity. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q33/p15 Kufan grammarians posit a waw of ṣarf that shifts the verb to subjunctive; the cited instance is the accusative reading of ويسفك. — BURHAN (الكوفيون): Kufan grammarians posit a waw of ṣarf that shifts the verb to subjunctive; the cited instance is the accusative reading of ويسفك. [1 notes: BURHAN(الكوفيون)]
- 2:30/q33/p16 Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads the verb with ḍamma on the fāʾ and also reports forms from asfaka and the doubled form of safaka. — ALUSI-FULL (إبن أبي عبلة): Ibn Abī ʿAbla reads the verb with ḍamma on the fāʾ and also reports forms from asfaka and the doubled form of safaka. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(إبن أبي عبلة)]
- 2:30/q33/p17 Ibn Hurmuz reads the final kāf with fatḥa, parsed as a subjunctive answering the interrogative; a passive reading is also transmitted, with an understood “among them” referring back to man. — ALUSI-FULL (إبن هرمز): Ibn Hurmuz reads the final kāf with fatḥa, parsed as a subjunctive answering the interrogative; a passive reading is also transmitted, with an understood “among them” referring back to man. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(إبن هرمز)]

## 2:30/q34 What does safk mean?  [وَيَسْفِكُ · meaning]
- 2:30/q34/p1 Safaka means to pour or spill something. — ZAJJAJ (author): Safaka means to pour or spill something. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q34/p2 Safk means pouring out blood specifically, unlike water and other liquids; safḥ is similar but also applies to any liquid wasted. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): Safk means pouring out blood specifically, unlike water and other liquids; safḥ is similar but also applies to any liquid wasted. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p3 Safk ordinarily means spilling blood, but can also describe speaking at length. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Safk ordinarily means spilling blood, but can also describe speaking at length. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p4 Safk means to pour or spill blood; it is specific to blood, whereas ṣabb, safḥ, and irāqa can describe blood or other things. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Safk means to pour or spill blood; it is specific to blood, whereas ṣabb, safḥ, and irāqa can describe blood or other things. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 2:30/q34/p5 Safk means pouring or spilling; Ibn Faris and al-Jawhari say it can describe pouring tears as well as blood, while safak means a slanderer or one capable of speech. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Safk means pouring or spilling; Ibn Faris and al-Jawhari say it can describe pouring tears as well as blood, while safak means a slanderer or one capable of speech. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p6 Al-Mahdawi says safk is used only for blood, though it may also mean scattering speech, as in the expression safaka al-kalam. — QURTUBI-FULL (المهدوي): Al-Mahdawi says safk is used only for blood, though it may also mean scattering speech, as in the expression safaka al-kalam. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(المهدوي)]
- 2:30/q34/p7 The related verbs all mean pouring: سفك is used of blood and tears, سبك of molten jewels, سفح for pouring from above, and شن (also سن) for pouring from a waterskin’s mouth. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The related verbs all mean pouring: سفك is used of blood and tears, سبك of molten jewels, سفح for pouring from above, and شن (also سن) for pouring from a waterskin’s mouth. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p8 Safk means pouring or spilling and is used only of blood; safaka, safika, and asfaka share the same meaning, with two present-tense patterns. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Safk means pouring or spilling and is used only of blood; safaka, safika, and asfaka share the same meaning, with two present-tense patterns. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p9 Safk means pouring or spilling and is used only of blood, or of blood and tears; the blood intended is unlawful by contextual indication, though another view makes the term comprehensive. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Safk means pouring or spilling and is used only of blood, or of blood and tears; the blood intended is unlawful by contextual indication, though another view makes the term comprehensive. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q34/p10 سفك means pouring out blood; grammarians disagree whether دم follows فعل or فعِل. — TABRISI (author): سفك means pouring out blood; grammarians disagree whether دم follows فعل or فعِل. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q34/p11 سفك means pouring and is normally used only of blood; Ibn Fāris and al-Jawharī also allow tears, while al-Mahdawī adds scattered speech. — SAMIN-DURR (author): سفك means pouring and is normally used only of blood; Ibn Fāris and al-Jawharī also allow tears, while al-Mahdawī adds scattered speech. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:30/q35 What is the form and root of damāʾ?  [ٱلدِّمَآءَ · grammar]
- 2:30/q35/p1 The singular of «الدماء» is «دم»; no noun has only two letters without an omitted letter, here a yāʾ retained in poetic usage. — NAHHAS (author): The singular of «الدماء» is «دم»; no noun has only two letters without an omitted letter, here a yāʾ retained in poetic usage. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q35/p2 The singular dam has a deleted final letter, said to be ya; its original form appears in the poetic dual al-damiyan, showing that the noun was not originally only two letters. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The singular dam has a deleted final letter, said to be ya; its original form appears in the poetic dual al-damiyan, showing that the noun was not originally only two letters. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q35/p3 Damāʾ is the plural of dam; its omitted final weak radical may be yāʾ or wāw, as shown by damiyān and damwān. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Damāʾ is the plural of dam; its omitted final weak radical may be yāʾ or wāw, as shown by damiyān and damwān. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q35/p4 The language also attests shortening and consonant doubling for dam, while grammarians differ over the deleted radical's original vowel pattern. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The language also attests shortening and consonant doubling for dam, while grammarians differ over the deleted radical's original vowel pattern. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q35/p5 دماء is the plural of دم; a declinable noun cannot consist of two letters, so the third radical, its final letter, is omitted. — SAMIN-DURR (author): دماء is the plural of دم; a declinable noun cannot consist of two letters, so the third radical, its final letter, is omitted. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q35/p6 The deleted radical may disappear and دم be used as shortened دما, as in the cited poem. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The deleted radical may disappear and دم be used as shortened دما, as in the cited poem. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:30/q36 Why single out bloodshed and repeat “therein”?  [فِيهَا وَيَسْفِكُ ٱلدِّمَآءَ · rhetoric]
- 2:30/q36/p1 They explain corruption as sins and bloodshed as spilling blood without right. — THALABI (author): They explain corruption as sins and bloodshed as spilling blood without right. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q36/p2 Because yufsid is affirmative and does not imply every kind of corruption, the angels name bloodshed as the gravest form, which destroys God's created bodies. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Because yufsid is affirmative and does not imply every kind of corruption, the angels name bloodshed as the gravest form, which destroys God's created bodies. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q36/p3 Repeating fīhā after corruption highlights the reversal of a place of worship into one of wrongdoing; the parallel is 2:11, while omission after bloodshed avoids three repetitions. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Repeating fīhā after corruption highlights the reversal of a place of worship into one of wrongdoing; the parallel is 2:11, while omission after bloodshed avoids three repetitions. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q36/p4 Repeating the locative phrase emphasizes how implausible the angels find the prospect of corruption on earth. — BURSEVI (author): Repeating the locative phrase emphasizes how implausible the angels find the prospect of corruption on earth. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q36/p5 The angels mean unjustly spilling blood, and the wording names bloodshed because it is the ugliest kind of killing. — BURSEVI (author): The angels mean unjustly spilling blood, and the wording names bloodshed because it is the ugliest kind of killing. [1 notes: BURSEVI]

## 2:30/q37 How does “while we” attach syntactically?  [وَنَحْنُ · grammar]
- 2:30/q37/p1 The wāw is circumstantial: ‘Would You appoint one while we are more deserving of favor?’ — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The wāw is circumstantial: ‘Would You appoint one while we are more deserving of favor?’ [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q37/p2 This circumstantial clause explains the question as a friend asking why a trusted stranger is favored: why appoint sinners when we are sinless and more entitled? Its purpose is to ask what favors humans, not to — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): This circumstantial clause explains the question as a friend asking why a trusted stranger is favored: why appoint sinners when we are sinless and more entitled? Its purpose is to ask what favors humans, not to boast. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q37/p3 The waw in ونحن is circumstantial, and بحمدك is also circumstantial: the angels glorify while praising God. — NASAFI-FULL (author): The waw in ونحن is circumstantial, and بحمدك is also circumstantial: the angels glorify while praising God. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q37/p4 The circumstantial clause wa-naḥnu nusabbiḥu has several reported senses: divine transcendence (Qatāda), remembrance aloud (al-Mufaḍḍal), humility (Ibn al-Anbārī), or prayer (Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās). — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The circumstantial clause wa-naḥnu nusabbiḥu has several reported senses: divine transcendence (Qatāda), remembrance aloud (al-Mufaḍḍal), humility (Ibn al-Anbārī), or prayer (Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās). [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q37/p5 نقدس coordinates with نسبح and is a predicate of نحن; its omitted object is ourselves and our actions. — SAMIN-DURR (author): نقدس coordinates with نسبح and is a predicate of نحن; its omitted object is ourselves and our actions. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q37/p6 The wāw is circumstantial; ونحن نسبح is a nominal clause in the accusative as a circumstantial clause. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The wāw is circumstantial; ونحن نسبح is a nominal clause in the accusative as a circumstantial clause. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q37/p7 Ibn ʿAṭiyya accepts placing ونحن نسبح inside an implied question: وأنحن نسبح أم نتغير. — SAMIN-DURR (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya accepts placing ونحن نسبح inside an implied question: وأنحن نسبح أم نتغير. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية)]
- 2:30/q37/p8 The third category is wāw of circumstance, which enters a nominal clause, as in the angels' “while we glorify You.” — ITQAN (author): The third category is wāw of circumstance, which enters a nominal clause, as in the angels' “while we glorify You.” [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q37/p9 It is not established that a nominal clause used as an emphatic circumstantial clause must omit its pronoun and conjunction; al-Alusi cites Sharh al-Tashil. — ALUSI-FULL (author): It is not established that a nominal clause used as an emphatic circumstantial clause must omit its pronoun and conjunction; al-Alusi cites Sharh al-Tashil. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:30/q38 What do the angels imply by contrasting their worship?  [وَنَحْنُ نُسَبِّحُ · interpretation]
- 2:30/q38/p1 Abū ʿAlī says they asked to be placed on earth because it would benefit them. — JISHUMI (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī says they asked to be placed on earth because it would benefit them. [1 notes: JISHUMI(أبو علي)]
- 2:30/q38/p2 They may ask for earth, or part of it, for themselves if beneficial; God chose heaven for angels and earth for people, for their religious good. — RAZI-FULL (author): They may ask for earth, or part of it, for themselves if beneficial; God chose heaven for angels and earth for people, for their religious good. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q38/p3 The wāw in ‘and we glorify’ is circumstantial, like saying, ‘Would you favor someone while I am more deserving of favor?’ — RAZI-FULL (author): The wāw in ‘and we glorify’ is circumstantial, like saying, ‘Would you favor someone while I am more deserving of favor?’ [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q38/p4 The angels’ question expresses their claim to greater entitlement to the caliphate and asks why humans were preferred despite their expected corruption. — BURSEVI (author): The angels’ question expresses their claim to greater entitlement to the caliphate and asks why humans were preferred despite their expected corruption. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q38/p5 The angels offer themselves as God's successors, or ask Him to appoint them, questioning the benefit of an earthly successor. — TABATABAI (author): The angels offer themselves as God's successors, or ask Him to appoint them, questioning the benefit of an earthly successor. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 2:30/q38/p6 Angels inhabit heaven, Adam and descendants earth, including worshippers. — MUQATIL (author): Angels inhabit heaven, Adam and descendants earth, including worshippers. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 2:30/q38/p7 Angels remember God, pray and magnify His command; compare thunder in 13:13. — MUQATIL (author): Angels remember God, pray and magnify His command; compare thunder in 13:13. [1 notes: MUQATIL]
- 2:30/q38/p8 On the prayer interpretation, the angels pray and recite the Opening of the Book in their prayer. — THALABI (author): On the prayer interpretation, the angels pray and recite the Opening of the Book in their prayer. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q38/p9 Without God’s grace through guidance and kindness, the angels could not worship Him. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Without God’s grace through guidance and kindness, the angels could not worship Him. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q38/p10 The angels' praise is compared with the wording about angels praising their Lord in 42:5. — TABRISI (author): The angels' praise is compared with the wording about angels praising their Lord in 42:5. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q38/p11 Despite abundant worship and never slipping, angels remain fearful and apprehensive of God. — RAZI-FULL (author): Despite abundant worship and never slipping, angels remain fearful and apprehensive of God. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q38/p12 They explain the angels’ words as saying that they pray to God. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وغيرهما من الصحابة): They explain the angels’ words as saying that they pray to God. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وغيرهما من الصحابة)]
- 2:30/q38/p13 Alternatively, the angels mean “Glory be to God and praise be to Him.” — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Alternatively, the angels mean “Glory be to God and praise be to Him.” [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]

## 2:30/q39 What does the angels' tasbīḥ mean?  [نُسَبِّحُ · meaning]
turns on: Whether tasbīḥ here is transcendence, spoken glorification, remembrance, or prayer.
- 2:30/q39/p1 Tasbīḥ is glorification and exaltation — ABDURRAZZAQ (author): Qatada glosses tasbih as glorification. TAB-FULL (بعض أهل العربية): Some Arab grammarians say the question was not a protest against God but a request to learn; the angels mentioned their own glorification because they disliked disobedience after the jinn had disobeyed. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada takes tasbih here in its familiar, literal sense of glorification. TAB-FULL (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that when asked which words God loves most, the Prophet named the words God chose for the angels: repeated glorification of the Lord with praise. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari explains that the angels wondered at a creation that would sin and implied that they themselves glorified, prayed to, and obeyed God. SULAMI (الواسطي): Whoever says “I” contests divine power; the angels' boastful claim to glorify God also objected to lordship, despite being a setting for divine instruction. MAWARDI-FULL (المفضل): Al-Mufaḍḍal takes it as the familiar act of tasbīḥ, citing Jarīr’s verse about pilgrims glorifying God. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Though others may be described as pure, tasbīḥ is a religious term for the highest degree of exaltation, due only to God. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid understands it as glorifying God. QUSHAYRI (author): God drew out the angels’ concealed self-admiration in their worship, making them disclose their hidden thoughts by citing their glorification. QUSHAYRI (author): Angelic glorification has no significance without God’s favor, and human sins cause no harm when met by His pardon. QUSHAYRI (author): The angels’ glorification displays their deeds and merit, while forgiveness of human sins displays the Creator’s generosity and mercy. QUSHAYRI (author): God needs no worship; their glorification establishes their own praise, while His forgiveness establishes the Creator’s praise. QUSHAYRI (author): God knows humans’ humbled, broken hearts despite their bad deeds, whereas angels’ hearts become proud when they display glorification. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Another explanation is that the angels wondered whether the successor would be from the jinn, whose predecessors corrupted the earth, or from the angels, who glorify God; God did not reveal that Adam’s descendants would include both obedient and disobedient people. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The word's root means distancing; anyone praising God and distancing Him from evil has glorified and purified Him. WAHIDI-BASIT (بعض أهل المعاني): Some linguists explain tasbih with praise as speaking God's praise: praise and gratitude themselves honor Him and acknowledge His worthiness to be purified and exalted. JISHUMI (author): Another account says glorification benefits the angels, while their ability to perform it comes from God’s enabling. JISHUMI (author): One rejects that they asked to replace people because this implies a divine will without evidence; another rearranges the phrase to mean they glorify by God’s guidance and then praise Him. JISHUMI (قتادة): Qatāda interprets the angels’ tasbīḥ as the familiar act of glorification. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Glorifying God with praise means declaring Him free of what is unfitting to His essence and attributes. IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعض المتأولين): Some read “while we glorify You” as a question about whether angels should continue in that state or change from it; Ibn Atiyya says this suits a strictly interrogative reading. IBNATIYYA-FULL (قوم): Some understand the angels to mean that if God had appointed them to succession on earth, they would glorify Him; this suits the astonishment reading. TABRISI (مجاهد): Mujahid says praising God is tasbih because praise exalts Him and recognizes He deserves glorification. RAZI-FULL (author): Angels are honored, do not disdain worship, and glorify God day and night without tiring. RAZI-FULL (علي): Ali describes angels in varied worship postures, glorifying tirelessly without sleep, lapse, bodily fatigue, or forgetfulness. RAZI-FULL (المخالف): An opponent says the angels' self-praise and claim of exclusive glorification resemble conceit and backbiting. RAZI-FULL (author): Uninterrupted glorification day and night is incompatible with committing sin. RAZI-FULL (author): One explanation is that glorifying God with praise recognizes that his praise and majesty make him deserving of glorification. RAZI-FULL (author): A second explanation is that people can glorify God only through his enabling grace, as David’s example illustrates. RAZI-FULL (author): Their self-praise may itself amount to glorifying themselves; later humans will instead humble themselves by confessing wrong and asking mercy. RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): The Muʿtazilites argue that the angels attribute their glorification and purification to themselves; if God did those deeds, there would be no merit in them over bloodshed. QURTUBI-FULL (المفضل): Al-Mufaddal says the angels' tasbih is raising the voice in remembrance, supported by Jarir's line about pilgrims glorifying and proclaiming God's greatness. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): We purify our souls from sins for Your sake; the angels set glorification against the corruption (understood by some as idolatry) and purification against the grave vice of bloodshed. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another proposed complement contrasts the question with the angels' state: will You change us, though we glorify You? ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): In bi-ḥamdika, the bāʾ can mark accompaniment: the angels glorify while joined by praise, a circumstantial phrase within another circumstantial phrase. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (صفي الدين أبو عبد الله الحسين ابن الوزير أبي الحسن علي بن أبي المنصور الخزرجي): The author of Fakk al-Azrar assigns the apparent objection to Iblis and the glorification and obedience to the angels, who had first spoken in a collective answer. DURR-FULL (أبو صالح): Abu Salih glosses the angels' words as magnifying and glorifying God. ALUSI-FULL (أهل الله): Their cosmology next describes absolute darkness receiving light, the Throne appearing with the Merciful's manifest Name, and surrounding angels whose sole occupation is glorification. ALUSI-FULL (author): The first group are the exalted and proximate angels, constantly glorifying God without fatigue. ALUSI-FULL (صفي الدين الخزرجي): Al-Khazraji says the speakers differ: Iblis, included among the angels, voiced the objection, while the other angels voiced glorification after his refusal separated him from them; al-Alusi calls this an interpretive gloss, not tafsir. ALUSI-FULL (author): Putting the subject before the verbal predicate conveys restriction: the angels, in contrast to the proposed vicegerent, glorify God. ALUSI-FULL (author): If “Your praise” is construed as an objective genitive, the angels mean that they praise God because He enabled them to glorify Him. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Another class surrounds the Throne and glorifies God. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He rejects reading the angels’ glorification as a question with an omitted interrogative and an omitted alternative, since the syntax gives no evidence for either deletion. [44 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(أبو ذر,بعض أهل العربية,قتادة); SULAMI(الواسطي); MAWARDI-FULL(المفضل,مجاهد); QUSHAYRI; WAHIDI-BASIT(بعض أهل المعاني); JISHUMI(قتادة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعض المتأولين,قوم); TABRISI(مجاهد); RAZI-FULL(المخالف,المعتزلة,علي); QURTUBI-FULL(المفضل); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(صفي الدين أبو عبد الله الحسين ابن الوزير أبي الحسن علي بن أبي المنصور الخزرجي); DURR-FULL(أبو صالح); ALUSI-FULL(أهل الله,صفي الدين الخزرجي); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:30/q39/p2 Tasbīḥ denotes prayer — AKHFASH (author): Remembrance and prayer can be tasbih; glorification is accompanied by praise, with 40:7 and 110:3. TAB-FULL (author): In Arab usage, remembrance and prayer can be called tasbih; the report also records the angels’ repeated phrase of glorification with praise. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari reconciles prayer and glorification with purification: prayer magnifies God and cleanses him of the false attributions made by disbelievers. ZAJJAJ (author): Any act intended for God may be called tasbīḥ; completing prayer is one Arabic use of tasbīḥ. THALABI (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the angels utter “Glory and praise belong to God”; this is the creatures’ prayer and glorification, through which they receive provision. THALABI (author): Another interpretation takes tasbih as prayer commanded by God; prayer is called subha. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس وابن مسعود): Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd interpret the angels’ tasbīḥ as prayer. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Tasbih means declaring God free of every evil, and it can also mean prayer; to glorify God is to pray to Him. JISHUMI (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās interprets the angels’ tasbīḥ as prayer. BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): Another explanation is that the angels mean they pray in accordance with God's command; Ibn Abbas says tasbih throughout the Quran means prayer. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas and Ibn Masud say the angels’ glorification is their prayer to God. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas and Ibn Masʿud interpret ‘we glorify’ as ‘we pray,’ taking tasbih to mean prayer. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud and Ibn ʿAbbas interpret the angels' tasbih as prayer, citing the description of Jonah as one of the tasbih-makers in 37:143. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Tasbīḥ is speech, or speech with action, that magnifies and declares God free of deficiency; hence remembrance and prayer can be called tasbīḥ. [14 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; THALABI(الحسن); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس وابن مسعود); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(ابن عباس); BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن مسعود); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q39/p3 Tasbīḥ declares God free of imperfection — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari glosses tasbih with praise as magnifying God through praise and gratitude; he also defines its root sense as declaring God free of attributes unworthy of him. ZAJJAJ (author): To glorify God with praise means to declare Him free of evil. ZAJJAJ (سيبويه وغيره من النحويين): Sībawayh and other grammarians gloss subḥān Allāh as declaring God free of evil and exalting Him above it. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Tasbīḥ is declaring God free of evil in a mode of exaltation; al-Aʿshā’s line uses subḥān as disavowal. WAHIDI-BASIT (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says exegetes and linguists agree that tasbih means declaring God transcendent and free of evil. RAZI-FULL (أهل التذكير): Ahl al-tadhkir say tasbih in the Qur’an can mean transcendence: God has no peer, administers the heavens and worlds, is above wrong descriptions, self-sufficient and sovereign, all-knowing, and without consort or child. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The angels' tasbih means declaring God exalted above what does not suit His attributes; in Arabic it is magnifying Him by declaring Him free of evil, as al-Aʿsha's line uses subhan for disavowal. SAMIN-DURR (author): تسبيح means declaring transcendence and disavowal; its root سبْح denotes distance, as in swimming. SAMIN-DURR (author): سبحان الله declares God transcendent and free of what does not befit Him; the poem uses سبحان من علقمة for تنزيها. [9 notes: TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ(سيبويه وغيره من النحويين); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(الأزهري); RAZI-FULL(أهل التذكير); QURTUBI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q39/p4 Al-Tabari says tasbih fundamentally declares God free of attributes that do not belong to him; al-Asha’s verse means, reproachfully, ‘Glory be to God against alqama’s boasting.’ — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari says tasbih fundamentally declares God free of attributes that do not belong to him; al-Asha’s verse means, reproachfully, ‘Glory be to God against alqama’s boasting.’ [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q39/p5 Al-Aʿshā’s line uses the word سبحان to mean disavowal of ʿAlqama and his boasting. — ZAJJAJ (author): Al-Aʿshā’s line uses the word سبحان to mean disavowal of ʿAlqama and his boasting. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q39/p6 Al-Hasan explains the angels' phrase as saying, Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الحسن): Al-Hasan explains the angels' phrase as saying, Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الحسن)]
- 2:30/q39/p7 Subhan Allah means God's freedom from evil and became an exclamation of wonder; al-A'sha's verse uses it to express astonishment. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Subhan Allah means God's freedom from evil and became an exclamation of wonder; al-A'sha's verse uses it to express astonishment. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:30/q39/p8 Tasbīḥ and taqdīs mean declaring God far removed from evil; their roots also convey going far away. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Tasbīḥ and taqdīs mean declaring God far removed from evil; their roots also convey going far away. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q39/p9 Qatada takes tasbih in its ordinary linguistic sense: saying “subhan Allah.” — IBNATIYYA-FULL (قتادة): Qatada takes tasbih in its ordinary linguistic sense: saying “subhan Allah.” [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q39/p10 Muqātil interprets their tasbīḥ as saying ‘subḥāna Allāh.’ — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مقتادة): Muqātil interprets their tasbīḥ as saying ‘subḥāna Allāh.’ [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مقتادة)]
- 2:30/q39/p11 Al-Rāzī says the preposition bi in the angels' tasbīḥ requires grammatical analysis to determine what it attaches to. — RAZI-FULL (author): Al-Rāzī says the preposition bi in the angels' tasbīḥ requires grammatical analysis to determine what it attaches to. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q39/p12 God needs no creatures’ tasbih; — RAZI-FULL (author): God needs no creatures’ tasbih; because nonliving things do not need its reward, God gives their reward to the human who serves him, showing that the whole world can be put at the servant’s service. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q39/p13 They urge tasbih for divine favor and relief, including seeking God’s pleasure and praising him morning and evening. — RAZI-FULL (أهل التذكير): They urge tasbih for divine favor and relief, including seeking God’s pleasure and praising him morning and evening. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(أهل التذكير)]
- 2:30/q39/p14 Tasbih and taqdis mean distancing God from what is evil or unfitting. — RAZI-FULL (author): Tasbih and taqdis mean distancing God from what is evil or unfitting. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]

## 2:30/q40 What formulas and practices of angelic praise are reported?  [نُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِكَ · reports]
- 2:30/q40/p1 Al-Hasan says the angels say Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi; this is the prayer of creation, beasts, and other creatures besides humans, by which they receive provision. — BAGHAWI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the angels say Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi; this is the prayer of creation, beasts, and other creatures besides humans, by which they receive provision. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(الحسن)]
- 2:30/q40/p2 Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet named Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi as the best speech chosen by God for His angels or servants; Baghawi gives no grading. — BAGHAWI-FULL (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet named Subhan Allah wa bihamdihi as the best speech chosen by God for His angels or servants; Baghawi gives no grading. - 2:30/q40/p3 ʿAbd al-Rahman b. Qurt reports that on the night of the Night Journey the Prophet heard glorification in the highest heavens: subhan al-ʿAli al-Aʿla; al-Bayhaqi reports it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن قرط)]

## 2:30/q41 How does “with Your praise” work and what does it mean?  [بِحَمْدِكَ · grammar]
- 2:30/q41/p1 بحمدك is circumstantial: they glorify God while praising Him and joined to His praise. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): بحمدك is circumstantial: they glorify God while praising Him and joined to His praise. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p2 Bi-hamdika can mean joining praise to glorification; alternatively, it is an interjection: “we glorify and sanctify,” then a submission that God is praised for guiding them to this. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Bi-hamdika can mean joining praise to glorification; alternatively, it is an interjection: “we glorify and sanctify,” then a submission that God is praised for guiding them to this. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p3 Al-Kashshaf treats ‘with your praise’ as a circumstantial phrase: we glorify you while praising you and clothed in your praise. — RAZI-FULL (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf treats ‘with your praise’ as a circumstantial phrase: we glorify you while praising you and clothed in your praise. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(صاحب الكشاف)]
- 2:30/q41/p4 Bi-hamdika may mean that the angels join praise to tasbih; al-hamd means praise. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Bi-hamdika may mean that the angels join praise to tasbih; al-hamd means praise. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p5 Alternatively, bi-hamdika is a parenthetical acknowledgment: the angels say they glorify and sanctify, while affirming that God is praised for guiding them to this worship. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Alternatively, bi-hamdika is a parenthetical acknowledgment: the angels say they glorify and sanctify, while affirming that God is praised for guiding them to this worship. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p6 Qatada takes tasbih in its ordinary linguistic sense, saying subhan Allah; al-Qurtubi calls this correct and cites the Prophet's report that subhan Allah wa bi-hamdih is among God's chosen words. — QURTUBI-FULL (قتادة): Qatada takes tasbih in its ordinary linguistic sense, saying subhan Allah; al-Qurtubi calls this correct and cites the Prophet's report that subhan Allah wa bi-hamdih is among God's chosen words. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قتادة)+]
- 2:30/q41/p7 Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet was asked which speech is best and replied that it is what God chose for His angels or servants: subhan Allah wa bi-hamdih; Muslim transmits it. — QURTUBI-FULL (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports that the Prophet was asked which speech is best and replied that it is what God chose for His angels or servants: subhan Allah wa bi-hamdih; Muslim transmits it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو ذر)]
- 2:30/q41/p8 بحمدك is circumstantial: ‘while praising You for teaching us to know You and enabling our glorification’; this qualification prevents the angels’ self-attribution of praise from implying conceit. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): بحمدك is circumstantial: ‘while praising You for teaching us to know You and enabling our glorification’; this qualification prevents the angels’ self-attribution of praise from implying conceit. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p9 Alternatively, bāʾ marks cause: praise arises from enabling and beneficence, so the angels glorify because God grants them good; ḥamd is annexed to its object. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Alternatively, bāʾ marks cause: praise arises from enabling and beneficence, so the angels glorify because God grants them good; ḥamd is annexed to its object. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p10 الحمد is a verbal noun annexed to its object, with subject omitted as بحمدنا إياك; the claim that the noun itself contains a subject is mistaken. — SAMIN-DURR (author): الحمد is a verbal noun annexed to its object, with subject omitted as بحمدنا إياك; the claim that the noun itself contains a subject is mistaken. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q41/p11 بحمدك attaches to an omitted circumstantial phrase: they glorify accompanied by praise, two nested circumstantial states. — SAMIN-DURR (author): بحمدك attaches to an omitted circumstantial phrase: they glorify accompanied by praise, two nested circumstantial states. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:30/q41/p12 The phrase “with Your praise” is circumstantial; the bāʾ denotes continued companionship and accompaniment. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The phrase “with Your praise” is circumstantial; the bāʾ denotes continued companionship and accompaniment. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p13 The bāʾ marks accompaniment: the angels glorify God with praise, since their utterance includes praise and exaltation, avoiding strained analyses of the phrase. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The bāʾ marks accompaniment: the angels glorify God with praise, since their utterance includes praise and exaltation, avoiding strained analyses of the phrase. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q41/p14 An alternate rendering is We glorify Thee while praising Thee. — STUDYQURAN (Z): An alternate rendering is We glorify Thee while praising Thee. [1 notes: STUDYQURAN(Z)]

## 2:30/q42 What does the angels' taqdīs mean?  [وَنُقَدِّسُ · meaning]
turns on: Whether taqdīs purifies God in speech or the angels and their deeds.
- 2:30/q42/p1 The angels ascribe purity and holiness to God — MUQATIL (author): God prayed mercy upon Adam's first praise and said he was created for glorification and sanctification. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari defines taqdis as purification and magnification: subbuh denotes declaring God free of defects, quddus his purity and exaltation, and holy land is purified land. TAB-FULL (أبو صالح): Abu Salih interprets taqdis as magnifying and glorifying God. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says taqdis means to magnify and exalt God. SULAMI (الواسطي): After the angels boasted of praise and sanctification, God taught Adam the names, making them recognize their own shortfall and confess ignorance. SULAMI (أبو عثمان المغربي): The angels' claim to praise and sanctify God exemplifies how creaturely claims lead to ignorance. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Naqdis means to purify and declare God free of any unbefitting deficiency; taqdis and quds mean purification, and bayt al-maqdis is the purified house. JISHUMI (author): Another account treats the lām as extra and the sanctification as directed to God Himself. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid, Abū Ṣāliḥ, and others explain “we sanctify You” as magnifying God and declaring His remembrance free of anything unworthy. TABRISI (سيبويه): سبحان means God's release from every evil and His sanctification; Sibawayh treats it as a fixed proper name, and poets use tanwin by necessity. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid, Abu Salih, and others say taqdis means magnifying God and purifying His mention of what heretics attribute to Him. QURTUBI-FULL (أرباب المعاني): They say God addressed angels not for counsel but to elicit their view of action, worship, praise, and sanctification, then restored their rank by ordering prostration to Adam. SAMIN-DURR (author): Al-Samīn takes Ibn ʿAṭiyya to aim at a causal bāʾ with an omitted verb: praise of God occasioned their glorification and sanctification. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir defines taqdis as glorification and purification: subbuh denotes declaring God free of defect and quddus denotes His purity and exaltedness; the angels disavow what polytheists attribute to Him and affirm His purity from impure qualities. BURSEVI (author): The lām in “for You” may explain the act, as in a supplication formula, or be an added particle; the implied object is sanctifying God. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Taqdīs may also be an act of belief; the hadith lā quddisat umma means God has not purified a community from satanic impurities when its weak cannot claim rights. TABATABAI (author): God does not deny that the earthly successor will cause corruption and bloodshed, nor does He dispute the angels' own glorification and sanctification. [17 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(أبو صالح,مجاهد); SULAMI(أبو عثمان المغربي,الواسطي); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA-FULL(مجاهد); TABRISI(سيبويه); QURTUBI-FULL(أرباب المعاني,مجاهد); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير); BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:30/q42/p2 The angels purify themselves or their deeds for God — SULAMI (الواسطي): Glorification and sanctification do not themselves bring one near to God; nearness comes through pre-eternal grace, which sins cannot diminish. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو إسحاق): Abu Ishaq reads the lām as purposive: the angels purify themselves for God, cleansing their hearts of idolatry and bodies of disobedience; bayt al-maqdis is the purified house or place where one is purified of sins. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abū Muslim takes the angels’ sanctification as purifying themselves from sins for God’s sake and pleasure. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak and others explain “we sanctify for You” as purifying themselves to seek God’s approval; sanctification means purification. QURTUBI-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak and others interpret taqdis as purifying themselves for God in pursuit of His pleasure. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk and others interpret taqdīs as purifying themselves for God from impurities; Abū Muslim instead makes its object their deeds, purified from sins. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قول غير مسمى): One proposed explanation is that God knew the angels were magnifying themselves through their glorification and sanctification. IBNKATHIR-FULL (قيل): Another explanation takes God's reply as pointing to Iblis's presence among the angels, despite their description of themselves as glorifying and sanctifying God. ALUSI-FULL (author): Taqdis may also mean purification: the angels purify themselves from impurities, their acts from sins, or their hearts from attending to anything besides God. [9 notes: SULAMI(الواسطي); WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو إسحاق); JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الضحاك); QURTUBI-FULL(الضحاك); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الضحاك,قول غير مسمى); IBNKATHIR-FULL(قيل); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p3 Taqdīs means prayer — ABDURRAZZAQ (author): Qatada glosses taqdis as prayer. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the angels’ taqdis means prayer. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مجاهد وأبو صالح): Mujāhid and Abū Ṣāliḥ understand taqdīs as magnifying and glorifying God; other reports interpret it as prayer or purification from human deeds. [3 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(قتادة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مجاهد وأبو صالح)]
- 2:30/q42/p4 Angels purify their deeds for God — SULAMI (جعفر): When the angels boasted of their deeds, glorification, and sanctification, God struck them with ignorance until they confessed they had no knowledge. THALABI (بعض العلماء): Some scholars reorder the sense as “we glorify and sanctify You by Your praise”: otherwise the angels seem to boast of their deeds, whereas this credits every act of worship to God. [2 notes: SULAMI(جعفر); THALABI(بعض العلماء)]
- 2:30/q42/p5 Quddus follows fa'ul from quds, purity. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Quddus follows fa'ul from quds, purity. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB]
- 2:30/q42/p6 Hazirat al-Quds is Paradise, free of excrement, urine and menstruation. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Hazirat al-Quds is Paradise, free of excrement, urine and menstruation. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB(أهل النظر)]
- 2:30/q42/p7 Al-Dahhak glosses taqdis as purification. — TAB-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak glosses taqdis as purification. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 2:30/q42/p8 One interpretation of the angels’ taqdīs is that they pray. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): One interpretation of the angels’ taqdīs is that they pray. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p9 Another analysis treats the lām as expressing purpose: we sanctify for Your sake and pleasure. — JISHUMI (author): Another analysis treats the lām as expressing purpose: we sanctify for Your sake and pleasure. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q42/p10 The root means purification, as in “the holy land,” Bayt al-Maqdis, and al-Quds, the thing used to purify. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The root means purification, as in “the holy land,” Bayt al-Maqdis, and al-Quds, the thing used to purify. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p11 تقديس means purification, opposed to defilement; القدس can also mean a vessel for washing. — TABRISI (author): تقديس means purification, opposed to defilement; القدس can also mean a vessel for washing. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q42/p12 Quds means purity. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Quds means purity. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 2:30/q42/p13 Taqdis means purification, as in the expression ‘the Holy Land.’ — RAZI-FULL (author): Taqdis means purification, as in the expression ‘the Holy Land.’ [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p14 ʿAisha reports that the Prophet said in bowing and prostration, subbuh quddus, Lord of the angels and the Spirit; Muslim transmits it. — QURTUBI-FULL (عائشة): ʿAisha reports that the Prophet said in bowing and prostration, subbuh quddus, Lord of the angels and the Spirit; Muslim transmits it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 2:30/q42/p15 Across its forms, quds means purification: the Holy Land is purified, al-Malik al-Quddus is the Pure, and the sacred valley is purified; Bayt al-Maqdis names a place of purification from sins. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Across its forms, quds means purification: the Holy Land is purified, al-Malik al-Quddus is the Pure, and the sacred valley is purified; Bayt al-Maqdis names a place of purification from sins. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p16 Glorification denies partner, incapacity, and defect; sanctification also denies attachment, passivity, contingency, multiplicity, and potentiality, so every sanctified being glorifies but not vice versa. — QASHANI (author): Glorification denies partner, incapacity, and defect; sanctification also denies attachment, passivity, contingency, multiplicity, and potentiality, so every sanctified being glorifies but not vice versa. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q42/p17 The nearest angels, pure spirits, are sanctifiers because they are un-veiled, overcome lower realms with light, act on others, and possess all perfections actually; other angels glorify. — QASHANI (author): The nearest angels, pure spirits, are sanctifiers because they are un-veiled, overcome lower realms with light, act on others, and possess all perfections actually; other angels glorify. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q42/p18 Angels purify their hearts for God — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another spiritual interpretation takes taqdīs as cleansing hearts from attention to anything besides God. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p19 تقديس means purification, as in الأرض المقدسة, بيت المقدس, and روح القدس. — SAMIN-DURR (author): تقديس means purification, as in الأرض المقدسة, بيت المقدس, and روح القدس. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q42/p20 Al-Dahhak explains taqdis as purification. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak explains taqdis as purification. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 2:30/q42/p21 The angels' wording pairs corruption with glorification and bloodshed with sanctification: glorification supports reform, while sanctification protects blood; the latter is nobler. — BURHAN (author): The angels' wording pairs corruption with glorification and bloodshed with sanctification: glorification supports reform, while sanctification protects blood; the latter is nobler. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q42/p22 Ibn Masud and some Companions gloss the angels' “we sanctify You” as “we pray to You.” — DURR-FULL (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud and some Companions gloss the angels' “we sanctify You” as “we pray to You.” [1 notes: DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 2:30/q42/p23 Ibn Abbas says taqdis means purification. — DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says taqdis means purification. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q42/p24 Angels vary in form and size; only people with sanctified souls see them in their true forms, though they may appear in bodies visible to all. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Angels vary in form and size; only people with sanctified souls see them in their true forms, though they may appear in bodies visible to all. - 2:30/q42/p25 Taqdīs means declaring free of deficiency and purifying; it can be enacted in practice, as the word muqaddas in Imruʾ al-Qays's verse suggests. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Taqdīs means declaring free of deficiency and purifying; it can be enacted in practice, as the word muqaddas in Imruʾ al-Qays's verse suggests. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

- 2:30/q42/p27 They declare God transcendent above whatever is unbefitting Him. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): They declare God transcendent above whatever is unbefitting Him. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 2:30/q42/p28 One interpretation is that the angels declare God transcendent above bodily attributes and purify Him of reprehensible acts. — JISHUMI (author): One interpretation is that the angels declare God transcendent above bodily attributes and purify Him of reprehensible acts. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q42/p29 Qaddas means to praise God as holy and pure; other explanations are to purify themselves for obedience or to declare Him transcendent. — BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Qaddas means to praise God as holy and pure; other explanations are to purify themselves for obedience or to declare Him transcendent. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q42/p30 Some interpret “we sanctify You” as “we pray to You”; Ibn ʿAṭiyya judges this weak. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (قوم): Some interpret “we sanctify You” as “we pray to You”; Ibn ʿAṭiyya judges this weak. [0 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(قوم)-]

## 2:30/q43 What is the force of “for You” after nuqaddisu?  [لَكَ · grammar]
- 2:30/q43/p1 Nuqaddisuka and nuqaddisu laka have the same meaning, like forms of nusabbih. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Nuqaddisuka and nuqaddisu laka have the same meaning, like forms of nusabbih. [1 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB]
- 2:30/q43/p2 Al-Tabari says ونقدسك could replace ونقدس لك with no change in meaning; both transitive constructions are idiomatic, as Qur’anic usage also shows. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari says ونقدسك could replace ونقدس لك with no change in meaning; both transitive constructions are idiomatic, as Qur’anic usage also shows. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p3 Nuqaddisu laka means purifying ourselves for God; the obedient person is purified, and Bayt al-Maqdis is the purified house or place of purification from sins. — ZAJJAJ (author): Nuqaddisu laka means purifying ourselves for God; the obedient person is purified, and Bayt al-Maqdis is the purified house or place of purification from sins. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q43/p4 When a consonant precedes the suffix yāʾ, speakers agree on fatḥa in examples such as بشراي and غلاماي, supporting that as its underlying vowel after a vowel too. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): When a consonant precedes the suffix yāʾ, speakers agree on fatḥa in examples such as بشراي and غلاماي, supporting that as its underlying vowel after a vowel too. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q43/p5 Nuqaddisu means declaring God transcendent; on one analysis the lām is merely connective. — THALABI (author): Nuqaddisu means declaring God transcendent; on one analysis the lām is merely connective. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q43/p6 The Hudhaylī line using ألكني is cited as evidence for the sense “send me.” — MAWARDI-FULL (author): The Hudhaylī line using ألكني is cited as evidence for the sense “send me.” [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p7 Abu Ali al-Farisi says the angels declare God free of evil and refuse to attribute evil to Him; the lām is not purposive but functions like that in 27:72. He also analyzes al-muqaddas as either a — WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو علي الفارسي): Abu Ali al-Farisi says the angels declare God free of evil and refuse to attribute evil to Him; the lām is not purposive but functions like that in 27:72. He also analyzes al-muqaddas as either a verbal noun or a place, with bayt al-maqdis a place of purification cleansed of idols, as in 2:125. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو علي الفارسي)]
- 2:30/q43/p8 One analysis treats the lām as extra and reads the phrase as nuqaddisuka, we sanctify You. — JISHUMI (author): One analysis treats the lām as extra and reads the phrase as nuqaddisuka, we sanctify You. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q43/p9 The lām is an added connective particle. — BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The lām is an added connective particle. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p10 One explanation is declaring God free of deficient attributes and unworthy things; on this reading the lām is extra. — TABRISI (author): One explanation is declaring God free of deficient attributes and unworthy things; on this reading the lām is extra. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q43/p11 Some Arabs keep the hamza, but most shift its vowel to the lām and drop it, saying ملك. — TABRISI (author): Some Arabs keep the hamza, but most shift its vowel to the lām and drop it, saying ملك. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q43/p12 Some hold that the lām in لك is extra. — BAYDAWI-FULL (قيل): Some hold that the lām in لك is extra. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q43/p13 نقدس لك means purifying ourselves for God; alternatively tasbih and taqdis mean declaring God free of evil, with a root sense of going away and distancing. — NASAFI-FULL (author): نقدس لك means purifying ourselves for God; alternatively tasbih and taqdis mean declaring God free of evil, with a root sense of going away and distancing. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p14 The lām in laka may be extra, purposive, attached to nuqaddisu or nusabbiḥu, transitive, or explanatory with an omitted taqdīsuna laka; Abū Ḥayyān prefers the transitive function. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The lām in laka may be extra, purposive, attached to nuqaddisu or nusabbiḥu, transitive, or explanatory with an omitted taqdīsuna laka; Abū Ḥayyān prefers the transitive function. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p15 Wa-nuqaddisu laka reinforces wa-nusabbiḥu because purification and declaring God free of evil are close in meaning. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Wa-nuqaddisu laka reinforces wa-nusabbiḥu because purification and declaring God free of evil are close in meaning. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p16 لك may attach to نقدس or نسبح with a causal sense; it may also be redundant (a weak view), transitive, or explanatory as in سقيا لك. — SAMIN-DURR (author): لك may attach to نقدس or نسبح with a causal sense; it may also be redundant (a weak view), transitive, or explanatory as in سقيا لك. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q43/p17 The estimate تقديسنا لك suits the context better than estimating أعني. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The estimate تقديسنا لك suits the context better than estimating أعني. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:30/q43/p18 Although qaddasa is transitive directly, the lām with its object intensifies the action, as in shakarta laka and naṣaḥtu laka; Arabic usage treats this as eloquent emphasis. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Although qaddasa is transitive directly, the lām with its object intensifies the action, as in shakarta laka and naṣaḥtu laka; Arabic usage treats this as eloquent emphasis. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q43/p19 Alternatively, the lām marks purpose: the angels purify their hearts of associating partners with God and their bodies of disobedience for His sake. — THALABI (author): Alternatively, the lām marks purpose: the angels purify their hearts of associating partners with God and their bodies of disobedience for His sake. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q43/p20 The author rejects analyses that attach laka to an omitted word or make it mean for your sake with an omitted object, calling these departures from eloquent usage; an explanatory lām remains possible. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The author rejects analyses that attach laka to an omitted word or make it mean for your sake with an omitted object, calling these departures from eloquent usage; an explanatory lām remains possible. [0 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL-]

## 2:30/q44 How are tasbīḥ and taqdīs related?  [نُسَبِّحُ وَنُقَدِّسُ · interpretation]
- 2:30/q44/p1 The angels’ tasbih clears God of what polytheists attribute to him and includes prayer; taqdis ascribes purity to him, free from what disbelievers allege. — TAB-FULL (author): The angels’ tasbih clears God of what polytheists attribute to him and includes prayer; taqdis ascribes purity to him, free from what disbelievers allege. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q44/p2 Tasbih is declaring God free of evil and unworthy attributes; السبوح deserves glorification and القدوس purification and sanctification. — TABRISI (author): Tasbih is declaring God free of evil and unworthy attributes; السبوح deserves glorification and القدوس purification and sanctification. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q44/p3 A group including Qatada says taqdis means prayer. Ibn ʿAtiyya calls this weak, but al-Qurtubi says it is sound because prayer includes magnification, sanctification, and tasbih. — QURTUBI-FULL (قتادة): A group including Qatada says taqdis means prayer. Ibn ʿAtiyya calls this weak, but al-Qurtubi says it is sound because prayer includes magnification, sanctification, and tasbih. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(قتادة)+]
- 2:30/q44/p4 Qatada glosses tasbih as tasbih itself and taqdis as prayer. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada glosses tasbih as tasbih itself and taqdis as prayer. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q44/p5 Qatada explains the angels' tasbih as glorification and their taqdis as prayer. — DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada explains the angels' tasbih as glorification and their taqdis as prayer. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(قتادة)]
