- 2:30/q44/p6 Tasbih declares God free of what is unfitting; praise recalls His blessings; taqdis describes Him with fitting exaltation and denies what is unfitting. — BURSEVI (author): Tasbih declares God free of what is unfitting; praise recalls His blessings; taqdis describes Him with fitting exaltation and denies what is unfitting. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q44/p7 Al-Taysir distinguishes tasbih as negating what is unfitting for God and taqdis as affirming what is fitting for Him. — BURSEVI (التيسير): Al-Taysir distinguishes tasbih as negating what is unfitting for God and taqdis as affirming what is fitting for Him. [1 notes: BURSEVI(التيسير)]
- 2:30/q44/p8 Dawud al-Qaysari says tasbih is broader: taqdis also negates perfections ascribed to created things, since their attachment to creation restricts them. — BURSEVI (داود القيصري): Dawud al-Qaysari says tasbih is broader: taqdis also negates perfections ascribed to created things, since their attachment to creation restricts them. [1 notes: BURSEVI(داود القيصري)]
- 2:30/q44/p9 Taqdis is commonly treated as synonymous with tasbih. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Taqdis is commonly treated as synonymous with tasbih. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q44/p10 To avoid redundancy, one view assigns tasbih to deeds and taqdis to beliefs. — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): To avoid redundancy, one view assigns tasbih to deeds and taqdis to beliefs. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q44/p11 Another view makes tasbih transcendence from what is unbefitting and taqdis a stronger transcendence of God's essence from what He does not deem fitting; the pairing “سبوح قدوس” supports this. — ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another view makes tasbih transcendence from what is unbefitting and taqdis a stronger transcendence of God's essence from what He does not deem fitting; the pairing “سبوح قدوس” supports this. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]

## 2:30/q45 How is “I know what” parsed?  [إِنِّىٓ أَعْلَمُ مَا · grammar]
- 2:30/q45/p1 «أعلم» can be a present verb or a participle meaning “knowing,” as «الله أكبر» can mean “God is great.” — NAHHAS (author): «أعلم» can be a present verb or a participle meaning “knowing,” as «الله أكبر» can mean “God is great.” [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q45/p2 For “I know what you do not know,” the more apparent parsing makes aʿlam a future verb and mā its direct object. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): For “I know what you do not know,” the more apparent parsing makes aʿlam a future verb and mā its direct object. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL+]
- 2:30/q45/p3 An alternative parses aʿlam as a noun with mā in the genitive as its complement. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (قوم): An alternative parses aʿlam as a noun with mā in the genitive as its complement. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(قوم)]
- 2:30/q45/p4 Sībawayh and al-Khalīl say an afʿal form used as an indefinite proper name does not take nunation; al-Akhfash allows it. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (سيبويه والخليل): Sībawayh and al-Khalīl say an afʿal form used as an indefinite proper name does not take nunation; al-Akhfash allows it. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(سيبويه والخليل)]
- 2:30/q45/p5 ما is a relative pronoun with an omitted object pronoun understood as لا تعلمونه; the clause is the accusative object of أعلم. — TABRISI (author): ما is a relative pronoun with an omitted object pronoun understood as لا تعلمونه; the clause is the accusative object of أعلم. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q45/p6 Aʿlam may be a future verb or an adjective meaning knowing, as in Allahu akbar meaning great; in the first reading ma is its accusative object, in the second it is genitive by annexation. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Aʿlam may be a future verb or an adjective meaning knowing, as in Allahu akbar meaning great; in the first reading ma is its accusative object, in the second it is genitive by annexation. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q45/p7 Ibn ʿAtiyya says aʿlam cannot take nunation here by grammarians' agreement; Sibawayh and al-Khalil do not nunate an indefinite proper name on the afʿal pattern, while al-Akhfash does. — QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya says aʿlam cannot take nunation here by grammarians' agreement; Sibawayh and al-Khalil do not nunate an indefinite proper name on the afʿal pattern, while al-Akhfash does. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)]
- 2:30/q45/p8 Al-Mahdawi allows an assumed tanwin on aʿlam if it means ʿalim, with ma then accusative, analogous to the construction hajjaj bayt Allah. — QURTUBI-FULL (المهدوي): Al-Mahdawi allows an assumed tanwin on aʿlam if it means ʿalim, with ma then accusative, analogous to the construction hajjaj bayt Allah. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(المهدوي)]
- 2:30/q45/p9 ما is a relative pronoun and object of أعلم, with its antecedent omitted. — NASAFI-FULL (author): ما is a relative pronoun and object of أعلم, with its antecedent omitted. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q45/p10 He prefers parsing ما after أعلم as a relative pronoun and rejects treating it as an indefinite adjective. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He prefers parsing ما after أعلم as a relative pronoun and rejects treating it as an indefinite adjective. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:30/q45/p11 Makkī, al-Mahdawī, and others allow أعلم as an active participle; on that parse ما may be genitive by annexation or accusative. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي بن أبي طالب والمهدوي وغيرهما): Makkī, al-Mahdawī, and others allow أعلم as an active participle; on that parse ما may be genitive by annexation or accusative. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مكي بن أبي طالب والمهدوي وغيرهما)]
- 2:30/q45/p12 Others parse أعلم as a comparative meaning more knowing than you, with ما governed as the object of an implied verb meaning I know. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعضهم): Others parse أعلم as a comparative meaning more knowing than you, with ما governed as the object of an implied verb meaning I know. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q45/p13 The clause إني أعلم ما لا تعلمون occupies the accusative position as content of قال. — SAMIN-DURR (author): The clause إني أعلم ما لا تعلمون occupies the accusative position as content of قال. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q45/p14 أعلم is apparently an imperfect verb governing ما as object; ما may be relative or indefinite with the resumptive pronoun omitted. — SAMIN-DURR (author): أعلم is apparently an imperfect verb governing ما as object; ما may be relative or indefinite with the resumptive pronoun omitted. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:30/q45/p15 Al-Mahdawī, Makkī, and Abū al-Baqāʾ treat أعلم as the noun عالم; ما may be genitive or accusative and أعلم is diptote. — SAMIN-DURR (المهدوي): Al-Mahdawī, Makkī, and Abū al-Baqāʾ treat أعلم as the noun عالم; ما may be genitive or accusative and أعلم is diptote. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(المهدوي)]
- 2:30/q45/p16 Another analysis takes أعلم as comparative with منكم omitted and ما as object of implied علمت, since the comparative does not govern it. — SAMIN-DURR (author): Another analysis takes أعلم as comparative with منكم omitted and ما as object of implied علمت, since the comparative does not govern it. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q45/p17 Ma is declarative or relative before laysa, lam, or la, and after illa, as in the cited examples. — BURHAN (author): Ma is declarative or relative before laysa, lam, or la, and after illa, as in the cited examples. [1 notes: BURHAN]

## 2:30/q46 What does God know that the angels do not?  [أَعْلَمُ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ · interpretation]
turns on: Whether the hidden matter is Iblis, righteous descendants, Adam's fitness, forgiveness, or God's wider unseen knowledge.
- 2:30/q46/p1 God knew righteous people would arise from Adam's descendants — TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says God knew that Adam's descendants would include prophets, messengers, righteous people, and inhabitants of Paradise. JISHUMI (author): One explanation is that God knows prophets, scholars, and saints will arise among Adam’s descendants and knows their great work in religion and worldly affairs. TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says God knows which prophets and righteous people will descend from Adam. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (قتادة): Qatāda says God knows that prophets and righteous people will emerge from Adam’s descendants. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says God knows how events end: testing may lead someone thought obedient, like Iblīs, to sin, while someone thought sinful may obey. QURTUBI-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says God's hidden knowledge was that among those to be created on earth would be prophets, virtuous people, and the obedient. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says God knew that prophets and righteous people would descend from the vicegerent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says God knows outcomes: trials may lead someone thought obedient into sin, or someone thought sinful into obedience. [8 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة); JISHUMI; TABRISI(قتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج,قتادة); QURTUBI-FULL(قتادة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزجاج,قتادة)]
- 2:30/q46/p2 God knew Iblis's hidden pride and coming disobedience — MUJAHID (author): God knew Iblis's disobedience and created him for it. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says God knew Iblis's disobedience and that He created him for it. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says God knew Iblis's disobedience and created him for it, while knowing Adam's obedience and creating him for it. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): A report from Ibn Abbas says Iblis led the angels of the lowest heaven, then became proud and rebellious; God knew the rebelliousness within Iblis. Al-Suddi is also reported similarly. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid says God knew Iblis's disobedience and created him for it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي عن أشياخه): Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and al-Suddī from his teachers say God knew what was in Iblis’s mind despite his rebellion and disobedience. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One explanation is that God knows Iblis's coming disobedience and Adam's obedience. TAB-FULL (ابن مسعود، وأصحاب النبي): Al-Tabari transmits from Ibn Masud and other Companions the gloss that God's knowledge concerned Iblis's affair. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid once glosses the phrase as God's knowledge of Iblis's created-for disobedience, and once as referring to Adam. [9 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); TAB-FULL(ابن مسعود، وأصحاب النبي,مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,مجاهد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي عن أشياخه); ALUSI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q46/p3 God's knowledge encompasses unseen realities — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): The verse before the story summarizes both Adam's narrative and the two requirements of vicegerency: the earth is given to people and God knows everything. STUDYQURAN (R): The phrase generally means that good will come from humanity despite inevitable bloodshed and corruption, but only God knows what that good is. [2 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR; STUDYQURAN(R)]
- 2:30/q46/p4 God knew His mercy and forgiveness for humans — THALABI (author): Another explanation is that God knows humans will sin and that He will forgive them. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q46/p5 God knew the hidden wisdom and benefits of the appointment — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): God knows benefits and wise purposes hidden from the angels. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q46/p6 God knows the believers’ pure beliefs and keen inward protection of their covenantal love, even when disobedience stains their outward state. — QUSHAYRI (author): God knows the believers’ pure beliefs and keen inward protection of their covenantal love, even when disobedience stains their outward state. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q46/p7 God knows His love for them and the inward secrets He hides in them, unlike angels who openly display their states. — QUSHAYRI (author): God knows His love for them and the inward secrets He hides in them, unlike angels who openly display their states. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q46/p8 Qatada says God knew Adam’s descendants would include people who obey Him. — WAHIDI-BASIT (قتادة): Qatada says God knew Adam’s descendants would include people who obey Him. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q46/p9 A further estimate is that God knew their states when He spoke to the angels. — JISHUMI (author): A further estimate is that God knew their states when He spoke to the angels. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q46/p10 Creating people whom God knows will disbelieve can still be wise and right. — JISHUMI (author): Creating people whom God knows will disbelieve can still be wise and right. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q46/p11 On Ibn ʿAbbās’s account, God’s “I know what you do not know” refers to the discordant pride hidden within Iblīs, unknown to the angels. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس): On Ibn ʿAbbās’s account, God’s “I know what you do not know” refers to the discordant pride hidden within Iblīs, unknown to the angels. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q46/p12 God knew Iblīs harbored pride and disobedience and wished to reveal it to the angels; this is related from Ibn ʿAbbās and by al-Suddī from his elders. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس والسدي عن أشياخه): God knew Iblīs harbored pride and disobedience and wished to reveal it to the angels; this is related from Ibn ʿAbbās and by al-Suddī from his elders. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس والسدي عن أشياخه)]
- 2:30/q46/p13 One interpretation is that God knows the pride and disobedience within Iblīs; this is attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and al-Suddī from his elders. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي عن أشياخه): One interpretation is that God knows the pride and disobedience within Iblīs; this is attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and al-Suddī from his elders. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي عن أشياخه)]
- 2:30/q46/p14 Ibn Zayd takes the words to mean that God knows He will fill Hell with jinn and humans. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd takes the words to mean that God knows He will fill Hell with jinn and humans. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن زيد)]
- 2:30/q46/p15 On al-Qaffal's reading, God knows humanity's hidden virtues and reasons for creating them, beyond their outward corruption. — RAZI-FULL (القفال): On al-Qaffal's reading, God knows humanity's hidden virtues and reasons for creating them, beyond their outward corruption. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(القفال)]
- 2:30/q46/p16 Ibn Zayd says God knew which jinn and humans would fill Hell. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says God knew which jinn and humans would fill Hell. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن زيد)]
- 2:30/q46/p17 The wording leaves unspecified what God knows beyond the angels’ knowledge; that claim requires their submission and acceptance of his decision because his knowledge encompasses what no scholar can encompass. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The wording leaves unspecified what God knows beyond the angels’ knowledge; that claim requires their submission and acceptance of his decision because his knowledge encompasses what no scholar can encompass. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q46/p18 The human capacity for the hidden of the hidden is the concealed secret in human preparedness that God knows and the angels do not. — BURSEVI (author): The human capacity for the hidden of the hidden is the concealed secret in human preparedness that God knows and the angels do not. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q46/p19 God knew Adam's knowledge and fitness — BURSEVI (author): God's answer to the angels is expanded by Adam's election, teaching him the names, and creating him with His hands: succession is bestowed by God, not earned through abundant worship. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q46/p20 The detailed reply explains the earlier summary that God knows what the angels do not; a genuine summary and its detail have the same meaning. — ELMALILI (author): The detailed reply explains the earlier summary that God knows what the angels do not; a genuine summary and its detail have the same meaning. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q46/p21 The wording need not add what the angels do know after what they do not know, since that is obvious; the emphasis is on the reach of God's knowledge beyond theirs. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The wording need not add what the angels do know after what they do not know, since that is obvious; the emphasis is on the reach of God's knowledge beyond theirs. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q46/p22 Ibn Ishaq reads the phrase as God's knowledge of what was among them, which He had not disclosed to the angels: disobedience, corruption, and bloodshed. — TAB-FULL (ابن إسحاق): Ibn Ishaq reads the phrase as God's knowledge of what was among them, which He had not disclosed to the angels: disobedience, corruption, and bloodshed. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن إسحاق)]
- 2:30/q46/p23 Other proposed purposes are to expose angels' inability to encompass God's knowledge and to honor Adam in advance so they welcome him when created. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other proposed purposes are to expose angels' inability to encompass God's knowledge and to honor Adam in advance so they welcome him when created. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q46/p24 God's knowledge of Adam also includes hidden faults unknown to angels: bodily dispositions shared with animals and vices produced by the commanding self when the spirit follows it without the discipline of sacred law. — BURSEVI (author): God's knowledge of Adam also includes hidden faults unknown to angels: bodily dispositions shared with animals and vices produced by the commanding self when the spirit follows it without the discipline of sacred law. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q46/p25 Successful vicegerency requires authority to manage the entrusted domain and the capacity and knowledge to do so; the preceding verse summarizes both through creation of the earth for people and God's knowledge. — SAMARRAI-ASRAR (author): Successful vicegerency requires authority to manage the entrusted domain and the capacity and knowledge to do so; the preceding verse summarizes both through creation of the earth for people and God's knowledge. [1 notes: SAMARRAI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q46/p26 One explanation is that Adam's descendants will include obedient prophets, saints, and scholars. — BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): One explanation is that Adam's descendants will include obedient prophets, saints, and scholars. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q46/p27 A further explanation is that humans will sin and God will forgive them. — BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): A further explanation is that humans will sin and God will forgive them. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q46/p28 A further view takes the words to mean God's exclusive knowledge of how to manage people's interests. — TABRISI (author): A further view takes the words to mean God's exclusive knowledge of how to manage people's interests. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q46/p29 The shift from general address to the Prophet's particular address hints that he receives the greatest share of what follows; Abū Ḥayyān recalls his universal mission and precedence among prophets. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The shift from general address to the Prophet's particular address hints that he receives the greatest share of what follows; Abū Ḥayyān recalls his universal mission and precedence among prophets. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q46/p30 He argues that creation contains hidden realities: even the natural world, its narrowest and lowest domain, continually yields new unknowns as scientific inquiry uncovers facts. — TABATABAI (author): He argues that creation contains hidden realities: even the natural world, its narrowest and lowest domain, continually yields new unknowns as scientific inquiry uncovers facts. [1 notes: TABATABAI]

## 2:30/q47 What wisdom makes the successor's appointment fitting despite corruption?  [أَعْلَمُ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ · theology]
- 2:30/q47/p1 The interrogative is affirmative like the poet's 'Are you not the best riders?' — AKHFASH (author): The interrogative is affirmative like the poet's 'Are you not the best riders?' [1 notes: AKHFASH]
- 2:30/q47/p2 Ibn Zayd interprets the angels’ question as amazement that God would create beings who disobey their creator, rather than as knowledge that this would certainly occur. — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd interprets the angels’ question as amazement that God would create beings who disobey their creator, rather than as knowledge that this would certainly occur. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد)]
- 2:30/q47/p3 The angels are the best of living beings and the most intelligent of creatures. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): The angels are the best of living beings and the most intelligent of creatures. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p4 Al-Zajjaj gives the concise sense of the angels' words as declaring God free of evil. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj gives the concise sense of the angels' words as declaring God free of evil. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q47/p5 The angels declare God free of every evil and say, Glory and praise belong to God. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The angels declare God free of every evil and say, Glory and praise belong to God. [1 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 2:30/q47/p6 One proposed question is how God could create beings He knows will disbelieve, when all His acts are good and He cannot do wrong. — JISHUMI (author): One proposed question is how God could create beings He knows will disbelieve, when all His acts are good and He cannot do wrong. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q47/p7 Abū ʿAlī says God knows the interests involved and which location is best for angels and people. — JISHUMI (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī says God knows the interests involved and which location is best for angels and people. [1 notes: JISHUMI(أبو علي)]
- 2:30/q47/p8 God’s answer implies He does not do evil; it is meaningful as an appeal to knowledge of governance and interests, leading Him to do what is best. — JISHUMI (author): God’s answer implies He does not do evil; it is meaningful as an appeal to knowledge of governance and interests, leading Him to do what is best. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q47/p9 God's answer points to the wisdom and benefit in His decision. — BAGHAWI-FULL (author): God's answer points to the wisdom and benefit in His decision. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p10 This reading requires ellipsis: God knows future corruption and hidden interests, the angels implicitly compare their obedience with humans, and His reply supplies what they do not know. — TABRISI (author): This reading requires ellipsis: God knows future corruption and hidden interests, the angels implicitly compare their obedience with humans, and His reply supplies what they do not know. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q47/p11 God knows the interests better and does what is most fitting; the reply implies He does not perform evil, since otherwise it would have no force. — TABRISI (author): God knows the interests better and does what is most fitting; the reply implies He does not perform evil, since otherwise it would have no force. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q47/p12 They divide angels into celestial governors, related to spheres as human souls to bodies, and proximate angels absorbed in knowing, loving, and obeying God; some add good or evil angels governing the lower world. — RAZI-FULL (الفلاسفة): They divide angels into celestial governors, related to spheres as human souls to bodies, and proximate angels absorbed in knowing, loving, and obeying God; some add good or evil angels governing the lower world. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الفلاسفة)]
- 2:30/q47/p13 Philosophers agree on celestial governors and angels devoted to God, while some also posit terrestrial angels governing the lower world, good as angels and evil as devils. — RAZI-FULL (الفلاسفة): Philosophers agree on celestial governors and angels devoted to God, while some also posit terrestrial angels governing the lower world, good as angels and evil as devils. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الفلاسفة)]
- 2:30/q47/p14 Philosophers say evil necessarily accompanies worldly good, but good outweighs evil; abandoning much good to avoid little evil would be worse. — RAZI-FULL (الحكماء): Philosophers say evil necessarily accompanies worldly good, but good outweighs evil; abandoning much good to avoid little evil would be worse. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الحكماء)]
- 2:30/q47/p15 On this account, good from the lower world's composition exceeds its evils, so wisdom requires creating it. — RAZI-FULL (الحكماء): On this account, good from the lower world's composition exceeds its evils, so wisdom requires creating it. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الحكماء)]
- 2:30/q47/p16 Most Mu'tazilites and many jurists say angels can do both good and evil. — RAZI-FULL (جمهور المعتزلة وكثير من الفقهاء): Most Mu'tazilites and many jurists say angels can do both good and evil. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(جمهور المعتزلة وكثير من الفقهاء)]
- 2:30/q47/p17 The poet's phrase thawb al-muqaddas means a purified garment; prayer purifies a servant from sins and is entered in the worshipper's best state because it is the best deed. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The poet's phrase thawb al-muqaddas means a purified garment; prayer purifies a servant from sins and is entered in the worshipper's best state because it is the best deed. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p18 God’s announcement teaches consultation, honors the future inhabitant by naming him before creation, and displays his preponderant virtue through the angels’ question and answer; wisdom requires abundant good even when accompanied by lesser evil. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): God’s announcement teaches consultation, honors the future inhabitant by naming him before creation, and displays his preponderant virtue through the angels’ question and answer; wisdom requires abundant good even when accompanied by lesser evil. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p19 God knows hidden wisdom: among humans will be prophets, saints, and scholars, along with matters the angels do not know. — NASAFI-FULL (author): God knows hidden wisdom: among humans will be prophets, saints, and scholars, along with matters the angels do not know. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p20 Each angelic rank knows what is below and within itself but not above; they see the soul as evil-bearing intermediary between luminous spirit and dark body. — QASHANI (author): Each angelic rank knows what is below and within itself but not above; they see the soul as evil-bearing intermediary between luminous spirit and dark body. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q47/p21 He says the best explanation of the unspecified knowledge is God’s statement that he knows the unseen of the heavens and earth. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He says the best explanation of the unspecified knowledge is God’s statement that he knows the unseen of the heavens and earth. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:30/q47/p22 The story of Adam is a manifest example that humanity is the intended purpose of this existence and that wisdom requires resurrection to a realm without disorder, where divine wisdom is fully evident. — BIQAI-FULL (author): The story of Adam is a manifest example that humanity is the intended purpose of this existence and that wisdom requires resurrection to a realm without disorder, where divine wisdom is fully evident. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p23 God knows the wisdom of appointing Adam: his descendants include obedient and disobedient people, through whom merit and justice become manifest; creatures cannot know the Creator’s hidden governance. — BURSEVI (author): God knows the wisdom of appointing Adam: his descendants include obedient and disobedient people, through whom merit and justice become manifest; creatures cannot know the Creator’s hidden governance. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q47/p24 Wisdom permits a small evil when it prevents a much greater evil, as removing a diseased limb can save the whole body. — BURSEVI (author): Wisdom permits a small evil when it prevents a much greater evil, as removing a diseased limb can save the whole body. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q47/p25 Some gnostics identify the disputers as earth angels, jinn, and devils dominated by darkness, rather than heavenly angels who recognize the Perfect Human’s rank. — BURSEVI (بعض العارفين): Some gnostics identify the disputers as earth angels, jinn, and devils dominated by darkness, rather than heavenly angels who recognize the Perfect Human’s rank. [1 notes: BURSEVI(بعض العارفين)]
- 2:30/q47/p26 Those soul-born faults include vanity, ostentation, seeking reputation, envy, preferring worldly life, innovation, deviation, and holding evil opinions. — BURSEVI (author): Those soul-born faults include vanity, ostentation, seeking reputation, envy, preferring worldly life, innovation, deviation, and holding evil opinions. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q47/p27 The best-known interpretation identifies the khalīfa as Adam, matching the report, singular wording, and context; corruption and bloodshed are attributed to him causally or as potential in those succeeding him. — ALUSI-FULL (المشهور): The best-known interpretation identifies the khalīfa as Adam, matching the report, singular wording, and context; corruption and bloodshed are attributed to him causally or as potential in those succeeding him. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(المشهور)]
- 2:30/q47/p28 In a report transmitted from Ubadah ibn al-Samit from Abu Dharr, the Prophet calls “Glory be to God and praise be to Him” the best utterance God chose for His angels or servants; no grading is — ALUSI-FULL (أبو ذر عن النبي): In a report transmitted from Ubadah ibn al-Samit from Abu Dharr, the Prophet calls “Glory be to God and praise be to Him” the best utterance God chose for His angels or servants; no grading is stated. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أبو ذر عن النبي)]
- 2:30/q47/p29 The angels understood the honor of vicegerency but feared the possible evil of giving a creature on earth such high discretionary authority. — ELMALILI (author): The angels understood the honor of vicegerency but feared the possible evil of giving a creature on earth such high discretionary authority. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q47/p30 The following verse's teaching of Adam and demonstration of his distinction give detailed evidence for his fitness as steward, explaining what the angels did not know. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The following verse's teaching of Adam and demonstration of his distinction give detailed evidence for his fitness as steward, explaining what the angels did not know. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p31 Although 4:1 and 39:6 later describe Adam's creation and human reproduction, this passage concerns capacities, responsibilities, authority, and blessings bestowed on humanity. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Although 4:1 and 39:6 later describe Adam's creation and human reproduction, this passage concerns capacities, responsibilities, authority, and blessings bestowed on humanity. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q47/p32 Alternatively, the angels may have witnessed Adam's creation and inferred from his material and spiritual traits that he could do both good and evil. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Alternatively, the angels may have witnessed Adam's creation and inferred from his material and spiritual traits that he could do both good and evil. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR+]
- 2:30/q47/p33 God makes part of the wisdom clear in the following account by teaching Adam the names. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): God makes part of the wisdom clear in the following account by teaching Adam the names. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p34 Alternatively, they affirmed God's divinity and wisdom to show they sought details rather than attacking Him. — RAZI-FULL (author): Alternatively, they affirmed God's divinity and wisdom to show they sought details rather than attacking Him. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q47/p35 God connects His acts to hidden causes and wisdom, yet no creature can claim entitlement through a cause: He creates causes and grants outcomes, and His will is the true original cause. — ELMALILI (author): God connects His acts to hidden causes and wisdom, yet no creature can claim entitlement through a cause: He creates causes and grants outcomes, and His will is the true original cause. [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 2:30/q48 What does the exchange teach about praising oneself or seeking office?  [وَنَحْنُ نُسَبِّحُ · law]
- 2:30/q48/p1 The angels privately claimed that no creation would surpass them in knowledge or honor; God tested that boast by teaching Adam the names and challenging them to name them. — TAB-FULL (الحسن وقتادة): The angels privately claimed that no creation would surpass them in knowledge or honor; God tested that boast by teaching Adam the names and challenging them to name them. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الحسن وقتادة)]
- 2:30/q48/p2 Al-Rabi reports that the angels privately boasted of superior knowledge and honor; God taught Adam the names to show his superiority, exposing their public words and hidden claim. — TAB-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi reports that the angels privately boasted of superior knowledge and honor; God taught Adam the names to show his superiority, exposing their public words and hidden claim. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(الربيع بن أنس)]
- 2:30/q48/p3 Pride in one's work and abundant worship makes ignorance one's home; the angels' boast led them to confess ignorance. — SULAMI (بعضهم): Pride in one's work and abundant worship makes ignorance one's home; the angels' boast led them to confess ignorance. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q48/p4 One view says the angels were not objecting or boasting of their deeds; they were expressing wonder and seeking the wisdom behind the decision. — BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): One view says the angels were not objecting or boasting of their deeds; they were expressing wonder and seeking the wisdom behind the decision. [1 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل)]
- 2:30/q48/p5 Others take the words as self-praise and a description of their state, as in Joseph’s “I am a careful keeper, knowledgeable”; Ibn Atiyya says this suits their a — IBNATIYYA-FULL (آخرون): Others take the words as self-praise and a description of their state, as in Joseph’s “I am a careful keeper, knowledgeable”; Ibn Atiyya says this suits their astonishment at God appointing a disobedient successor. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(آخرون)]
- 2:30/q48/p6 The Prophet lists self-admiration among three ruinous traits, used against angelic self-praise. — RAZI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet lists self-admiration among three ruinous traits, used against angelic self-praise. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(النبي)]
- 2:30/q48/p7 Self-praise is not always forbidden, since 93:11 says to speak of God's blessing. — RAZI-FULL (author): Self-praise is not always forbidden, since 93:11 says to speak of God's blessing. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q48/p8 One view takes the angels’ self-description as permission to praise one’s qualifications before an appointing authority when seeking office, if one can avoid injustice and sees a benefit. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قول غير مسمى): One view takes the angels’ self-description as permission to praise one’s qualifications before an appointing authority when seeking office, if one can avoid injustice and sees a benefit. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قول غير مسمى)]
- 2:30/q48/p9 The angels’ example permits Joseph to seek office and describe his own qualifications, as he did when asking to oversee Egypt’s storehouses. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The angels’ example permits Joseph to seek office and describe his own qualifications, as he did when asking to oversee Egypt’s storehouses. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q48/p10 After the angels boasted, God favored Adam with knowledge of the names; their prostration showed that God needs no service and that grace belongs to Him. — BURSEVI (author): After the angels boasted, God favored Adam with knowledge of the names; their prostration showed that God needs no service and that grace belongs to Him. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q48/p11 This is not forbidden backbiting: the angels speak in a pre-legal realm, for public advice can justify mentioning faults, the subject is an unnamed class, and no harm of backbiting arose here. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): This is not forbidden backbiting: the angels speak in a pre-legal realm, for public advice can justify mentioning faults, the subject is an unnamed class, and no harm of backbiting arose here. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]

## 2:30/q49 What do the Hārūt and Mārūt reports say about this exchange?  [أَتَجْعَلُ · reports]
- 2:30/q49/p1 The Prophet reports Harut and Marut were tested after angels questioned human corruption; a woman led them into idolatry, murder, wine and sex, and they chose worldly punishment. — MUSNAD-AHMAD (author): The Prophet reports Harut and Marut were tested after angels questioned human corruption; a woman led them into idolatry, murder, wine and sex, and they chose worldly punishment. [1 notes: MUSNAD-AHMAD(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q49/p2 Ibn ʿUmar reports that after Adam descended, angels questioned humanity’s corruption; God tested Hārūt and Mārūt on earth, where a woman’s demands led them to shirk, drunkenness, sex, and murder before they chose worldly punishment. — IBNHIBBAN (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar reports that after Adam descended, angels questioned humanity’s corruption; God tested Hārūt and Mārūt on earth, where a woman’s demands led them to shirk, drunkenness, sex, and murder before they chose worldly punishment. [1 notes: IBNHIBBAN(ابن عمر)]
- 2:30/q49/p3 Ibn Umar reports angels' challenge and God sending Harut and Marut to earth; they refused idolatry and killing a child, but wine led to intercourse and murder, after which they chose worldly punishment. — DURR-FULL (عبد الله بن عمر): Ibn Umar reports angels' challenge and God sending Harut and Marut to earth; they refused idolatry and killing a child, but wine led to intercourse and murder, after which they chose worldly punishment. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(عبد الله بن عمر)]
- 2:30/q49/p4 The Futuhat says Harut and Marut were among the angels who disputed Adam and were tested by God through manifesting corruption and bloodshed. — BURSEVI (الفتوحات): The Futuhat says Harut and Marut were among the angels who disputed Adam and were tested by God through manifesting corruption and bloodshed. [1 notes: BURSEVI(الفتوحات)]
- 2:30/q49/p5 One Harut and Marut version says a woman demanded idol worship; lust overcame them after they refused, and she rose to their heavenly place as Venus, which they linked to mocking Adam's descendants. — RAZI-FULL (المخالفون): One Harut and Marut version says a woman demanded idol worship; lust overcame them after they refused, and she rose to their heavenly place as Venus, which they linked to mocking Adam's descendants. [0 notes: RAZI-FULL(المخالفون)-]
- 2:30/q49/p6 Razi rejects the Harut and Marut tale: saying they would not sin if tested accuses God of lying or ignorance; even the Hashwiyya concede pre-descent immunity. — RAZI-FULL (author): Razi rejects the Harut and Marut tale: saying they would not sin if tested accuses God of lying or ignorance; even the Hashwiyya concede pre-descent immunity. [0 notes: RAZI-FULL-]

## 2:30/q50 What reports connect the exchange to the heavenly and earthly Houses?  [أَتَجْعَلُ · reports]
- 2:30/q50/p1 Angels ask why a successor other than themselves was appointed, since they would not corrupt, kill or rival. — AZRAQI (author): Angels ask why a successor other than themselves was appointed, since they would not corrupt, kill or rival. [1 notes: AZRAQI(الملائكة)]
- 2:30/q50/p2 Angels took God's answer as rebuke and circled the Throne weeping three hours. — AZRAQI (author): Angels took God's answer as rebuke and circled the Throne weeping three hours. [1 notes: AZRAQI(علي بن الحسين)]
- 2:30/q50/p3 God placed al-Dirah beneath the Throne on four emerald pillars under red ruby. — AZRAQI (author): God placed al-Dirah beneath the Throne on four emerald pillars under red ruby. [1 notes: AZRAQI(علي بن الحسين)]
- 2:30/q50/p4 Angels were told to circle al-Dirah, identified as the Frequented House. — AZRAQI (author): Angels were told to circle al-Dirah, identified as the Frequented House. [1 notes: AZRAQI(علي بن الحسين)]
- 2:30/q50/p5 Seventy thousand angels enter the Frequented House daily and nightly, never to return. — AZRAQI (author): Seventy thousand angels enter the Frequented House daily and nightly, never to return. [1 notes: AZRAQI(علي بن الحسين)]
- 2:30/q50/p6 Angels built an earthly house to match the heavenly House and earth's people were told to circle it. — AZRAQI (author): Angels built an earthly house to match the heavenly House and earth's people were told to circle it. [1 notes: AZRAQI(علي بن الحسين)]

## 2:30/q51 What inward lesson is drawn from Adam's temptation?  [خَلِيفَةًۭ · inward]
- 2:30/q51/p1 Adam's forgetfulness means leaving the covenant, with 20:88 and 32:14. — TUSTARI (author): Adam's forgetfulness means leaving the covenant, with 20:88 and 32:14. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p2 Another forgetfulness is losing a memory, illustrated by Moses and the fish, al-Khidr, and 87:6. — TUSTARI (author): Another forgetfulness is losing a memory, illustrated by Moses and the fish, al-Khidr, and 87:6. [1 notes: TUSTARI(بعض التابعين)]
- 2:30/q51/p3 Adam's thought was innate appetite rather than reflective contemplation. — TUSTARI (author): Adam's thought was innate appetite rather than reflective contemplation. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p4 A heart settled on other than God faces Satan's whisper; Adam's deed teaches descendants through the Last Day. — TUSTARI (author): A heart settled on other than God faces Satan's whisper; Adam's deed teaches descendants through the Last Day. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p5 The issue was Adam's inward aspiration, not eating itself. — TUSTARI (author): The issue was Adam's inward aspiration, not eating itself. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p6 Desire for what is not one's own risks abandonment without God's protection. — TUSTARI (author): Desire for what is not one's own risks abandonment without God's protection. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p7 Adam planned for permanence, so appetite overpowered reason by prior decree. — TUSTARI (author): Adam planned for permanence, so appetite overpowered reason by prior decree. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p8 The Prophet is quoted that appetite can overcome knowledge and reason. — TUSTARI (author): The Prophet is quoted that appetite can overcome knowledge and reason. [1 notes: TUSTARI(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q51/p9 Angels purify themselves through utterances God inspired. — TUSTARI (author): Angels purify themselves through utterances God inspired. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p10 God taught Adam to seek help in obedience, protection from sin, gratitude and patience against his lower soul. — TUSTARI (author): God taught Adam to seek help in obedience, protection from sin, gratitude and patience against his lower soul. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p11 The heart must reject even slight whispers of the lower soul. — TUSTARI (author): The heart must reject even slight whispers of the lower soul. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p12 Obedience and protection from sin depend on God's aid. — TUSTARI (author): Obedience and protection from sin depend on God's aid. [1 notes: TUSTARI]
- 2:30/q51/p13 Adam desired permanence after seeing the forbidden tree; Satan exploited the wish, with 7:20. — TUSTARI (author): Adam desired permanence after seeing the forbidden tree; Satan exploited the wish, with 7:20. [1 notes: TUSTARI]

## 2:30/q52 What inward account is given of human vicegerency?  [خَلِيفَةًۭ · inward]
- 2:30/q52/p1 Vicegerency requires seeing things through the divine essence in separation and connection, though neither is ever truly detached from God; temporal beings cannot unite with the pre-eternal. — SULAMI (بعض العراقيين): Vicegerency requires seeing things through the divine essence in separation and connection, though neither is ever truly detached from God; temporal beings cannot unite with the pre-eternal. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعض العراقيين)]
- 2:30/q52/p2 The angels' objection and claim to precedence reflect their inability to see divine attributes manifest uniquely in the human composite encompassing both worlds. — QASHANI (author): The angels' objection and claim to precedence reflect their inability to see divine attributes manifest uniquely in the human composite encompassing both worlds. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p3 Corruption and bloodshed signify beastly lust and predatory anger, powers necessary when spirit is joined to body; angels know their own purity and these lower acts. — QASHANI (author): Corruption and bloodshed signify beastly lust and predatory anger, powers necessary when spirit is joined to body; angels know their own purity and these lower acts. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p4 The angels do not know that human comprehensiveness attracts divine light, the secret of God's statement that He knows what they do not. — QASHANI (author): The angels do not know that human comprehensiveness attracts divine light, the secret of God's statement that He knows what they do not. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p5 The time reference points to eternity; God's saying casts His will to create Adam into sacred angelic and spiritual beings. — QASHANI (author): The time reference points to eternity; God's saying casts His will to create Adam into sacred angelic and spiritual beings. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p6 Human speech and action similarly exist first in spirit and deeper unseen levels, then the self's lower unseen level, before appearing on the limbs. — QASHANI (author): Human speech and action similarly exist first in spirit and deeper unseen levels, then the self's lower unseen level, before appearing on the limbs. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p7 Making is broader than origination or formation, so God says 'making' rather than 'creating'; the human combines the two worlds. — QASHANI (author): Making is broader than origination or formation, so God says 'making' rather than 'creating'; the human combines the two worlds. [1 notes: QASHANI]
- 2:30/q52/p8 A vicegerent mediates divine influx because God is utterly transcendent while recipients are entangled in bodily needs and blameworthy traits; the mediator has both transcendence and attachment. — BURSEVI (author): A vicegerent mediates divine influx because God is utterly transcendent while recipients are entangled in bodily needs and blameworthy traits; the mediator has both transcendence and attachment. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q52/p9 The Sufi masters exclude the two world-dwelling angels from the address: other angels bearing divine names and attributes are addressed, while the absorbed ones neither perceive nor prostrate. — ALUSI-FULL (السادة الصوفية): The Sufi masters exclude the two world-dwelling angels from the address: other angels bearing divine names and attributes are addressed, while the absorbed ones neither perceive nor prostrate. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(السادة الصوفية)]
- 2:30/q52/p10 They say the Perfect Human's vicegerency continues until and beyond Resurrection; if this cosmic spirit departed, the world would die, since humanity is its inward support. — ALUSI-FULL (أهل الله): They say the Perfect Human's vicegerency continues until and beyond Resurrection; if this cosmic spirit departed, the world would die, since humanity is its inward support. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أهل الله)]
- 2:30/q52/p11 The mystical reading sees the verse as explaining the complete wisdom of vicegerency: God manifests His names and attributes through a fully prepared human mirror, since the angels' limited readiness cannot manifest them all. — ALUSI-FULL (قوم): The mystical reading sees the verse as explaining the complete wisdom of vicegerency: God manifests His names and attributes through a fully prepared human mirror, since the angels' limited readiness cannot manifest them all. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(قوم)]

## 2:30/q53 What inward lesson is drawn from the angels and God's favor?  [إِنِّىٓ أَعْلَمُ · inward]
- 2:30/q53/p1 The angels made their prayer a means to God; He burned thousands of them for an hour, and they admitted their — SULAMI (ابن عطاء): The angels made their prayer a means to God; He burned thousands of them for an hour, and they admitted their incapacity. [1 notes: SULAMI(ابن عطاء)]
- 2:30/q53/p2 God adorned Adam with special gifts and manifested attributes of pre-eternity; bowing to him became nearness to God, while pride toward him brought distance. — SULAMI (بعض البغداديين): God adorned Adam with special gifts and manifested attributes of pre-eternity; bowing to him became nearness to God, while pride toward him brought distance. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعض البغداديين)]
- 2:30/q53/p3 God exposed the angels' hidden self-regard and displayed generosity: prior care is not undone by offenses, and honoring them for their deeds would have concealed that grace. — SULAMI (الواسطي): God exposed the angels' hidden self-regard and displayed generosity: prior care is not undone by offenses, and honoring them for their deeds would have concealed that grace. [1 notes: SULAMI(الواسطي)]
- 2:30/q53/p4 Their inability to grasp what was decreed taught them their shortfall before the realities of the Real. — SULAMI (بعضهم): Their inability to grasp what was decreed taught them their shortfall before the realities of the Real. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q53/p5 Knowledge of God is more complete than spiritual exertion. — SULAMI (بعضهم): Knowledge of God is more complete than spiritual exertion. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q53/p6 The angels reproached and belittled Adam without recognizing his unique creation, so God ordered them to prostrate to him. — SULAMI (بعضهم): The angels reproached and belittled Adam without recognizing his unique creation, so God ordered them to prostrate to him. [1 notes: SULAMI(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q53/p7 He cites an unnamed poet saying that among the many beauties seen, the speaker's choice fell upon the beloved, illustrating Adam's special selection. — QUSHAYRI (author): He cites an unnamed poet saying that among the many beauties seen, the speaker's choice fell upon the beloved, illustrating Adam's special selection. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p8 God clothed the angels in obedience and purification, but clothed humans in pardon, mercy, generosity, and forgiveness. — QUSHAYRI (author): God clothed the angels in obedience and purification, but clothed humans in pardon, mercy, generosity, and forgiveness. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p9 If God’s protection made the angels fortunate, His mercy has also reached human beings. — QUSHAYRI (author): If God’s protection made the angels fortunate, His mercy has also reached human beings. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p10 Knowledge is a greater merit than action: the angels had more and earlier worship, while Adam had more knowledge, which established his rank. — QUSHAYRI (author): Knowledge is a greater merit than action: the angels had more and earlier worship, while Adam had more knowledge, which established his rank. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p11 God’s answer that He knows what the angels do not know refers to His forgiveness of Adam’s descendants. — QUSHAYRI (author): God’s answer that He knows what the angels do not know refers to His forgiveness of Adam’s descendants. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p12 The brokenness of servants’ hearts carries powerful standing with God, despite their outwardly disobedient condition. — QUSHAYRI (author): The brokenness of servants’ hearts carries powerful standing with God, despite their outwardly disobedient condition. [1 notes: QUSHAYRI]
- 2:30/q53/p13 Some mystically inclined interpreters see divine care as preceding Adam's sin and surviving it: although disobedience caused expulsion, God did not strip Adam of honor or succession and later accepted him. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أهل الإشارة): Some mystically inclined interpreters see divine care as preceding Adam's sin and surviving it: although disobedience caused expulsion, God did not strip Adam of honor or succession and later accepted him. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أهل الإشارة)]

## 2:30/q54 How does God's speech to angels relate to eternal divine speech?  [قَالَ رَبُّكَ · theology]
- 2:30/q54/p1 Angelic speech is truly a matter of meaning, not verbal or nominal forms; otherwise a statement made in summary would not amount to its detailed version. — ELMALILI (author): Angelic speech is truly a matter of meaning, not verbal or nominal forms; otherwise a statement made in summary would not amount to its detailed version. [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 2:30/q55 How is the first person yāʾ in innī aʿlam read?  [إِنِّىٓ أَعْلَمُ · readings]
turns on: Whether the first person yāʾ is opened, left still, or omitted in recitation.
- 2:30/q55/p1 The first person yāʾ is opened — IBNMUJAHID (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr opens the first-person yāʾ after a kasra before an open or kasra hamza attached to a noun or verb, unless the letter is lengthened. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): Opening the possessive yāʾ treats it as a built pronoun like hāʾ or kāf; the vowel is fitting after the preceding kasra. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (القراء): One group opens yāʾ before a hamza with fatḥa but uses sukūn before ḍamma or kasra, avoiding those heavier vowel combinations. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (القراء): Another group uses sukūn before ḍamma, the heaviest vowel, but opens yāʾ before the other hamzas. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr opens the attached yāʾ after a kasra before an open or kasra hamza; this rule includes إني أعلم. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو عمرو): He does not open the yāʾ before a hamza with ḍamma, as in the cited examples; his opening rule is restricted to the open and kasra hamza. FARISI-HUJJA (author): The case for opening the attached yāʾ is that its original vowel is fatḥa, parallel to the addressee kāf, which is also opened. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr’s reason for opening yāʾ before a light hamza is that hamza can induce fatḥa in otherwise unopen forms such as يقرأ and يبرأ. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr and Ibn Kathir pronounce the first-person yāʾ with fatḥa before an open hamza, as in innī aʿlam and innī arā; Abu Amr also does so before a kasra hamza, and Nafi adds cases before a ḍamma hamza. BAGHAWI-FULL (أهل الحجاز والبصرة): The Hijaz and Basra readers open the ya in inni a'lam and in most possessive ya forms before an alif with fatḥa; readers differ on the detailed exceptions. NASHR (المدنيان وابن كثير وأبو عمرو): The two Medinan readers, Ibn Kathīr, and Abū ʿAmr open the yāʾ in إني أعلم at both occurrences. QIRAAT-ER (author): The canonical innīya with fatḥa and innī with sukūn differ phonologically in pause or connection, with no semantic change. [12 notes: IBNMUJAHID(أبو عمرو); IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA(القراء); FARISI-HUJJA(أبو عمرو); WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو عمرو); BAGHAWI-FULL(أهل الحجاز والبصرة); NASHR(المدنيان وابن كثير وأبو عمرو); QIRAAT-ER]
- 2:30/q55/p2 The first person yāʾ is omitted — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (القراء): A third group deletes the possessive yāʾ entirely and relies on the remaining vowel. [1 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA(القراء)]
- 2:30/q55/p3 Ibn Kathīr does not follow one consistent rule for these attached yāʾ vowels; his individual cases are listed separately by sūra. — FARISI-HUJJA (ابن كثير): Ibn Kathīr does not follow one consistent rule for these attached yāʾ vowels; his individual cases are listed separately by sūra. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(ابن كثير)]
- 2:30/q55/p4 An unnamed poet's «الدميان» shows the original yāʾ in the dual form of «دم». — NAHHAS (الشاعر): An unnamed poet's «الدميان» shows the original yāʾ in the dual form of «دم». [1 notes: NAHHAS(الشاعر)]
- 2:30/q55/p5 Those who vocalize the yāʾ in «إني» avoid leaving a one-letter noun without a vowel. — NAHHAS (من حرك الياء): Those who vocalize the yāʾ in «إني» avoid leaving a one-letter noun without a vowel. [1 notes: NAHHAS(من حرك الياء)]
- 2:30/q55/p6 Those who leave the yāʾ quiescent say it has joined to what precedes it. — NAHHAS (من أسكنها): Those who leave the yāʾ quiescent say it has joined to what precedes it. [1 notes: NAHHAS(من أسكنها)]
- 2:30/q55/p7 When attached to a one-letter word, opening yāʾ is preferable so elision at two adjacent sukūns does not leave a one-letter word; sukūn remains permissible. — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): When attached to a one-letter word, opening yāʾ is preferable so elision at two adjacent sukūns does not leave a one-letter word; sukūn remains permissible. [1 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q55/p8 Arabic permits four possessive-yāʾ forms: opening, sukūn, adding an alif to soften the vowel, or deleting the yāʾ for brevity. — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): Arabic permits four possessive-yāʾ forms: opening, sukūn, adding an alif to soften the vowel, or deleting the yāʾ for brevity. [1 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q55/p9 Those who leave the yāʾ still argue that even fatḥa is avoided on yāʾ; Arabic compounds can preserve it still, and poetry extends that practice. — FARISI-HUJJA (مُسْكِنُو الياء): Those who leave the yāʾ still argue that even fatḥa is avoided on yāʾ; Arabic compounds can preserve it still, and poetry extends that practice. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA(مُسْكِنُو الياء)]
- 2:30/q55/p10 Hamza with fatḥa or kasra can draw fatḥa onto the preceding yāʾ, but a ḍamma hamza does not; the cited word examples show the distinction. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): Hamza with fatḥa or kasra can draw fatḥa onto the preceding yāʾ, but a ḍamma hamza does not; the cited word examples show the distinction. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q55/p11 Those who open the yāʾ argue that its vowel is original, parallel to the addressee’s kaf; fatḥa is tolerated on yāʾ, as declension and forms with a preceding vowelless consonant show. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Those who open the yāʾ argue that its vowel is original, parallel to the addressee’s kaf; fatḥa is tolerated on yāʾ, as declension and forms with a preceding vowelless consonant show. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:30/q55/p12 Those who leave the yāʾ vowelless argue that fatḥa, like the other vowels, is disliked with yāʾ; Arabic can suppress it even when grammar would otherwise require it. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Those who leave the yāʾ vowelless argue that fatḥa, like the other vowels, is disliked with yāʾ; Arabic can suppress it even when grammar would otherwise require it. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]
- 2:30/q55/p13 Inna conveys emphasis grounded in the speaker’s own self and knowledge, while the first-person suffix marks attribution to oneself. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Inna conveys emphasis grounded in the speaker’s own self and knowledge, while the first-person suffix marks attribution to oneself. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q55/p14 Dam has a weak final root consonant, either yāʾ or wāw; short and doubled forms are attested, and its original pattern is faʿl or fuʿl. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Dam has a weak final root consonant, either yāʾ or wāw; short and doubled forms are attested, and its original pattern is faʿl or fuʿl. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q55/p15 The explanation that fatḥa merely clarifies the faint yāʾ is rejected: if that were the cause, Abū ʿAmr would also open it before ḍamma hamza and other gutturals. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): The explanation that fatḥa merely clarifies the faint yāʾ is rejected: if that were the cause, Abū ʿAmr would also open it before ḍamma hamza and other gutturals. [0 notes: FARISI-HUJJA-]

## 2:30/q56 What assimilation rules or other recitational details cite this verse?  [نُسَبِّحُ · readings]
- 2:30/q56/p1 The nūn cannot be assimilated into the following nūn because the result would put two quiescent consonants together. — NAHHAS (author): The nūn cannot be assimilated into the following nūn because the result would put two quiescent consonants together. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q56/p2 Mīm may assimilate into mīm. ما is accusative with أعلم if verbal; if nominal, it may be genitive by annexation or accusative, with tanwīn dropped because the form is diptote. — NAHHAS (author): Mīm may assimilate into mīm. ما is accusative with أعلم if verbal; if nominal, it may be genitive by annexation or accusative, with tanwīn dropped because the form is diptote. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q56/p3 Sukūn is reinforced by yāʾ’s similarity to alif: they are close in articulation, can substitute for each other, and the long vowel supplies what movement would otherwise provide. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): Sukūn is reinforced by yāʾ’s similarity to alif: they are close in articulation, can substitute for each other, and the long vowel supplies what movement would otherwise provide. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q56/p4 The construction nahnu was discussed earlier; its nuns cannot be assimilated because that would bring two vowelless consonants together. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The construction nahnu was discussed earlier; its nuns cannot be assimilated because that would bring two vowelless consonants together. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q56/p5 The al-Yazīdī transmission assimilates the discussed sequence, while the remaining group articulates it; Ibn Mujāhid preferred articulation. — NASHR (ابن مجاهد): The al-Yazīdī transmission assimilates the discussed sequence, while the remaining group articulates it; Ibn Mujāhid preferred articulation. [1 notes: NASHR(ابن مجاهد)+]
- 2:30/q56/p6 Via ʿIṣma b. ʿUrwa, Abū ʿAmr is reported to avoid assimilation because the word has few letters. — NASHR (أبو عمرو): Via ʿIṣma b. ʿUrwa, Abū ʿAmr is reported to avoid assimilation because the word has few letters. [1 notes: NASHR(أبو عمرو)]
- 2:30/q56/p7 For wāw preceded by ḍamma, Ibn Farḥ and several other routes transmit assimilation, with named route exceptions; Egyptian and Maghribi authorities also preferred it. — NASHR (ابن فرح): For wāw preceded by ḍamma, Ibn Farḥ and several other routes transmit assimilation, with named route exceptions; Egyptian and Maghribi authorities also preferred it. [1 notes: NASHR(ابن فرح)]
- 2:30/q56/p8 The continuation reports assimilation through al-Yazīdī and Shujāʿ, while other transmitters of Abū ʿAmr articulate; Abū Ṭāhir preferred articulation because of the shīn’s spreading quality. — NASHR (اليزيدي وشجاع): The continuation reports assimilation through al-Yazīdī and Shujāʿ, while other transmitters of Abū ʿAmr articulate; Abū Ṭāhir preferred articulation because of the shīn’s spreading quality. [1 notes: NASHR(اليزيدي وشجاع)]
- 2:30/q56/p9 The author says sīn’s hiss does not prevent assimilation, so the two reported faces are balanced and both are sound; he read and accepts both. — NASHR (author): The author says sīn’s hiss does not prevent assimilation, so the two reported faces are balanced and both are sound; he read and accepts both. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q56/p10 Qāf assimilates into kāf in the listed contexts; the source gives examples both with a vowel before qāf and after a preceding sukūn. — NASHR (author): Qāf assimilates into kāf in the listed contexts; the source gives examples both with a vowel before qāf and after a preceding sukūn. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q56/p11 Kāf assimilates into qāf when preceded by a vowel; when preceded by sukūn, the source says it is not assimilated and lists examples. — NASHR (author): Kāf assimilates into qāf when preceded by a vowel; when preceded by sukūn, the source says it is not assimilated and lists examples. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q56/p12 Lām assimilates into rā when preceded by a vowel; the passage begins listing examples of this rule. — NASHR (author): Lām assimilates into rā when preceded by a vowel; the passage begins listing examples of this rule. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q56/p13 Zayd ibn ʿAli read khalifa with qaf as khalīqa. — THALABI (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn ʿAli read khalifa with qaf as khalīqa. [1 notes: THALABI(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p14 A variant reads خليقة, with qāf instead of fāʾ. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): A variant reads خليقة, with qāf instead of fāʾ. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q56/p15 Zayd ibn Ali reads khalīqa with qāf. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn Ali reads khalīqa with qāf. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p16 A variant reading has خليقة with qaf. — RAZI-FULL (author): A variant reading has خليقة with qaf. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q56/p17 The general reading is khalifa with faʾ; Zayd ibn ʿAli reads khalīqa with qaf. — QURTUBI-FULL (زيد بن علي): The general reading is khalifa with faʾ; Zayd ibn ʿAli reads khalīqa with qaf. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p18 Zayd ibn ʿAlī and Abū al-Barahsim ʿImrān read khalīqa with qāf, a variant whose meaning is clear. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn ʿAlī and Abū al-Barahsim ʿImrān read khalīqa with qāf, a variant whose meaning is clear. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p19 A reading substitutes خليقة with qāf for the verse’s خليفة. — SAMIN-DURR (author): A reading substitutes خليقة with qāf for the verse’s خليفة. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q56/p20 A noncanonical reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli reads khalifa as khalīqa; al-Zamakhshari and others transmit it, and al-Qurtubi also records it. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (زيد بن علي): A noncanonical reading attributed to Zayd ibn ʿAli reads khalifa as khalīqa; al-Zamakhshari and others transmit it, and al-Qurtubi also records it. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p21 Zayd ibn ʿAlī reads khalīfa with qāf as khalīqa; the meaning remains clear. — ALUSI-FULL (زيد بن علي): Zayd ibn ʿAlī reads khalīfa with qāf as khalīqa; the meaning remains clear. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(زيد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q56/p22 Al-Dānī rejects the few-letters objection: consensus assimilates لك كيدا, which is shorter in pronunciation than آل. — NASHR (الداني): Al-Dānī rejects the few-letters objection: consensus assimilates لك كيدا, which is shorter in pronunciation than آل. [0 notes: NASHR(الداني)-]

## 2:30/q57 How does the following account explain this verse?  [أَعْلَمُ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ · links]
- 2:30/q57/p1 Another view is that God meant Adam’s superiority, the angels’ commanded prostration, and Adam’s advantage through knowledge of the names; the angels had assumed none would be more honored than they. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Another view is that God meant Adam’s superiority, the angels’ commanded prostration, and Adam’s advantage through knowledge of the names; the angels had assumed none would be more honored than they. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT]

## 2:30/q58 What other grammatical points are made about this verse?  [* · grammar]
- 2:30/q58/p1 Surplus letters may be dropped without changing intended construction. — MAJAZ (author): Surplus letters may be dropped without changing intended construction. [1 notes: MAJAZ]
- 2:30/q58/p2 Angels sought knowledge rather than objected, or disliked disobedience after the jinn's example. — AKHFASH (author): Angels sought knowledge rather than objected, or disliked disobedience after the jinn's example. [1 notes: AKHFASH]
- 2:30/q58/p3 On this reconstruction, angels generalized from some descendants’ corruption to all of them; God had concealed that many would obey, reform the earth, preserve blood and attain honor. — TAB-FULL (author): On this reconstruction, angels generalized from some descendants’ corruption to all of them; God had concealed that many would obey, reform the earth, preserve blood and attain honor. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p4 Sukūn avoids the heaviness of a vowel on yāʾ and restores the pronoun's underlying built form. — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): Sukūn avoids the heaviness of a vowel on yāʾ and restores the pronoun's underlying built form. [1 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q58/p5 Although vowels on weak letters are often avoided, fatḥa is acceptable: yāʾ in al-qāḍī is inflected with fatḥa, and accusative غَوَاشٍ retains its yāʾ. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): Although vowels on weak letters are often avoided, fatḥa is acceptable: yāʾ in al-qāḍī is inflected with fatḥa, and accusative غَوَاشٍ retains its yāʾ. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q58/p6 The moving feminine/plural nūn in فعلن and يفعلن is another pronominal parallel; despite an objection that it moves only to avoid adjacent sukūns, its likeness to kāf and tāʾ supports movement. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): The moving feminine/plural nūn in فعلن and يفعلن is another pronominal parallel; despite an objection that it moves only to avoid adjacent sukūns, its likeness to kāf and tāʾ supports movement. [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]
- 2:30/q58/p7 A khalīfa is one who takes another’s place, from the verb khalafa, “to succeed someone.” — MAWARDI-FULL (author): A khalīfa is one who takes another’s place, from the verb khalafa, “to succeed someone.” [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p8 The angels may have asked to learn the wisdom, not objected: after the jinn were removed, they occupied earth and asked why corrupting humans would replace them. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): The angels may have asked to learn the wisdom, not objected: after the jinn were removed, they occupied earth and asked why corrupting humans would replace them. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q58/p9 An unnamed poet’s line supports ellipsis: after a conditional أينما, the understood verb ذهب is omitted. — JISHUMI (الشاعر): An unnamed poet’s line supports ellipsis: after a conditional أينما, the understood verb ذهب is omitted. [1 notes: JISHUMI(الشاعر)]
- 2:30/q58/p10 Al-Mahdawi parses the accusative as an answer to the question; Ibn Atiyya prefers the wa al-sarf analysis. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (المهدوي): Al-Mahdawi parses the accusative as an answer to the question; Ibn Atiyya prefers the wa al-sarf analysis. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(المهدوي)]
- 2:30/q58/p11 A fourth objection infers angels are punished because 74:31 calls them keepers of Hell and 2:39 describes its eternal inhabitants. — RAZI-FULL (المخالفون): A fourth objection infers angels are punished because 74:31 calls them keepers of Hell and 2:39 describes its eternal inhabitants. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المخالفون)]
- 2:30/q58/p12 The relative pronoun man is the first object of jaʿala and fiha stands in for the second; yufsidu agrees with man in form, though a non-Qur'anic plural yufsidoon may agree by meaning. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The relative pronoun man is the first object of jaʿala and fiha stands in for the second; yufsidu agrees with man in form, though a non-Qur'anic plural yufsidoon may agree by meaning. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p13 Al-Jawhari explains that hajjaj bayt Allah is genitive by annexation for women who made pilgrimage, but accusative if no pilgrimage occurred and tanwin is intended. — QURTUBI-FULL (الجوهري): Al-Jawhari explains that hajjaj bayt Allah is genitive by annexation for women who made pilgrimage, but accusative if no pilgrimage occurred and tanwin is intended. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الجوهري)]
- 2:30/q58/p14 The angels express surprise that a corruptor be appointed to cultivate and repair the earth, or that sinful people replace its obedient inhabitants; they seek the hidden wisdom like learners seeking clarification, not objecting to God. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The angels express surprise that a corruptor be appointed to cultivate and repair the earth, or that sinful people replace its obedient inhabitants; they seek the hidden wisdom like learners seeking clarification, not objecting to God. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p15 The construction is like 5:61, where entering with disbelief means entering while disbelieving. — NASAFI-FULL (author): The construction is like 5:61, where entering with disbelief means entering while disbelieving. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p16 The predicate after qāla is introduced by anna to stress that the reported statement is certain to occur. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The predicate after qāla is introduced by anna to stress that the reported statement is certain to occur. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p17 Sībawayh says that when the active participle operates, using it as an operative form is better than annexing it to its object. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (سيبويه): Sībawayh says that when the active participle operates, using it as an operative form is better than annexing it to its object. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(سيبويه)]
- 2:30/q58/p18 Al-Kisāʾī regards the operative and annexed constructions as equal; Abū Ḥayyān chooses annexation as better. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الكسائي): Al-Kisāʾī regards the operative and annexed constructions as equal; Abū Ḥayyān chooses annexation as better. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الكسائي)]
- 2:30/q58/p19 One view makes jaʿala mean create, taking one object; Abū Rawq holds this, close to reports from al-Ḥasan and Qatāda. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو روق): One view makes jaʿala mean create, taking one object; Abū Rawq holds this, close to reports from al-Ḥasan and Qatāda. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو روق)]
- 2:30/q58/p20 A second view, held by al-Farrāʾ, makes jaʿala mean turn into, taking two objects, with fī al-arḍ supplying the second; al-Zamakhsharī gives only this view. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الفراء): A second view, held by al-Farrāʾ, makes jaʿala mean turn into, taking two objects, with fī al-arḍ supplying the second; al-Zamakhsharī gives only this view. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الفراء)]
- 2:30/q58/p21 Views differ on Adam's delegated task: judging with truth and justice, or cultivating the earth through farming, construction, and canals. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Views differ on Adam's delegated task: judging with truth and justice, or cultivating the earth through farming, construction, and canals. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p22 The Basrans allow omission of the subject in a verbal noun construction; unlike some grammarians, Abū Ḥayyān says this is not forbidden because generic nouns do not take pronouns. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The Basrans allow omission of the subject in a verbal noun construction; unlike some grammarians, Abū Ḥayyān says this is not forbidden because generic nouns do not take pronouns. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p23 Islamic doctrine holds angels immune from sin and objection; this premise led exegetes to interpret their words in a linguistically plausible way, against a minority of Hashwiyya. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Islamic doctrine holds angels immune from sin and objection; this premise led exegetes to interpret their words in a linguistically plausible way, against a minority of Hashwiyya. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p24 He insists that the apparent reading is the present-tense verb أعلم and warns against imposing unattested grammatical patterns on Quranic wording. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): He insists that the apparent reading is the present-tense verb أعلم and warns against imposing unattested grammatical patterns on Quranic wording. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p25 من may be a relative pronoun, leaving the following clause without an independent syntactic position; as an indefinite it makes the clause accusative. — SAMIN-DURR (author): من may be a relative pronoun, leaving the following clause without an independent syntactic position; as an indefinite it makes the clause accusative. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q58/p26 Grammarians disagree whether a verbal noun used in place of a verb can contain an implicit pronoun. — SAMIN-DURR (author): Grammarians disagree whether a verbal noun used in place of a verb can contain an implicit pronoun. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q58/p27 إني contracts إنني by dropping one of three adjacent nūns; the correct view deletes the middle nūn rather than the nūn of protection. — SAMIN-DURR (author): إني contracts إنني by dropping one of three adjacent nūns; the correct view deletes the middle nūn rather than the nūn of protection. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR+]
- 2:30/q58/p28 The angels’ question sought guidance about what they did not know, not denial or objection; Ibn Jarir prefers this explanation. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن جرير): The angels’ question sought guidance about what they did not know, not denial or objection; Ibn Jarir prefers this explanation. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير)+]
- 2:30/q58/p29 The catalogue gives 139 instances for mīm, 70 for nūn, and 18 for wāw, split into 13 after a ḍamma and five after sukūn. — NASHR (author): The catalogue gives 139 instances for mīm, 70 for nūn, and 18 for wāw, split into 13 after a ḍamma and five after sukūn. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q58/p30 Qawl is the outward arrangement of verbal forms, like the joining of visible forms; the heart apprehends speech through the ear as it apprehends visible things through sight. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Qawl is the outward arrangement of verbal forms, like the joining of visible forms; the heart apprehends speech through the ear as it apprehends visible things through sight. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p31 The angels first submitted, then objected that a successor would corrupt earth; after God turned from them, they circled the Throne six years seeking forgiveness and repenting. — DURR-FULL (أنس): The angels first submitted, then objected that a successor would corrupt earth; after God turned from them, they circled the Throne six years seeking forgiveness and repenting. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(أنس)]
- 2:30/q58/p32 The recipient phrase routinely comes first in this construction because the quoted speech is often long, while its placement adds emphasis and anticipation. — BURSEVI (author): The recipient phrase routinely comes first in this construction because the quoted speech is often long, while its placement adds emphasis and anticipation. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q58/p33 Bursevī illustrates mediation with cartilage between bone and flesh, a minister between ruler and subjects, and dry wood between fire and wet wood. — BURSEVI (author): Bursevī illustrates mediation with cartilage between bone and flesh, a minister between ruler and subjects, and dry wood between fire and wet wood. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q58/p34 The angels’ objection and dispute before the wise Creator is excused because of His perfect wisdom and workmanship. — BURSEVI (author): The angels’ objection and dispute before the wise Creator is excused because of His perfect wisdom and workmanship. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q58/p35 The plural's feminine agreement reflects the semantic feminine of a group, as grammarians say every collective is feminine by construing it as a community. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The plural's feminine agreement reflects the semantic feminine of a group, as grammarians say every collective is feminine by construing it as a community. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p36 أعلم is an imperfect verb here; construing it as the elative adjective “more knowing” is not an acceptable reading of the Qur'an. — ALUSI-FULL (author): أعلم is an imperfect verb here; construing it as the elative adjective “more knowing” is not an acceptable reading of the Qur'an. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p37 The lām in “for You” may mark purpose with nuqaddisu, be a transitive marker as in “I prostrated to God,” indicate clarification, or be extra with the object understood as the prepositional phrase. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The lām in “for You” may mark purpose with nuqaddisu, be a transitive marker as in “I prostrated to God,” indicate clarification, or be extra with the object understood as the prepositional phrase.
