- 2:30/q58/p39 If “Your praise” is construed as an active genitive, it figuratively means God's enabling and guidance, denying any implication of angelic self-congratulation. — ALUSI-FULL (author): If “Your praise” is construed as an active genitive, it figuratively means God's enabling and guidance, denying any implication of angelic self-congratulation. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p40 A third aspect of the Qur’an’s miracle is its wisdom-rich meanings and scientific indications, subjects largely absent from eloquent Arab speech. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): A third aspect of the Qur’an’s miracle is its wisdom-rich meanings and scientific indications, subjects largely absent from eloquent Arab speech. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p41 The imperfect verbs indicate recurring occurrences of corruption and bloodshed, not behavior continuously displayed by humanity. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The imperfect verbs indicate recurring occurrences of corruption and bloodshed, not behavior continuously displayed by humanity. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q58/p42 Such accounts provide subjective certainty to the experiencers but do not bind those who hear them. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Such accounts provide subjective certainty to the experiencers but do not bind those who hear them. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q58/p43 Ibn ʿAṭiy — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya calls the accusative a waṣr wāw: the conjunction redirects a verb from its expected mood to accusative, as in other Qurʾanic readings. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية)]

## 2:30/q59 What other historical reports are attached to this account?  [* · reports]
- 2:30/q59/p1 Suddi's report says descendants would corrupt, envy and kill. — TAB-FULL (author): Suddi's report says descendants would corrupt, envy and kill. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي، عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وأصحاب النبي)]
- 2:30/q59/p2 Al-Ṭabarī explicitly says this is his description of how the report may be interpreted, not the interpretation he chooses for the verse itself. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī explicitly says this is his description of how the report may be interpreted, not the interpretation he chooses for the verse itself. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q59/p3 Qatada reports that God sought the angels’ counsel about Adam; they knew bloodshed and earthly corruption were especially hateful to God, while Adam’s line would also include prophets, messengers, righteous people and people of Paradise. — TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada reports that God sought the angels’ counsel about Adam; they knew bloodshed and earthly corruption were especially hateful to God, while Adam’s line would also include prophets, messengers, righteous people and people of Paradise. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q59/p4 Al-Tabari says knowledge of past events is reliable only through transmission that rules out collusion, lying, error and forgetfulness; the cited reports do not meet that standard, so the text-supported interpretation is preferable. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Tabari says knowledge of past events is reliable only through transmission that rules out collusion, lying, error and forgetfulness; the cited reports do not meet that standard, so the text-supported interpretation is preferable. [1 notes: TAB-FULL]
- 2:30/q59/p5 Al-Suddi reports from Ibn Abbas, Ibn Masud and some Companions that the angels’ phrase means they pray to God. — TAB-FULL (السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وأصحاب النبي): Al-Suddi reports from Ibn Abbas, Ibn Masud and some Companions that the angels’ phrase means they pray to God. [1 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي عن ابن عباس وابن مسعود وأصحاب النبي)]
- 2:30/q59/p6 The report about Adam and the angels’ prostration proves Muhammad’s prophethood to scriptural communities: Arabs did not know it, while scripture readers or revelation did. — ZAJJAJ (author): The report about Adam and the angels’ prostration proves Muhammad’s prophethood to scriptural communities: Arabs did not know it, while scripture readers or revelation did. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q59/p7 In the reported account, God gave the angels leave to ask about the matter. — ZAJJAJ (author): In the reported account, God gave the angels leave to ask about the matter. [1 notes: ZAJJAJ]
- 2:30/q59/p8 Ibn Sabit reports that the earth spread from Mecca and angels first circumambulated the House; he glosses the location of the khalifa as Mecca. — IBNABIHATIM (ابن سابط): Ibn Sabit reports that the earth spread from Mecca and angels first circumambulated the House; he glosses the location of the khalifa as Mecca. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن سابط)]
- 2:30/q59/p9 Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali reports that the angel al-Sijill saw Adam's creation and affairs in the Preserved Tablet and told Harut and Marut; they then spoke from a sense of superiority over the angels. — IBNABIHATIM (محمد بن علي): Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali reports that the angel al-Sijill saw Adam's creation and affairs in the Preserved Tablet and told Harut and Marut; they then spoke from a sense of superiority over the angels. [1 notes: IBNABIHATIM(محمد بن علي)]
- 2:30/q59/p10 The continuation of the quoted poem concludes the example for omitting the connective fa; its gloss is “so I was their neighbor.” — THALABI (author): The continuation of the quoted poem concludes the example for omitting the connective fa; its gloss is “so I was their neighbor.” [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q59/p11 One reported explanation is that among those appointed on earth will be prophets, saints, scholars, and righteous people. — THALABI (author): One reported explanation is that among those appointed on earth will be prophets, saints, scholars, and righteous people. [1 notes: THALABI]
- 2:30/q59/p12 A second report from Ibn ʿAbbās says Iblīs’s pride arose after God sent him to defeat and kill the corrupt jinn with his army. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس): A second report from Ibn ʿAbbās says Iblīs’s pride arose after God sent him to defeat and kill the corrupt jinn with his army. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q59/p13 Sibawayh reports a pronunciation with fatḥa in سبوح قدوس; ḍamma is more common, while fatḥa is more analogical. — TABRISI (سيبويه): Sibawayh reports a pronunciation with fatḥa in سبوح قدوس; ḍamma is more common, while fatḥa is more analogical. [1 notes: TABRISI(سيبويه)]
- 2:30/q59/p14 Such concise ellipsis is Qur'anic eloquence when the remaining wording signals what was omitted; the segment introduces al-Shanfara's poem as evidence. — TABRISI (author): Such concise ellipsis is Qur'anic eloquence when the remaining wording signals what was omitted; the segment introduces al-Shanfara's poem as evidence. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q59/p15 A report places Noah, Hud, Salih, and Shuʿayb's graves between Zamzam and the Corner and Station. — TABRISI (author): A report places Noah, Hud, Salih, and Shuʿayb's graves between Zamzam and the Corner and Station. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q59/p16 Al-Shanfara's poem omits “do not bury me, but leave me to be eaten by the hyena”; the intended continuation is recoverable. — TABRISI (author): Al-Shanfara's poem omits “do not bury me, but leave me to be eaten by the hyena”; the intended continuation is recoverable. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q59/p17 Abu Dawud's poem omits “if you disapprove, leave”; the omitted clause follows from the conditional context. — TABRISI (author): Abu Dawud's poem omits “if you disapprove, leave”; the omitted clause follows from the conditional context. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q59/p18 These authorities hold that God had informed the angels the khalīfa’s descendants would corrupt; al-Suddī reports they were told the descendants would envy and kill one another. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن مسعود وابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة وابن زيد وابن قتيبة): These authorities hold that God had informed the angels the khalīfa’s descendants would corrupt; al-Suddī reports they were told the descendants would envy and kill one another. - 2:30/q59/p19 The Prophet said Adam was created after the afternoon prayer on Friday, in its final hour before night; Muslim alone reports this. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said Adam was created after the afternoon prayer on Friday, in its final hour before night; Muslim alone reports this. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q59/p20 In that report, an angel could not say how long he had existed, only that God had created a star every 400,000 years and that many such stars had appeared since his creation. — RAZI-FULL (ملك): In that report, an angel could not say how long he had existed, only that God had created a star every 400,000 years and that many such stars had appeared since his creation. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(ملك)]
- 2:30/q59/p21 Razi says all prophets agree on the existence of angels, a matter of consensus among them, established by transmitted reports. — RAZI-FULL (الأنبياء عليهم السلام): Razi says all prophets agree on the existence of angels, a matter of consensus among them, established by transmitted reports. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الأنبياء عليهم السلام)]
- 2:30/q59/p22 A tafsir report says the heavens hear revelation like a chain striking stone, become frightened, then ask what God said and answer that He said the truth. — RAZI-FULL (التفسير): A tafsir report says the heavens hear revelation like a chain striking stone, become frightened, then ask what God said and answer that He said the truth. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(التفسير)]
- 2:30/q59/p23 A report from the Prophet s — RAZI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن سابط): A report from the Prophet says earth was spread from Mecca, where angels first circled the House; that place is the earth in the verse. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن سابط)]
- 2:30/q59/p24 One explanation, reported from Ibn Abbas, is that Adam succeeded the jinn removed from earth before his arrival. — RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): One explanation, reported from Ibn Abbas, is that Adam succeeded the jinn removed from earth before his arrival. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q59/p25 The Prophet said a community is not purified if the weak person's right is not taken from the strong; Ibn Majah transmits it, meaning may God not purify it. — QURTUBI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet said a community is not purified if the weak person's right is not taken from the strong; Ibn Majah transmits it, meaning may God not purify it. - 2:30/q59/p26 Labid’s poem uses aluk for a message, describing a mother sending her boy with one. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Labid’s poem uses aluk for a message, describing a mother sending her boy with one. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q59/p27 A report from Abu Dharr has the Prophet confirm Adam was a sent prophet. — QURTUBI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report from Abu Dharr has the Prophet confirm Adam was a sent prophet. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]

- 2:30/q59/p29 An unnamed poem illustrates how a beloved's many virtues outweigh one fault; another poem likewise says a single lapse should not provoke blame. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): An unnamed poem illustrates how a beloved's many virtues outweigh one fault; another poem likewise says a single lapse should not provoke blame. - 2:30/q59/p31 The cited poem pairs being bright-eyed with spending a night as if stung, illustrating that the two actions can occur together. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The cited poem pairs being bright-eyed with spending a night as if stung, illustrating that the two actions can occur together. - 2:30/q59/p32 A report attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās says the angels mean that they purify themselves from the deeds of Adam's descendants. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس): A report attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās says the angels mean that they purify themselves from the deeds of Adam's descendants. - 2:30/q59/p37 An anonymous poem uses ثوب المقدس for a garment purified for its wearers. — SAMIN-DURR (author): An anonymous poem uses ثوب المقدس for a garment purified for its wearers. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q59/p38 When أم follows it signals the omitted hamza; a poem’s بسبع رمين الجمر أم بثمان implies أبسبع. — SAMIN-DURR (author): When أم follows it signals the omitted hamza; a poem’s بسبع رمين الجمر أم بثمان implies أبسبع. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]

## 2:30/q60 How is this verse cited in wider discussions?  [* · links]
- 2:30/q60/p1 Outside the Qurʾānic wording, the plural «يفسدون» is possible by sense. — NAHHAS (author): Outside the Qurʾānic wording, the plural «يفسدون» is possible by sense. [1 notes: NAHHAS]
- 2:30/q60/p2 Abū Ḥātim clarifies that the woman called al-Zuhara was a human woman of that time, not the celestial planet Venus. — IBNHIBBAN (أبو حاتم): Abū Ḥātim clarifies that the woman called al-Zuhara was a human woman of that time, not the celestial planet Venus. [1 notes: IBNHIBBAN(أبو حاتم)]
- 2:30/q60/p3 The word sifāḥ for fornication reflects the waste of its fluid. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): The word sifāḥ for fornication reflects the waste of its fluid. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p4 A third is the familiar meaning of consecration or declaring holiness. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): A third is the familiar meaning of consecration or declaring holiness. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p5 A third explanation is that God means the benefits whose planning He alone knows. — MAWARDI-FULL (author): A third explanation is that God means the benefits whose planning He alone knows. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p6 Al-Zajjaj connects qudus, ‘bucket,’ with purification because one cleanses oneself using it. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj connects qudus, ‘bucket,’ with purification because one cleanses oneself using it. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q60/p7 Al-Zajjaj says God tests those thought obedient and they may become disobedient, while those thought disobedient may become obedient. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says God tests those thought obedient and they may become disobedient, while those thought disobedient may become obedient. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج)]
- 2:30/q60/p8 The first poet reverses the analogy: a spear’s shaking resembles an animal’s tremor in appearance, but does not arise from an animal’s pain or fear. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): The first poet reverses the analogy: a spear’s shaking resembles an animal’s tremor in appearance, but does not arise from an animal’s pain or fear. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p9 Ibn al-Muʿtazz gives the sword’s trembling the same actual cause as trembling in an animal. — JURJANI-ASRAR (ابن المعتز): Ibn al-Muʿtazz gives the sword’s trembling the same actual cause as trembling in an animal. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR(ابن المعتز)]
- 2:30/q60/p10 Al-Buhturi makes the spears’ thrusting look like stumbling and treats it as an action with a cause, paralleling Ibn al-Muʿtazz’s sword image. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): Al-Buhturi makes the spears’ thrusting look like stumbling and treats it as an action with a cause, paralleling Ibn al-Muʿtazz’s sword image. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p11 These poets feign that the source of a comparison is actually present, then invent a cause for it: a sky-earth marriage, a buried beloved in clouds, or a released fasting fetter. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): These poets feign that the source of a comparison is actually present, then invent a cause for it: a sky-earth marriage, a buried beloved in clouds, or a released fasting fetter. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p12 Snow as camphor and cloud-rain as tears are familiar comparisons; the crescent as a broken fetter is novel, though a broken bracelet supplies a parall — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): Snow as camphor and cloud-rain as tears are familiar comparisons; the crescent as a broken fetter is novel, though a broken bracelet supplies a parallel image. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p13 Al-Mutanabbi’s point is that noble qualities are intrinsically noble: neither a person’s bodily sex nor a name’s grammatical gender adds or removes their merit. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): Al-Mutanabbi’s point is that noble qualities are intrinsically noble: neither a person’s bodily sex nor a name’s grammatical gender adds or removes their merit. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p14 Zuhayr’s horses and pack animals do not denote literal mounts; the image says youthful desire has departed, like a journey’s purpose fulfilled and its animals unloaded. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): Zuhayr’s horses and pack animals do not denote literal mounts; the image says youthful desire has departed, like a journey’s purpose fulfilled and its animals unloaded. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p15 One may read the horses as youthful impulses and powers, but this is strained and should not be imposed on every image. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): One may read the horses as youthful impulses and powers, but this is strained and should not be imposed on every image. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p16 Farazdaq’s mount in ignorance means that he often hurried into falsehood and ignorance; an elaborate allegory would miss the immediate sense. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): Farazdaq’s mount in ignorance means that he often hurried into falsehood and ignorance; an elaborate allegory would miss the immediate sense. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p17 In ‘loose reins,’ take the equestrian image as a mental picture transferred to a person; interpreting the reins as restraint is needless overreading. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): In ‘loose reins,’ take the equestrian image as a mental picture transferred to a person; interpreting the reins as restraint is needless overreading. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p18 The image works by abstracting the horse’s condition in the mind and then imagining a person according to that condition. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): The image works by abstracting the horse’s condition in the mind and then imagining a person according to that condition. [1 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR]
- 2:30/q60/p19 Fronting the person or topic before its predicate alerts the listener to whom the report concerns; it does not claim that the action belongs exclusively to them. — JURJANI-DALAIL (author): Fronting the person or topic before its predicate alerts the listener to whom the report concerns; it does not claim that the action belongs exclusively to them. [1 notes: JURJANI-DALAIL]
- 2:30/q60/p20 Sibawayh explains that fronting an object and resuming it with a pronoun first calls attention to it, then predicates the action of it. — JURJANI-DALAIL (صاحب الكتاب): Sibawayh explains that fronting an object and resuming it with a pronoun first calls attention to it, then predicates the action of it. [1 notes: JURJANI-DALAIL(صاحب الكتاب)]
- 2:30/q60/p21 An ungoverned noun signals that a predication about it is intended; the listener is prepared for the report, which therefore lands more firmly and with less doubt. — JURJANI-DALAIL (author): An ungoverned noun signals that a predication about it is intended; the listener is prepared for the report, which therefore lands more firmly and with less doubt. [1 notes: JURJANI-DALAIL]
- 2:30/q60/p22 He cites the fronted topic in the statement about adopted deities as a clear Qur'anic instance of drawing attention before the predicate. — JURJANI-DALAIL (author): He cites the fronted topic in the statement about adopted deities as a clear Qur'anic instance of drawing attention before the predicate. [1 notes: JURJANI-DALAIL]
- 2:30/q60/p23 He also cites the wording about people arriving professing faith as another clear instance of mentioning the topic before its report. — JURJANI-DALAIL (author): He also cites the wording about people arriving professing faith as another clear instance of mentioning the topic before its report. [1 notes: JURJANI-DALAIL]
- 2:30/q60/p24 For a competition contract with an invalid condition, the author's school keeps the contract valid and voids the condition; al-Shāfiʿī invalidates the contract. — JISHUMI (author): For a competition contract with an invalid condition, the author's school keeps the contract valid and voids the condition; al-Shāfiʿī invalidates the contract. - 2:30/q60/p25 One view treats expenditure as voluntary; Mujāhid, al-Aṣamm, and Abū Muslim call it an established obligation, namely zakāt. — JISHUMI (author): One view treats expenditure as voluntary; Mujāhid, al-Aṣamm, and Abū Muslim call it an established obligation, namely zakāt. [1 notes: JISHUMI]
- 2:30/q60/p26 Servants need only know that God’s actions are good and wise, even when they cannot see the reason. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Servants need only know that God’s actions are good and wise, even when they cannot see the reason. [1 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p27 Ibn ʿAṭiyya says Abū ʿUbayda’s explanation is the direction toward which Ibn Kaysān inclined. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya says Abū ʿUbayda’s explanation is the direction toward which Ibn Kaysān inclined. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p28 Ibn ʿAbbās says Iblīs became proud after God appointed him keeper of the lowest heaven, taking the honor as a special merit. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says Iblīs became proud after God appointed him keeper of the lowest heaven, taking the honor as a special merit. [1 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q60/p29 Other explanations take the phrase as praying for God's sake or purifying oneself from sins. — TABRISI (author): Other explanations take the phrase as praying for God's sake or purifying oneself from sins. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q60/p30 Antara's curse on a camel omits the wish that its milk dry up, explaining its inability to bear young and increased strength for travel. — TABRISI (author): Antara's curse on a camel omits the wish that its milk dry up, explaining its inability to bear young and increased strength for travel. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 2:30/q60/p31 Mujāhid interprets it as honoring and magnifying God. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مجاهد): Mujāhid interprets it as honoring and magnifying God. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مجاهد)]
- 2:30/q60/p32 Qatāda interprets it as praying to God. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (قتادة): Qatāda interprets it as praying to God. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(قتادة)]
- 2:30/q60/p33 Abū Ṣāliḥ interprets it as magnification and praise. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو صالح): Abū Ṣāliḥ interprets it as magnification and praise. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو صالح)]
- 2:30/q60/p34 Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim al-Anbārī interprets it as submission and humility. — IBNJAWZI-ZAD (محمد بن القاسم الأنباري): Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim al-Anbārī interprets it as submission and humility. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(محمد بن القاسم الأنباري)]
- 2:30/q60/p35 An unnamed view nests all knowledge in four books, Qur'anic sciences in the Fātiḥa, and the Fātiḥa's sciences in the bāʾ of the basmala. — RAZI-FULL (بعضهم): An unnamed view nests all knowledge in four books, Qur'anic sciences in the Fātiḥa, and the Fātiḥa's sciences in the bāʾ of the basmala. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 2:30/q60/p36 Al-Rāzī says the aim of knowledge is to bring the servant to God; the basmala's bāʾ denotes attachment and thus expresses that aim. — RAZI-FULL (author): Al-Rāzī says the aim of knowledge is to bring the servant to God; the basmala's bāʾ denotes attachment and thus expresses that aim. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p37 Min is declinable because its ending changes under different governing factors, as shown by min ibnika versus min al-rajuli. — RAZI-FULL (author): Min is declinable because its ending changes under different governing factors, as shown by min ibnika versus min al-rajuli. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p38 Min has four uses: indicating an origin, a part, an explanation, or an augmentative function. — RAZI-FULL (author): Min has four uses: indicating an origin, a part, an explanation, or an augmentative function. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p39 Al-Mubarrad holds that min originally marks the beginning of an extent, with its other uses derived from that sense. — RAZI-FULL (المبرد): Al-Mubarrad holds that min originally marks the beginning of an extent, with its other uses derived from that sense. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المبرد)]
- 2:30/q60/p40 Others hold that the partitive use is primary and that min's other uses derive from it. — RAZI-FULL (آخرون): Others hold that the partitive use is primary and that min's other uses derive from it. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(آخرون)]
- 2:30/q60/p41 Other passages state اذكر explicitly, and the Qur'an functions as one discourse, so their explicit wording can suffice here; they may have preceded this sura. — RAZI-FULL (author): Other passages state اذكر explicitly, and the Qur'an functions as one discourse, so their explicit wording can suffice here; they may have preceded this sura. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p42 Most Magians and dualists posit two eternal, transparent, choosing substances, Light and Darkness, opposite in nature and action; Light is good and beneficial, Darkness its contrary. — RAZI-FULL (معظم المجوس والثنوية): Most Magians and dualists posit two eternal, transparent, choosing substances, Light and Darkness, opposite in nature and action; Light is good and beneficial, Darkness its contrary. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(معظم المجوس والثنوية)]
- 2:30/q60/p43 Another proof says the celestial world is nobler and life, intellect, and speech nobler than their opposites, so it is implausible that these exist only in the dark lower world. — RAZI-FULL (بعض الناس): Another proof says the celestial world is nobler and life, intellect, and speech nobler than their opposites, so it is implausible that these exist only in the dark lower world. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(بعض الناس)]
- 2:30/q60/p44 A third proof invokes mystics' witnessing and unveiling, remarkable guidance for rare treatments and antidotes, and true dreams; it persuades hearers and is certain to those who experience it. — RAZI-FULL (أصحاب المجاهدات وأصحاب الحاجات والضرورات): A third proof invokes mystics' witnessing and unveiling, remarkable guidance for rare treatments and antidotes, and true dreams; it persuades hearers and is certain to those who experience it. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(أصحاب المجاهدات وأصحاب الحاجات والضرورات)]
- 2:30/q60/p45 Their words, supported elsewhere, show constant worship that God does not deny. — RAZI-FULL (author): Their words, supported elsewhere, show constant worship that God does not deny. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p46 They hasten to obey God out of reverence, as in their unanimous prostration. — RAZI-FULL (author): They hasten to obey God out of reverence, as in their unanimous prostration. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p47 They do nothing except by God's revelation and command. — RAZI-FULL (author): They do nothing except by God's revelation and command. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p48 Eight bearers carry the Throne and Kursi; the smaller Kursi exceeds all seven heavens, displaying their strength. — RAZI-FULL (author): Eight bearers carry the Throne and Kursi; the smaller Kursi exceeds all seven heavens, displaying their strength. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p49 A single trumpet blast makes those in heaven and earth swoon, and the next revives them, showing immense power. — RAZI-FULL (author): A single trumpet blast makes those in heaven and earth swoon, and the next revives them, showing immense power. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p50 The opponent also cites the prohibition on declaring oneself pure in 53:32. — RAZI-FULL (المخالف): The opponent also cites the prohibition on declaring oneself pure in 53:32. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المخالف)]
- 2:30/q60/p51 The command statement is universal because each commanded act allows exception, which removes what otherwise falls within the statement. — RAZI-FULL (author): The command statement is universal because each commanded act allows exception, which removes what otherwise falls within the statement. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p52 Their status as honored servants who do not speak ahead of God and act by His command clears them of sin. — RAZI-FULL (author): Their status as honored servants who do not speak ahead of God and act by His command clears them of sin. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p53 Their gloss clears God's essence of bodily attributes and His acts of blame and foolishness. — RAZI-FULL (المعتزلة): Their gloss clears God's essence of bodily attributes and His acts of blame and foolishness. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(المعتزلة)]
- 2:30/q60/p54 Remembering God benefits and purifies the servant, not God; the homily likens the command to a loan that God, though rich, repays tenfold. — RAZI-FULL (author): Remembering God benefits and purifies the servant, not God; the homily likens the command to a loan that God, though rich, repays tenfold. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p55 God needs no army, but promises to help the servant who helps him, although heaven and earth are already his armies. — RAZI-FULL (author): God needs no army, but promises to help the servant who helps him, although heaven and earth are already his armies. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p56 God needs no remembrance because all already remember him, yet remembers the servant who remembers him; he needs no service, since he is king and all creation prostrates, but brief service earns lasting rest. — RAZI-FULL (author): God needs no remembrance because all already remember him, yet remembers the servant who remembers him; he needs no service, since he is king and all creation prostrates, but brief service earns lasting rest. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p57 The terms are associated with swimming in water and traversing land, respectively, in the sense of going through and far away. — RAZI-FULL (author): The terms are associated with swimming in water and traversing land, respectively, in the sense of going through and far away. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p58 In essence, God is beyond possibility: denying possibility entails denying multiplicity, corporeality, accidents, opposites, and peers, affirming absolute unity and necessary existence. — RAZI-FULL (author): In essence, God is beyond possibility: denying possibility entails denying multiplicity, corporeality, accidents, opposites, and peers, affirming absolute unity and necessary existence. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p59 In attributes, God is free of ignorance and change, encompasses all knowables, and is able to do all that can be done. — RAZI-FULL (author): In attributes, God is free of ignorance and change, encompasses all knowables, and is able to do all that can be done. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p60 In acts, God neither seeks benefit nor avoids harm nor gains or loses perfection; he is independent of all existents and nonexistents and sovereign over their creation and annihilation. — RAZI-FULL (author): In acts, God neither seeks benefit nor avoids harm nor gains or loses perfection; he is independent of all existents and nonexistents and sovereign over their creation and annihilation. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p61 David says he cannot thank God, since he can reach gratitude for a blessing only through another blessing; God says recognizing this dependence is gratitude. — RAZI-FULL (داود): David says he cannot thank God, since he can reach gratitude for a blessing only through another blessing; God says recognizing this dependence is gratitude. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(داود)]
- 2:30/q60/p62 Saʿid ibn Jubayr recounts that a hypocrite rebuffed a Muslim who reminded him to pray; ʿUmar struck him, and the Prophet told ʿUmar his anger was honor and his approval judgment. — RAZI-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr recounts that a hypocrite rebuffed a Muslim who reminded him to pray; ʿUmar struck him, and the Prophet told ʿUmar his anger was honor and his approval judgment. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير)]
- 2:30/q60/p63 One interpretation addresses God: we declare him purified, describing him with the loftiness and might that befit him. — RAZI-FULL (author): One interpretation addresses God: we declare him purified, describing him with the loftiness and might that befit him. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p64 Abu Muslim says they purify their deeds of sin so the deeds are sincerely for God. — RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم): Abu Muslim says they purify their deeds of sin so the deeds are sincerely for God. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم)]
- 2:30/q60/p65 Another interpretation is purifying the heart of attention to anything besides God until it is absorbed in the lights of knowing him. — RAZI-FULL (author): Another interpretation is purifying the heart of attention to anything besides God until it is absorbed in the lights of knowing him. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p66 The Qur'anic phrasing uses singular agreement by form, while Arabic also allows plural agreement by meaning, as illustrated by clauses about listeners. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The Qur'anic phrasing uses singular agreement by form, while Arabic also allows plural agreement by meaning, as illustrated by clauses about listeners. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p67 The phrase may encompass everything that was, is, and will be. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The phrase may encompass everything that was, is, and will be. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p68 The ending in malaʾika can mark the feminine plural, like ṣalādimah. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): The ending in malaʾika can mark the feminine plural, like ṣalādimah. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p69 Alternatively, the ending intensifies the sense, like ʿallāma and nassāba. — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Alternatively, the ending intensifies the sense, like ʿallāma and nassāba. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p70 They overlooked that appetite and anger, disciplined by reason, yield chastity, courage, resisting desire, and justice; the human composite also enables grasping particulars, inventing crafts, and bringing nature’s potential benefits into actuality, the purpose of succession. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): They overlooked that appetite and anger, disciplined by reason, yield chastity, courage, resisting desire, and justice; the human composite also enables grasping particulars, inventing crafts, and bringing nature’s potential benefits into actuality, the purpose of succession. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p71 In 46:21, ‘remember the brother of ʿĀd when he warned his people’ means remember the event at that time: the event is omitted and its ظرف stands in its place. — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): In 46:21, ‘remember the brother of ʿĀd when he warned his people’ means remember the event at that time: the event is omitted and its ظرف stands in its place. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p72 The segment attributes a reading of إني to the Hijazis and Abu ʿAmr. — NASAFI-FULL (الحجازيون وأبو عمرو): The segment attributes a reading of إني to the Hijazis and Abu ʿAmr. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL(الحجازيون وأبو عمرو)]
- 2:30/q60/p73 Abu Hayyan calls that division a good interpretation, analogous to a collective quotation whose different statements belong to different groups. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abu Hayyan calls that division a good interpretation, analogous to a collective quotation whose different statements belong to different groups. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:30/q60/p74 The earlier grammarians maintain that أفعل always carries a comparative sense, and Abu Hayyan endorses their position. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (النحويون المتقدمون): The earlier grammarians maintain that أفعل always carries a comparative sense, and Abu Hayyan endorses their position. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(النحويون المتقدمون)]
- 2:30/q60/p75 Abu al-Abbas regards comparative-free use of أفعل as productive, while the cited grammarian says the sounder rule is to limit it to attested usage. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو العباس): Abu al-Abbas regards comparative-free use of أفعل as productive, while the cited grammarian says the sounder rule is to limit it to attested usage. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو العباس)]
- 2:30/q60/p76 Another explanation is God’s complete knowledge of outward and inward matters, subtle and evident, near and distant, good and bad, across changing conditions and times. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قول غير مسمى): Another explanation is God’s complete knowledge of outward and inward matters, subtle and evident, near and distant, good and bad, across changing conditions and times. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قول غير مسمى)]
- 2:30/q60/p77 Another explanation contrasts God’s knowledge, gained without acquisition, inquiry, reflection, or thought, with human knowledge. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قول غير مسمى): Another explanation contrasts God’s knowledge, gained without acquisition, inquiry, reflection, or thought, with human knowledge. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قول غير مسمى)]
- 2:30/q60/p78 Grammarians disagree whether دم follows فَعْل with a quiescent middle radical or فَعَل with it open. — SAMIN-DURR (author): Grammarians disagree whether دم follows فَعْل with a quiescent middle radical or فَعَل with it open. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 2:30/q60/p79 The Prophet says the deeds of night are raised to God before day, and the deeds of day before night. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the deeds of night are raised to God before day, and the deeds of day before night. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 2:30/q60/p80 Ibn Ishaq interprets the words as a commitment not to disobey God or do anything He dislikes. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (محمد بن إسحاق): Ibn Ishaq interprets the words as a commitment not to disobey God or do anything He dislikes. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(محمد بن إسحاق)]
- 2:30/q60/p81 The Qur'an was not collected as a codex during the Prophet's life while revelation continued; Abu Bakr and the Companions compiled it after his death, then Uthman copied and dispatched codices. — BURHAN (author): The Qur'an was not collected as a codex during the Prophet's life while revelation continued; Abu Bakr and the Companions compiled it after his death, then Uthman copied and dispatched codices. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q60/p82 Delaying the person praised or blamed can emphasize the intended focus: نعم الرجل زيد is stronger than placing Zayd first. — BURHAN (author): Delaying the person praised or blamed can emphasize the intended focus: نعم الرجل زيد is stronger than placing Zayd first. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q60/p83 تضمين can also mean inserting another speaker's words into one's discourse to reinforce meaning or shape composition; Zarkashi calls this ibdaʿ. — BURHAN (author): تضمين can also mean inserting another speaker's words into one's discourse to reinforce meaning or shape composition; Zarkashi calls this ibdaʿ. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 2:30/q60/p84 The author suggests Abū ʿAmr meant low frequency in the Qurʾān by few letters; recurrence matters, and Abū ʿAmr may also have weighed defectiveness and the received transmission. — NASHR (author): The author suggests Abū ʿAmr meant low frequency in the Qurʾān by few letters; recurrence matters, and Abū ʿAmr may also have weighed defectiveness and the received transmission. [1 notes: NASHR]
- 2:30/q60/p85 The phrase about being dead, then made alive, dying, and being revived can be attached to an implied command to remember these events; the beginning already lets the thoughtful infer the return. — BIQAI-FULL (author): The phrase about being dead, then made alive, dying, and being revived can be attached to an implied command to remember these events; the beginning already lets the thoughtful infer the return. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p86 Humanity was made for lasting life, not extinction: God created the cosmos for people, as provision, means, and support for their preservation and development. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Humanity was made for lasting life, not extinction: God created the cosmos for people, as provision, means, and support for their preservation and development. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p87 Biqai describes two levels of discourse, a general one and a higher, more particular one that yields increasingly elevated meanings. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Biqai describes two levels of discourse, a general one and a higher, more particular one that yields increasingly elevated meanings. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p88 Abu Malik says every Qur'anic statement introduced by “when” refers to something that has occurred. — DURR-FULL (أبو مالك): Abu Malik says every Qur'anic statement introduced by “when” refers to something that has occurred. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(أبو مالك)]
- 2:30/q60/p89 Al-Dahhak says every occurrence of “made” in the Qur'an means “created.” — DURR-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says every occurrence of “made” in the Qur'an means “created.” [1 notes: DURR-FULL(الضحاك)]
- 2:30/q60/p90 “Major” idgham has a moving first letter in the pair, whether the letters are identical, alike in articulation, or near in articulation or quality; several explanations are given for its name. — ITQAN (author): “Major” idgham has a moving first letter in the pair, whether the letters are identical, alike in articulation, or near in articulation or quality; several explanations are given for its name. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p91 Among the ten imams, Abu Amr ibn al-Ala is especially associated with major idgham; it is also reported from al-Hasan al-Basri, al-A'mash, Ibn Muhaysin, and others. — ITQAN (author): Among the ten imams, Abu Amr ibn al-Ala is especially associated with major idgham; it is also reported from al-Hasan al-Basri, al-A'mash, Ibn Muhaysin, and others. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p92 Its purpose is ease of pronunciation, though many prominent works on readings do not mention it. — ITQAN (author): Its purpose is ease of pronunciation, though many prominent works on readings do not mention it. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p93 Mutamathilain share articulation and quality; mutajanisain share articulation but differ in quality; mutaqaribain are close in articulation or quality. — ITQAN (author): Mutamathilain share articulation and quality; mutajanisain share articulation but differ in quality; mutaqaribain are close in articulation or quality. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p94 Idgham among identical letters occurs across seventeen letters; the verse's “we glorify You” is one example, as are other cited letter pairs. — ITQAN (author): Idgham among identical letters occurs across seventeen letters; the verse's “we glorify You” is one example, as are other cited letter pairs. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p95 The conditions include matching in writing and usually meeting across two words; exceptions include مناسككم and ما سلككم, while pronoun tāʾ, a doubled first letter, and tanwin prevent idgham. — ITQAN (author): The conditions include matching in writing and usually meeting across two words; exceptions include مناسككم and ما سلككم, while pronoun tāʾ, a doubled first letter, and tanwin prevent idgham. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p96 Qāf assimilates into kāf after a vowel and also in one word before mīm; kāf assimilates into qāf after a vowel, except when its preceding letter is still. — ITQAN (author): Qāf assimilates into kāf after a vowel and also in one word before mīm; kāf assimilates into qāf after a vowel, except when its preceding letter is still. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p97 To sanctify for God means to purify. — ITQAN (ابن عباس): To sanctify for God means to purify. [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن عباس)]
- 2:30/q60/p98 Tadmin has four senses: using a word for another whose meaning it contains; implying a meaning without naming it; linking post-rhyme wording to the rhyme; and embedding another's speech to reinforce meaning or structure. — ITQAN (author): Tadmin has four senses: using a word for another whose meaning it contains; implying a meaning without naming it; linking post-rhyme wording to the rhyme; and embedding another's speech to reinforce meaning or structure. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p99 Ibn Abi al-Isba says he found Qur'anic examples only in two places, each incorporating a passage from the Torah or Gospel: “life for life” and “Muhammad is God's messenger.” — ITQAN (ابن أبي الإصبع): Ibn Abi al-Isba says he found Qur'anic examples only in two places, each incorporating a passage from the Torah or Gospel: “life for life” and “Muhammad is God's messenger.” [1 notes: ITQAN(ابن أبي الإصبع)]
- 2:30/q60/p100 The intended first human and his spouse are Adam and Hawwa; her name is written long because she was created from a living person. — ITQAN (author): The intended first human and his spouse are Adam and Hawwa; her name is written long because she was created from a living person. [1 notes: ITQAN]
- 2:30/q60/p101 Bursevī also says even the wisest man, finest mount, and most devout woman need a counselor, a whip, and a spouse respectively. — BURSEVI (author): Bursevī also says even the wisest man, finest mount, and most devout woman need a counselor, a whip, and a spouse respectively. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q60/p102 The Prophet warns against gloating over a brother’s misfortune, since God may relieve him and test the one who gloats. — BURSEVI (النبي): The Prophet warns against gloating over a brother’s misfortune, since God may relieve him and test the one who gloats. [1 notes: BURSEVI(النبي)]
- 2:30/q60/p103 Israelites made the same entitlement argument against Talut; God answered that kingship rests on divine choice and superiority in knowledge and physique, not wealth. — BURSEVI (author): Israelites made the same entitlement argument against Talut; God answered that kingship rests on divine choice and superiority in knowledge and physique, not wealth. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 2:30/q60/p104 The Pen has 360 teeth and the Intellect 360 manifestations; each yields 360 classes of general knowledge unfolded on the Tablet, beginning with nature beneath the soul. — ALUSI-FULL (أهل الله): The Pen has 360 teeth and the Intellect 360 manifestations; each yields 360 classes of general knowledge unfolded on the Tablet, beginning with nature beneath the soul. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(أهل الله)]
- 2:30/q60/p105 After recounting favors to earlier people, God follows with a universal blessing and complete honor; beneficence to the ancestor also benefits the descendant. — ALUSI-FULL (author): After recounting favors to earlier people, God follows with a universal blessing and complete honor; beneficence to the ancestor also benefits the descendant. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p106 Some treat the word as hollow from lāka yalūku; the feminine ending is explained either as feminine plural or as an intensifier. — ALUSI-FULL (بعض): Some treat the word as hollow from lāka yalūku; the feminine ending is explained either as feminine plural or as an intensifier. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(بعض)]
- 2:30/q60/p107 They identify one addressed angel as al-Rūḥ, the highest Pen, or First Intellect, a mirror of the divine Essence and the axis of the worlds and their inhabitants. — ALUSI-FULL (السادة الصوفية): They identify one addressed angel as al-Rūḥ, the highest Pen, or First Intellect, a mirror of the divine Essence and the axis of the worlds and their inhabitants. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(السادة الصوفية)]
- 2:30/q60/p108 The comprehensive divine Name makes vicegerency and cosmic governance possible, while God alone truly acts; the author notes the difficulty of this doctrine and its many deniers. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The comprehensive divine Name makes vicegerency and cosmic governance possible, while God alone truly acts; the author notes the difficulty of this doctrine and its many deniers. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p109 ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn al-Mawṣilī chose the circumstantial reading; al-Ālūsī says respect restrains him from commenting on that choice and ultimately upholds the explanation he has set out. — ALUSI-FULL (علاء الدين الموصلي): ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn al-Mawṣilī chose the circumstantial reading; al-Ālūsī says respect restrains him from commenting on that choice and ultimately upholds the explanation he has set out. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(علاء الدين الموصلي)]
- 2:30/q60/p110 Al-Khazraji compares the two-part response to the divided alternative “Be Jews or Christians and you will be guided.” — ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Khazraji compares the two-part response to the divided alternative “Be Jews or Christians and you will be guided.” [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p111 Al-Alusi prefers explaining the unspecified knowledge by God's statement that He knows the unseen of the heavens and earth. — ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Alusi prefers explaining the unspecified knowledge by God's statement that He knows the unseen of the heavens and earth. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL+]
- 2:30/q60/p112 The lām in “for You” can mark devotion: the act is done for God and sincerely for His sake. — ALUSI-FULL (author): The lām in “for You” can mark devotion: the act is done for God and sincerely for His sake. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p113 He invokes the maxim that a person seeking judicial office should not be appointed to it. — ELMALILI (author): He invokes the maxim that a person seeking judicial office should not be appointed to it. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 2:30/q60/p114 He distinguishes conventional knowledge, defined by society and era, from true knowledge that perfects people and reveals beneficial realities; both can confer mastery and partly overlap. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): He distinguishes conventional knowledge, defined by society and era, from true knowledge that perfects people and reveals beneficial realities; both can confer mastery and partly overlap. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p115 Human virtues may emerge from paired defects: courage, for example, lies between recklessness and cowardice. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Human virtues may emerge from paired defects: courage, for example, lies between recklessness and cowardice. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:30/q60/p116 Al-Khattabi says Qur'anic rhetoric is miraculous because it combines three levels of eloquence, excluding the disapproved hybrid, to achieve both grandeur and sweetness. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi says Qur'anic rhetoric is miraculous because it combines three levels of eloquence, excluding the disapproved hybrid, to achieve both grandeur and sweetness. [1 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ(الخطابي)]
- 2:30/q60/p117 Al-Khattabi was an early observer of fine semantic distinctions among Qur'anic terms often treated as synonyms, including knowledge and knowing, praise and thanks, and emancipation and manumission. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi was an early observer of fine semantic distinctions among Qur'anic terms often treated as synonyms, including knowledge and knowing, praise and thanks, and emancipation and manumission. [1 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ(الخطابي)]
- 2:30/q60/p118 People must recognize their opportunities and blessings as God's property, entrusted for a purpose and condition, and use them according to God's will and approval. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): People must recognize their opportunities and blessings as God's property, entrusted for a purpose and condition, and use them according to God's will and approval. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q60/p119 An a fortiori argument says that if mortal conscious beings exist, immortal ones can also exist, and intelligent beings in the lower world make such beings in the nobler heavens all the more plausible. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): An a fortiori argument says that if mortal conscious beings exist, immortal ones can also exist, and intelligent beings in the lower world make such beings in the nobler heavens all the more plausible. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q60/p120 The Qur'an mentions the trumpet to be blown at the beginning of the resurrection and revival. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The Qur'an mentions the trumpet to be blown at the beginning of the resurrection and revival. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:30/q60/p121 The commentary finds two explanations more reasonable, closer to reality, and more consistent with the narrative style. — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The commentary finds two explanations more reasonable, closer to reality, and more consistent with the narrative style. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR+]
- 2:30/q60/p122 Tabari rejects Ibn Ishaq's meaning of inhabiting and cultivating. — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari rejects Ibn Ishaq's meaning of inhabiting and cultivating. [0 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن إسحاق)-]
- 2:30/q60/p123 The reading that a virtuous woman is ‘a man in meaning’ is rejected: it conflicts with the moon example and makes the couplet self-contradictory. — JURJANI-ASRAR (author): The reading that a virtuous woman is ‘a man in meaning’ is rejected: it conflicts with the moon example and makes the couplet self-contradictory. [0 notes: JURJANI-ASRAR-]
- 2:30/q60/p124 A proposed meaning is God’s knowledge of Iblīs’s concealed disobedience, but the author rejects it because Iblīs has not yet been mentioned. — JISHUMI (author): A proposed meaning is God’s knowledge of Iblīs’s concealed disobedience, but the author rejects it because Iblīs has not yet been mentioned. [0 notes: JISHUMI-]
- 2:30/q60/p125 Because the passage can mean zakāt, the author rejects its abrogation. — JISHUMI (author): Because the passage can mean zakāt, the author rejects its abrogation. [0 notes: JISHUMI-]
- 2:30/q60/p126 Choosing worldly or later punishment is incoherent: repentance should have been offered, as it is even to lifelong polytheists and prophet-persecutors. — RAZI-FULL (author): Choosing worldly or later punishment is incoherent: repentance should have been offered, as it is even to lifelong polytheists and prophet-persecutors. [0 notes: RAZI-FULL-]
- 2:30/q60/p127 Abū Ḥayyān counts — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān counts eight such analyses and says the Qurʾān should be kept clear of them. [0 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:30/q60/p128 Bint al-Shati rejects the implication that Qur'anic passages occupy lower eloquence levels: inimitability reaches the highest level, and one sura need not combine all three because the challenge concerns a single sura. — BINTSHATI-IJAZ (author): Bint al-Shati rejects the implication that Qur'anic passages occupy lower eloquence levels: inimitability reaches the highest level, and one sura need not combine all three because the challenge concerns a single sura. [0 notes: BINTSHATI-IJAZ-]
